Ansa stood in Cartography Bay Four with the candidate suspended above her rail. Its certified orbit shone green. Local thresholds surrounded it in amber layers that did not meet. Ren Adek sat on the floor beneath the display, sorting interval records from Remainder into columns no Authority software recognized.

“Candidate four,” she said.

“Halcyon passenger lane at plus nine hours,” one cartographer answered.

“Five.”

“Unregistered Serein reserve equipment inside the uncertainty cone.”

“Six.”

“Too little conductive mass. We could transmit a beautiful prediction and watch physics ignore us.”

Ansa collapsed the failed panes. Director Brace had granted narrow authority for three predicted positions. Finding three bodies acceptable to fourteen jurisdictions had already consumed two days. The incoming structures continued toward Asteron's magnetotail while the Cantilevers' outer phases gathered fine contamination.

“Use weighted thresholds,” she said. “Convert every submitted harm into expected evacuation hours.”

The bay went quiet enough for her to hear the cooling pumps alter pitch.

Pema Rill at the trajectory station turned around. “Rook did not provide an evacuation model.”

“Use Authority defaults.”

“Serein prohibited substitution.”

“Then preserve its original threshold as a constraint and optimize the others.”

“Vela prohibited aggregation.”

Ren looked up from the floor. “We have achieved communication with the outer jurisdictions.”

“No, we have achieved incompatible forms.”

“That may be their message.”

Ansa sent the data into her private model. Expected evacuation hours allowed unlike risks to enter one frame. A delayed convoy could be translated into atmosphere reserve draw. A damaged survey could be priced against replacement time and collision uncertainty. Bodies, heat, transport, and institutional embarrassment became comparable after enough assumptions.

The model selected three candidates in fourteen seconds.

Moonlet A-19 could cross a plane four meters early with negligible registered traffic effect. A shepherd moon near Asteron allowed a timing adjustment under seven seconds if pressure stayed below its surface-fracture limit. A spent ballast body near Rook offered the cleanest magnetic response but passed through an unregistered maintenance volume once every eleven months.

“Send these for certification,” Ansa said.

Pema studied the constraint pane. “Serein's reserve threshold disappeared.”

“It entered the evacuation-hours conversion.”

“They gave us distance.”

“Distance without consequence cannot be compared.”

“Perhaps they wanted it not compared.”

Ansa felt the irritation before she found its cause. Pema had repeated Edrin's argument in technical language, which made it harder to dismiss as family history.

“Show the conversion record,” she said.

The model opened a dependency tree. Serein's twelve-meter boundary had become a probability of route loss. Route loss became delay. Delay became passenger-support demand. Default docking capacity reduced the demand because Meridian standards assumed compatible pressure berths within six hours of any reserve route.

Serein had told Olan three stations lacked those berths.

The figure had not entered Ansa's local copy.

“Withdraw the candidates,” she said.

Pema's hand stopped above transmission. “They have not left the Array.”

“Then withdraw them from us.”

She requested the jurisdictional model again. Meridian Control returned a custody warning: local schedules carried restricted infrastructure details. Read access required fourteen separate agreements. Olan's experiment clause allowed thresholds but not underlying reserve maps.

Ansa called his office. Counsel answered with a twelve-minute queue and a reminder that the experiment deadline expired at the next Assembly review.

Ren rose, carrying his interval columns. “Grammar with mass has encountered licensing.”

“Do not make this charming.”

“I am trying to make it survivable.”

The call indicator shifted. Instead of Meridian counsel, an incoming personal request appeared: Edrin, Halcyon Ring.

Ansa almost deferred it. Her brother did not call during his school cycle unless transit allocations had failed. Then she saw the attached request from Halcyon local control. He had been assigned to verify passenger-support assumptions for the contact test.

She accepted with video.

Edrin stood in a classroom fabrication bay wearing a filter harness around his neck. Behind him, students packed seal patches into wall kits while an instructor checked their labels. The room's curve placed the far tables slightly above his head.

“You used six-hour compatible docking,” he said without greeting.

“I used an Authority default in a temporary model.”

“Your temporary model has reached our school-transfer board.”

“It has not been transmitted.”

“Optimization queries propagate resource holds. Two supported transfer slots now show provisional delay because your candidate moon might alter a feeder lane.”

Ansa checked. He was right. The model had reserved consequences while still calling itself private.

“I will release the holds.”

“After you tell me why twenty-six children became decimal support hours.”

“They did not. The model converts capacity demands into a common unit.”

“What does it call a child who cannot use a Meridian-standard berth?”

“An elevated support load.”

Edrin turned his screen toward a girl sealing a practice mask over a plastic face. The student had built a bright orange patch into one cheek of the mask so instructors could see where it would fail.

“Her name is Tavi,” he said. “She needs sulfur filtration during pressure transfers. Halcyon has it. Two reserve stations do not. Your average gives both stations partial credit because their atmosphere systems exceed the generic standard.”

“The model cannot carry every personal profile into an orbital test.”

“Then do not claim it carries consequence.”

“We need comparable limits.”

“You need an answer soon. Comparable limits happen to give you one.”

His accusation found a clean path through her defenses. Ansa had not hidden any threshold. She had preserved every input, published the conversion, and kept uncertainty attached. Yet the result still erased the precise condition that made Serein's reserve route unsafe for some travelers. Transparency did not repair a category chosen badly.

“What would Halcyon submit?” she asked.

“Non-substitutable atmosphere support. If your candidate creates any route change, local control chooses which transfer absorbs it.”

“That prevents the model from optimizing across regions.”

“Yes.”

“Edrin, fourteen local choices can make all three predictions impossible.”

“Then the system cannot afford your experiment under the terms you were given.”

She looked beyond him at the students. One boy had attached his seal patch backward. Tavi peeled it off, turned it, and pressed his hand over the adhesive until it held.

“What if we choose a plane rather than a displacement?” Ansa said. “We predict where the moon will cross but do not request an amount of change. The observed body can satisfy the prediction by altering its own pressure field, leaving the moon untouched.”

Edrin frowned. “Can it?”

“We do not know.”

“Then write that uncertainty next to every local threshold. Do not bury it in your expected value.”

“I can keep each jurisdiction's limit separate.”

“You can let them remain inconvenient.”

The old eclipse map entered her memory: four licensed shelters in blue, farm tunnels absent because no official route could terminate there. She had spent years believing better data would have corrected the map. Edrin kept insisting that correction also required surrendering the right to decide which forms counted.

“Send me Tavi's support class, without her identity,” Ansa said. “And the real docking compatibilities.”

“I will send the classes. Local control sends the maps.”

“Fair.”

He noticed the word and did not reward it. “The student holds clear in four minutes.”

“They are released now.”

A status light changed behind him. Edrin nodded once. “Then we have four minutes back.”

He ended the call to help Tavi test the mask.

Ansa rebuilt the candidates without a common harm unit. Fourteen sets of thresholds remained separate, visible, and allowed to veto a proposed change. The model no longer produced one optimum. It produced a ragged intersection where no submitted limit broke.

Only the Asteron shepherd moon remained.

It carried no settlement. Its small orbit maintained a gap between two larger moonlets whose debris histories threatened a sensor station every few decades. The experiment could name a future crossing plane without asking the moon to leave its certified uncertainty. Any external pressure acting on the surrounding field might alter the reference relation and make the crossing occur as predicted while never touching the moon.

Pema checked every jurisdictional threshold against the new design. Serein accepted. Vela demanded the transmission avoid its convoy timing code. Rook attached a requirement that all induced thermal effects remain below sensor noise. Halcyon reserved authority over any downstream passenger hold.

Olan's office certified the candidate with nine minutes left before review.

Ren placed his interval columns over the shepherd moon's track. “Before we transmit, you should see what Calder's canister carried back.”

The recovered shell had recorded the alien pressure as induced currents. Those currents rose and fell in bands too slow for equipment noise. Ren aligned them with the pulses Remainder had sent before release.

The first pressure change followed the declared water layer. A second tracked the tungsten bars. The central iron mass produced a longer response. The field had not only moved the canister. It had stepped through the human description of its parts.

“That could follow susceptibility,” Ansa said.

“It could. Then look at the gaps.”

Ren rescaled by canister spin. Response intervals formed ratios around the predicted crossing time. The final interval shortened after the orbit changed, matching the safer return loop.

Ansa tested clock drift, sensor latency, and the buoy's loop frequency. The relation survived. When she removed Remainder's declared prediction, the pattern lost coherence.

“It answered the estimate,” Ren said.

“It interacted with a timed force profile.”

“Your sentence uses more syllables to protect us from the same fact.”

“Facts need protection from memorable sentences.”

He smiled faintly. “There she is.”

Ansa overlaid the second survey-stone correction from SD2. Its plane crossing arrived early by the same ratio used in the canister's final interval. Two acts, one reference. Whatever shaped the field had preserved a relationship between expected motion and observed change.

Not vocabulary. Not greeting. A shared comparison.

She altered the shepherd-moon experiment. The transmission would state three positions: current measured orbit, predicted crossing under no external action, and a second crossing time impossible under local forces without violating the moon's certified tolerance. It would then repeat the canister ratio as a reference interval.

Pema read the sequence. “We are predicting something we know will not happen.”

“We are offering a condition under which it can happen without moving the moon.”

“If they read the plane as an obstacle?”

“Then we measure their response.”

“If they move the moon anyway?”

Every local threshold remained on the display. Ansa could see Tavi's support class among the Halcyon constraints, anonymous and physically specific.

“We stop,” she said. “No second transmission. All Cantilever phases hold.”

Ren sent the sequence for final witness. Fourteen observatories recorded the outgoing pulse. Meridian Control recorded Olan's certification. Remainder, still near the buoy with one fuel spine isolated, repeated the timing from local space.

The prediction traveled outward.

Light lag divided the event into jurisdictions of knowledge. Aphelion saw transmission first. Remainder received it later. Meridian would watch the shepherd moon cross after the fact by over forty minutes. No room in Pelagos could occupy a single present.

Ansa waited through two local shifts.

Waiting did not leave the experiment unchanged. Each jurisdiction watched a different future arrive. Serein received the outgoing sequence with enough time to inspect its reserve route again. Vela saw the catalyst convoy clear before the response window. Meridian defense received only the certified prediction and asked for live access to the Array's abort control.

Olan denied defense exclusive access but requested a single system abort threshold. Ansa looked at the fourteen limits still hanging around the shepherd moon. One threshold would reproduce the conversion she had just abandoned.

She built an authority table instead. Serein could stop any further transmission if its reserve distance changed. Rook held thermal veto. Vela controlled convoy timing. Halcyon could reject downstream holds on passenger support. The remaining jurisdictions received narrower controls tied to effects they could measure locally. None could erase the observations already made.

Pema read the table twice. “If four regions stop for unrelated reasons, who decides whether we transmit again?”

“No one in that moment.”

“That is not a decision structure.”

“It is a stop structure. We design the next decision after we know what stopped us.”

Ren added the table to the witnessed packet. “Our possible observer may now learn that humans communicate through fourteen varieties of refusal.”

“Better than teaching it that one human office can spend the others.”

The sentence sounded too complete. Ansa distrusted it at once. Distributed limits could also protect a wealthy route by blocking a correction needed elsewhere. No architecture removed politics. This one merely forced the politics to remain visible while the test ran.

During the first shift change, Serein invoked a temporary hold after a maintenance beacon entered the moon's long uncertainty cone. The beacon had no crew and enough fuel to move itself. Under Ansa's old model, it would have counted as a negligible adjustment. She waited while local control moved it eighteen kilometers and supplied the burn record. The hold lifted eleven minutes later, leaving the beacon on a locally chosen path.

During the second shift, Meridian defense protested that the local delay had degraded response clarity. Ren compared the revised timing against the canister ratio and found the reference survived. Their observer had used intervals across much larger variation. Human impatience, not signal physics, had created the deadline.

Ansa recorded that finding beside the experiment authority. The revision changed who could be blamed if no answer came. It also changed what success would prove. A response after fourteen visible constraints could no longer be treated as a private conversation between Aphelion and the incoming bodies.

The incoming bodies continued toward the magnetotail. Their visible sails changed curvature as Asteron's field strengthened. Smaller masses sheltered between adults, appearing and vanishing behind pressure veils. Public telescopes now carried enough detail for citizens to count structures, though no count agreed.

At the predicted response window, nothing approached the shepherd moon.

Its surface remained cold. Its orbit held inside three centimeters of the certified line. The crossing plane waited as a transparent sheet in Bay Four.

Pema said, “No induced field above noise.”

Ren watched the interval pane. “Wait for the canister ratio.”

The first interval passed.

A pressure change moved along the nearest incoming sail. It did not reach the shepherd moon. Instead it pressed against the resonance field outside the orbit, altering how Cantilevers 66, 68, and 69 measured the local plane. The physical moon continued on its path while the reference relation bent toward it.

Second interval.

The pressure eased near 66 and increased near 69. The plane rotated by a fraction no human control order had requested.

Third interval.

Cantilever 68 reported a correction residual, then another. Ansa froze assimilation before the network could teach the old model to swallow the event.

The shepherd moon crossed the specified plane at the predicted time.

No force above sensor threshold had touched it.

Bay Four erupted into overlapping reports. Pema confirmed surface temperature. Two cartographers rebuilt the orbit from independent clocks. Rook certified no thermal violation. Serein's reserve office confirmed its distance threshold held. Halcyon released the provisional passenger guard before Ansa asked.

Ren stood very still beneath the moving field plot. “It made the prediction true.”

“It changed the reference geometry.”

“Deliberately.”

Ansa checked the pressure bands. They repeated the canister ratio within measurement error. Natural coupling could not explain selecting that interval after a human transmission, not without turning coincidence into a force more extravagant than intent.

She opened the incident statement and typed: Observed field behavior responds to witnessed prediction. She did not type language. Intelligence now had evidence. Purpose did not.

The answer could mean acknowledgment. It could display superior control. It could warn that every human plane remained negotiable. It had accepted the offered condition while preserving the moon, which suggested attention to constraints but not care for the people behind them.

Ren touched the canister sequence. “We need more shared events. Before, after, same object, changed expectation.”

“Not from the Array alone.”

A fixed observatory could declare time but could not carry the declaration into the pressure field. Remainder had done that by accident. A deliberate exchange needed independent clocks, physical mass, and a witness capable of moving between human and Orison frames.

Ansa called the tug.

Sana Vi answered after the boundary delay. Her image arrived from the bridge rail, with Ivo beside propulsion status and the port spine marked isolated in amber.

“Your moon crossed,” Sana said.

“Our plane moved.”

Ivo glanced at the damage pane. “Cartographer's distinction?”

“A useful one. I need you to carry a clock.”

“We have several.”

“A physical shared clock. Independent pulse stack, correction mass, witnessed predictions, no connection to Cantilever phase control.”

“Toward the body?”

“Yes.”

Sana looked at Ivo rather than yielding the bridge to him. He read the proposed mass, the cracked-spine limits, and the separation geometry before answering.

“Send the hardware plan,” he said. “Then ask the crew.”

Ansa transmitted the request with every jurisdictional threshold still attached. Intelligence had answered her optimized plane only after she stopped optimizing away the people who could be moved. Now the next question would leave the Array in a tug old enough to show every repair.

On the plot, the altered plane relaxed behind the shepherd moon. The moon kept its original orbit. Remainder accepted the design packet for a shared clock and began calculating the fuel needed to carry it inward.

Chapter 6 — Seventy-Two Hours

The defense flotilla began mobilizing before Olan authorized its fuel.

Six launch tracks turned red across his wall. Two cutters left Meridia high orbit under standing perimeter orders. A carrier at Vela separated from its service ring while tug crews still cleared the radiator booms. By the time Vice Minister Solv requested emergency allocation, ships carrying enough kinetic mass to damage a moon had already entered transfer lanes.

Olan denied the request.

Solv's projection sharpened above the conference rail. “They cannot return without reserve.”

“Then whoever ordered departure can explain the stranded crews.”

“Public images show nineteen adult bodies inside the survey boundary.”

“Public images also show them avoiding inhabited routes.”

“They are positioning near Asteron.”

“They are moving toward its magnetotail.”

“Which contains the outer Cantilevers.”

The final point had force. Cantilevers 61 through 72 shaped transit, power exchange, and ballast control around Asteron's moons. An external pressure field had already reached through their resonance and corrected protected mass. If the nineteen bodies occupied the magnetotail, they could act on the lattice from inside its strongest outer coupling.

Behind Solv, officers assembled intercept solutions. Every line crossed the migration far outside useful weapons range and ended inside a region where human drives would struggle against magnetic shear. The plans existed because a plan could be docketed while uncertainty could not.

“Recall the cutters to holding distance,” Olan said. “Carrier remains at Vela pending civilian fuel allocation.”

Solv looked off-pane to someone Olan could not see. “Defense will comply with lawful civil control. We request an immediate phase lock.”

“Request received.”

“Director, if those structures pass Cantilever 68 before you decide, our lock geometry degrades.”

“How long?”

“Eleven hours at current approach.”

Olan ended the channel and opened the system consequence board.

The reserve order he had drafted on SD4 waited under his seal. Its trigger clauses had not been met cleanly. No uncommanded correction threatened inhabited infrastructure. Hostile acceleration remained unconfirmed. Outer phase contamination affected every station but stayed within recovery capacity.

A fourth clause allowed action to preserve control when an unknown mass entered a protected resonance volume. The law had intended ballast failures and disabled construction veils. It did not specify a maximum body count.

His controller placed a live model beside the order. Locking the outer twelve would harden their shared phase and create a steep resonant barrier across the migration's most efficient approach. It would also close eleven transit windows in the first hour, interrupt power exchange to Serein, and hold Neme's correction debt inside Asteron's system.

“Local reserves?” Olan asked.

“Meridia loses no essential load. Vela can support twenty-one days if the carrier stays docked. Halcyon sees delay but no immediate power loss. Serein drops to sixteen days under normal ice processing.”

“Outer transit?”

“Seventy-eight crewed vessels between decision points. Forty-one can return to origin. Twenty can reach alternate berths. Seventeen require directed holding or emergency fuel.”

“Passengers?”

“Just under nine thousand, including transfers.”

He thought of the twenty-six students Edrin had forced into Ansa's model. The lock would convert their particular support needs into the same list that had nearly erased them during a contact experiment. A list could still save them if someone retained authority to move resources.

“Open jurisdiction relay,” he said.

All fourteen delegates did not answer at once. Olan transmitted the same model with a response deadline adjusted for each light path. Inner delegates appeared nearly live. Outer statements arrived as time-stamped recordings. Laleh Sorn's image came from Serein local control with a nine-minute delay marker. She wore a pressure coat rather than Assembly black. Behind her, operators built reserve routes by hand because automated plans assumed open Cantilever windows.

Olan's packet carried no recommendation. It showed military positions, migration tracks, phase contamination, power reserves, and every stranded vessel. The adult count remained nineteen. Smaller associated masses exceeded four hundred, though sensor overlap prevented a firm classification.

Responses accumulated out of sequence. Vela supported a lock if defense returned the carrier's fuel. Rook requested six hours to clear a fabrication transfer. Halcyon opposed any action that canceled passenger support without local routing. Four inner regions supported immediate activation. Three asked to classify the bodies hostile first, as if the adjective could add thrust.

Laleh's first recording arrived after the inner arguments had begun. “Serein rejects. The lock places the migration outside the low-shear corridor while directing its pressure toward our reserve envelope. It cuts our ice-haul routes and preserves Meridian power. You are exporting risk under an emergency label.”

Olan recorded his reply. “The outer lattice protects all settlements from uncontrolled mass correction. Temporary phase hardening gives us time to establish intent.”

He sent it and continued hearing nearer regions while the signal crossed. Eighteen minutes passed before Laleh's answer completed the round trip.

“Protection for whom? You trap that mass beside the outer settlements. Ansa established intelligence two days ago. Calder's tug established bounded response before that. You have more information and are choosing less contact.”

Olan began another reply. “We have nineteen adult-scale bodies and no agreement about their purpose.”

Solv spoke from a Meridian room with negligible lag. “Delegate Sorn does not command system defense.”

Olan kept Solv's intervention off the outgoing packet. “She commands Serein's objection. You can answer it in your own filing.”

Laleh's next message had already left Serein before either man spoke. “We also have defense ships moving without fuel authorization. Lock them.”

Solv heard the statement nine minutes after Olan and demanded immediate rebuttal access. Olan denied it. The staggered channel made authority less theatrical but no less tempting. He controlled which local remark entered the next outer transmission.

He asked Laleh for an alternative inside eleven hours and waited through the response delay while controllers updated migration distance.

Her proposal arrived with a local technical attachment. “Hold outer phases at present values. Publish a no-approach zone. Complete the physical clock Ansa requested and let Remainder carry it.”

Olan answered, “The clock hardware needs at least three days. The migration reaches degraded lock geometry in eleven hours whether defense launches or not.”

Serein's return came eighteen minutes later. “Degraded for what purpose? Keeping them out. You have decided the outcome before you call it geometry.”

Olan examined the control model. She had identified the hidden verb. Phase hardening did not merely preserve options. It preserved a specific option: exclusion. Yet leaving the lattice soft also made some later corrections impossible. Time would choose even if he refused.

He sent the legal basis. “Tern Cascade authority permits seventy-two hours of central phase control when delay risks cross-jurisdictional failure.”

Laleh's recorded face changed less than the activity behind her. One operator stopped entering routes and looked toward the camera. “Tern involved failed human infrastructure, not a migrating ecology. The statute covers infrastructure placed under public law. You are applying it to bodies that never delegated you power.”

“I am applying it to the Cantilevers,” Olan recorded.

Her reply returned after another update to the approach clock. “Through their orbits.”

Olan had no answer that did not reveal the same distinction Ansa had tested with the shepherd moon. Alter the reference field, leave the body untouched, claim the intervention belongs only to infrastructure. Motion did not respect that jurisdictional fiction.

He transmitted his decision to every relay at once. “I intend to issue a 72-hour emergency phase lock. Cantilevers 61–72 will hold at coordinated outer phase. Local controls retain life-support allocation. Defense receives no firing authority from this order. I will bring renewal to an Assembly vote before the window expires.”

Requests arrived according to distance rather than urgency. Rook asked for its six-hour clearance. Olan allowed four. Halcyon demanded protected berths for the supported students and every passenger whose atmosphere class lacked a compatible alternate. He assigned Meridian reserve fuel to move two service pods outward before activation.

Vela secured fuel guarantees for the carrier's return. Three settlements placed objections on the record. Serein refused concurrence and requested adjudication.

Laleh's final recording arrived after Olan's decision had already reached her. “When the phase debt moves into our stations, your promise of a vote will not move it back. You already assigned the first cost.”

Her public challenge entered the Assembly docket before the relay closed.

For four hours, Pelagos rearranged itself around a wall that did not yet exist.

Transit Control recalled vessels where fuel permitted. Correction tugs ferried propellant to the seventeen that lacked safe berths. A medical transport bound for Halcyon refused a Meridian dock because three patients could not tolerate its atmosphere mix. Edrin's support roster forced controllers to open a fabrication station that had never been certified for passengers. Workers there printed adapter collars while the transport waited under reduced spin.

The station supervisor refused the docking order at first. Her facility stored powdered metals in compartments never designed to isolate passenger air. Bringing the transport inside could contaminate its filters. Leaving it outside consumed attitude fuel the vessel needed for final approach to Halcyon.

Olan joined the operational channel. The transport captain spoke from a bridge crowded with secured medical racks. “We have seven hours before our correction reserve reaches return-only.”

“Patient limits?” Olan asked.

“Three sulfur-sensitive. One cannot tolerate spin transition above point two gravity per minute. Twelve others stable for nine hours if temperature holds.”

The fabrication supervisor said, “My workers can clear berth four, but we need to stop nickel feed and purge the loading chamber. That costs two production lots and six hours.”

“What do the lots support?”

“Radiator vanes for Cantilever 65.”

A clean local rescue therefore increased future heat risk at a locked station. The emergency board offered no category for that exchange. Olan added both consequences by hand.

Edrin entered from Halcyon school logistics. He did not acknowledge Ansa's brother in the room because the relationship carried no operational value. “We can transfer two portable sulfur scrubbers by courier pod. Flight time one hour forty.”

“Dock compatibility?” the captain asked.

“Pods mate with your service collar, not the berth. You keep station outside and take the scrubbers.”

“Then we still burn holding fuel.”

Serein offered an ice-haul tug passing within range. Laleh's staff had found it on a local schedule the central board did not contain. The tug could hold the medical transport mechanically for three hours, preserving attitude fuel and keeping its patients under steady spin, but doing so meant missing its last open path before the lock.

Laleh came onto the channel. “Serein will assign the tug if Meridian replaces its ice delivery from inner reserve.”

Solv objected from defense allocation. “Inner reserve supports the carrier group.”

“The carrier left without authorized fuel,” Laleh said. “Patients did not.”

Olan opened the allocation sheet. Defense had already reserved the inner ice under a contingency request, though he had denied the mobilization fuel. Administrative possession had outrun legal authority.

“Release the ice to civilian reserve,” he said.

Solv's image appeared beside the transport. “That cuts carrier endurance by thirty-one hours.”

“The carrier remains docked.”

“Until it does not.”

“Then request fuel before departure next time.”

He transferred the allocation to Serein under an auditable exchange. The ice-haul tug altered course. Halcyon's courier launched with the scrubbers. The fabrication station kept berth four sealed and continued producing radiator vanes.

For ninety-eight minutes, four jurisdictions watched the medical transport drift between authorities. Its captain retained command of patient conditions. Serein controlled the holding tug. Halcyon owned the scrubbers. Olan's office assigned the replacement ice. No one possessed the whole rescue, and no part could succeed alone.

When the tug's clamp closed, attitude fuel use fell to maintenance flow. The courier pod arrived twenty-three minutes before the first patient's filter reached its conservative limit. A transport medic installed both scrubbers and sent the fabrication supervisor a pressure trace proving no nickel contamination had entered.

“Proceed to Halcyon after the lock stabilizes,” Olan said.

The captain checked her renewed reserve. “We proceed when local traffic confirms the lane. Not before.”

Olan accepted the correction. His order had created the hold, but that did not grant him the pilot's judgment.

Edrin transmitted a list of the next five vessels whose support profiles conflicted with Meridian berths. “These are not exceptions,” he said. “They are the passengers your standard forgot.”

Olan attached the profiles to the emergency routing rules. From then on, controllers had to verify atmosphere class before assigning any alternate berth. The requirement slowed every automated solution by twelve seconds. Across the system, it prevented nine later assignments that would have produced unsafe transfers.

Watching the new rule propagate, Olan understood why Laleh objected to promises made after a correction. A rescue could repair immediate danger. It could not return the fuel, labor, and time consumed because his first map had omitted the need.

Olan watched each exception enter the emergency ledger. Central authority did not make consequences disappear. It gave them one address, and the address filled faster than staff could answer.

The defense cutters reached holding distance and turned their weapon planes away from the migration under explicit order. Solv requested permission to arm targeting radar. Olan refused the request. Passive observation could continue.

At hour three, Rook's fabrication transfer cleared the outer lattice. At hour four, one ore hauler remained twelve minutes short of its safe window. Its captain requested delay.

Serein local control objected. A twelve-minute delay increased heat at Cantilever 64, narrowing its ice-haul recovery margin. Rook offered to accept the debt through a later fabrication shutdown. Serein refused an unwitnessed promise and demanded the shutdown order first.

Olan issued a reciprocal condition. Rook's local authority signed the future load reduction. Serein accepted the twelve minutes. The ore hauler crossed.

No elegant system model had produced that bargain. Named jurisdictions had exposed what they could spend, then bound the exchange in a form both could audit. Olan stored the procedure for the Assembly review even as he prepared to centralize the phase that made it necessary.

At 18:40 Meridian time, every outer transit lane reported clear or stranded under support.

Olan placed his palm against the authorization rail.

The order displayed its duration, legal basis, expected power losses, known transit effects, and disputed external consequences. Laleh's challenge appeared as a linked filing. Tern precedent appeared beneath it, followed by the names of the dead whose inquiry had created the statute. Pell's name sat twenty-third.

Olan did not touch it.

He sealed the order.

Cantilever 61 hardened first. Its control surface narrowed phase variation until local noise reflected instead of passing through. Stations 62 and 63 followed. Across Asteron's resonant volume, blue arcs on the wall brightened into a continuous barrier.

Cantilever 64 accumulated heat faster than forecast. Serein cut two ice processors. Vela canceled three transfer windows. Halcyon reported a six-hour delay to school movements but no unsupported passengers. Meridian power remained steady.

The incoming field changed.

Nineteen adult bodies did not turn away. Their pressure spread along the new barrier, searching gradients that had just closed. Smaller masses gathered behind the larger structures. Their loose formations contracted where the phase wall reduced access to the magnetotail.

“Lock complete,” the chief controller said. “All stations inside recovery bounds.”

Olan looked at the red margins behind the phrase. “Publish the bounds.”

“System-wide?”

“All fourteen jurisdictions.”

The controller transmitted them. Serein received a recovery band sixteen percent narrower than Meridian's public summary had predicted. Laleh appended the discrepancy to her challenge before anyone in Olan's office drafted an explanation.

Hours later, after the emergency chamber transferred to night staff, Olan opened the phase-wake annex.

Cold storage delivered twelve files spanning thirty-seven years. The earliest measured local coherence failures after heavy gate cycles. Later reports followed residual disturbances beyond Pelagos, where no infrastructure existed to be damaged. Researchers had detected a moving front but lacked instruments to resolve its full scale. They called it the Pale Interval in private notes, after regions where repeated phase references lost agreement.

One file modeled effects on complex magnetoplasma. The study used simulated ecologies because no known organism occupied Pelagos's outer field. Under sustained exposure, correction loops lost synchronization, developmental structures failed, and distributed memory fragmented.

Olan read the methods before the conclusions. Sample assumptions came from Asteron's magnetotail. Phase intensity depended on accumulated gate exhaust. The researchers found correlation between high-use years and sharper distant signatures. They did not demonstrate a causal chain across interstellar distance.

The annex summary recommended continued observation and classification until external validation. Every director since had renewed the restriction through annual bundles. Olan's seal appeared on the last two renewals.

He opened live outer-field data beside the old studies.

Before the lock, Pale Interval readings arrived as a broad, noisy disturbance behind the migration. After the outer Cantilevers synchronized, the pattern sharpened. Phase coherence in Pelagos had increased, giving the distant incoherence a cleaner reference against which to appear. Or the lock itself had amplified a connected wake. The two explanations produced the same present measurement and radically different liability.

Olan requested a blind analysis from the night technical team without attaching the annex. They reported increased confidence in the front's direction and estimated arrival inside one hundred system days. They could not identify source.

Correlation, Olan wrote in his private note, did not establish causation.

The sentence carried legal accuracy. It also delayed what the annex demanded of him. If Pelagos's gates had produced the front, the migration might not have entered as an aggressor. It might have arrived pursued by human infrastructure at interstellar scale.

He marked the annex for sealed technical review, then set the review date three days away. The emergency window would approach renewal by then. A public causal claim without replication could collapse power allocation before any replacement existed. That argument belonged in every responsible brief. It also protected the order already bearing his name.

A live alert opened over the archive.

Aphelion had resolved the smaller associated masses more clearly under the lock. They were not debris. Hundreds traveled in patterned shelter behind the adult bodies. As the phase wall hardened, their formations narrowed. Several outer clusters pressed so close that individual signatures overlapped and dropped from the count.

Olan called the controller. “Can we reduce pressure at one station without opening the corridor?”

“Not without breaking coordinated lock. We can shape a shallow pocket near 71, but it increases debt at 64 and 65.”

“Serein stations.”

“Yes.”

On the display, the adult bodies widened their sails against the resonant barrier. Behind them, the smaller lives had nowhere to widen. The lock held every Cantilever inside human recovery bounds while those clustered bodies compressed into the shrinking shelter of the adults.

Chapter 7 — Min's Departure

The docking collar jammed with six centimeters left to travel.

Ivo Calder felt the obstruction through his boots before Vela Control reported it. The deck gave one short recoil, too clean for a pressure fault and too hard for the collar's ordinary complaint. He caught the service rail, opened the local actuator panel, and watched three amber load bars climb against a latch that claimed it had already withdrawn.

"Hold seal pressure," he told the bridge. "Do not cycle the collar."

Sana Vi answered from somewhere behind two bulkheads. "Vela wants our berth in nineteen minutes."

"Then Vela can send someone to explain nineteen minutes to a bent guide tooth."

He locked the actuator out by hand and reached into the panel. The maintenance recess had been built for narrower shoulders and people who did not expect a ship to move while they worked. His sleeve caught on an exposed fastener. Beyond the collar, Vela's inner ring rotated with patient authority, dragging the berth's apparent floor under the tug once every eighty-six seconds.

The latch sat half retracted. A splinter of black composite had wedged behind its guide plate, probably shaved from the previous ship's collar and missed by berth inspection. Ivo hooked it out with a probe. The fragment measured less than his thumbnail. It had stopped six thousand tonnes of correction tug at the door.

"Clear," he said.

Sana eased the collar forward. Metal seated around them, clamps engaged, and Vela's atmosphere pressed against Remainder's outer hatch. The berth clock showed twelve minutes remaining.

Ivo taped the composite shard to the inspection slate before opening the hatch. "Charge their inspection office for the delay."

"We're the ones occupying a priority berth."

"Then they can deduct twelve minutes from the apology."

Sana met him at the pressure vestibule with the mission packet tucked beneath one arm. Her hair had escaped its tie during docking and spread in the tug's weak spin like a dark instrument brush. "Your daughter is waiting outside quarantine. She has a bag."

Ivo looked past her. "One bag or leaving bags?"

"One large enough to start an argument."

The hatch indicator turned green.

Vela smelled of wet filters and hot bread from the concourse ovens. Its station planners had put food near arrival gates so travelers would remember welcome before they noticed the customs queue. Today no queue moved. Families occupied the floor between shuttered phase-gate counters, surrounded by tagged cases and scholarship trunks. Every departure display carried the same blunt line: OUTER TRANSIT SUSPENDED UNDER PHASE LOCK.

Min waited beside an atmosphere-service cart. She wore gray apprentice coveralls with the knees polished pale and held a blue transfer bag by its severed shoulder strap. The bag had been cut free from an automated sorter after the canceled launch. A strip of routing foil still showed her original destination, a fabrication college beyond Pelagos that Ivo had pretended not to look up too often.

He stopped before reaching her. The last message she had sent him contained four words: Do not come fix this.

"You docked in an evacuation berth," she said.

"Inspection berth. The yellow line changed while you were away at school."

"The yellow line says medical transfer."

He glanced down. "Poor paint discipline."

Min did not smile. She lifted the cut strap. "Can your inspection office repair this too?"

"My inspection office has thirty-two people and a cracked port fuel spine. It can repair anything if you allow bad language."

She turned toward the service corridor. Ivo followed, matching her pace through a crowd that had stopped being travelers and had not yet accepted another name. Public notices urged patience. Berth workers unloaded meal packs onto folding tables. Near the locked gate, a scholarship coordinator repeated the same explanation to six students: no launch window, no certified reopening date, housing guaranteed for five local nights.

Min pushed through a staff door using her apprentice band. The service passage beyond ran under the concourse, all pipe brackets and condensate channels. She set her bag on an upturned filter crate.

"My placement expires in nine days," she said. "The transport office says the college may defer me. May. The scholarship office says cancellation under system emergency does not preserve the phase-gate scholarship because the award requires physical arrival."

Ivo crouched over the broken strap. Its anchor had torn through fabric rather than releasing at the clasp. "Who wrote that condition?"

"People who assumed gates open."

"Give me their office."

"No."

He found a roll of mesh repair tape on the service cart and began threading it through the anchor. "I can get you onto an Authority courier if any lane opens inward. From Meridia there may be a priority gate before the lock renews."

"I said no."

"You said don't come. I was already ordered here."

"You diverted six hours."

"Four."

"The berth board says six."

"The berth board includes their broken collar."

Min put one boot against the crate to hold it steady while he pulled the mesh tight. The gesture came from years of helping him repair things neither of them wanted to discuss. She knew how much counterforce to give without asking. He hated that competence when it made her look older than seventeen.

"If you call someone," she said, "they move me ahead of another person."

"That depends on the seat."

"Every seat depends on another seat now."

The tape cinched into a serviceable loop. Ivo tested it with both hands. "Your future should not be the first thing surrendered because Brace locked a set of machines."

"My future cannot decide policy for billions either."

He sat back on his heels. Condensate clicked into a drain under the wall. Above them, a departure announcement began in three jurisdictional dialects and ended after the first sentence, cut off by an operator who had realized there were no departures left to announce.

"I didn't say it should," he said.

"You offered to pull strings before you asked what the lock is doing."

"I know what it's doing."

"To your ship. Not to Vela."

Min pointed through the pipe rack toward the habitat beyond. Two of Vela's atmosphere plants drew balancing power through Cantilever 67. The lock had not shut them down, but it had pushed their phase loads into local capacitors meant for maintenance intervals. Min's apprentice crew had spent the previous thirty hours changing scrubber beds early because the plants could no longer tolerate a peak draw.

"Plant Three has eleven days of normal reserve," she said. "Eighteen if we ration fabrication. The hospitals want their reserve protected. The gate hostels want air for forty thousand stranded people. So tell me which part your courier solves."

"It solves you being trapped here."

"I'm not trapped. I'm assigned to Plant Three."

"You are a student."

"I'm an atmosphere technician who was going to become a better one somewhere else. Those facts can occupy the same corridor."

Ivo stood. The repaired bag strap hung between them, strong enough for its load and ugly enough to reveal the failure. "I don't want your scholarship used as proof that the lock must stay or proof that it must break."

"Then stop using it."

She took the bag. For a moment he thought she meant to leave. Instead she opened it and removed a hard case packed between folded clothes. Inside lay twelve membrane sensors, their ceramic faces printed with the mark of a college laboratory.

"They let me keep the training kit," she said. "Plant Three's old sensors drift in high carbon load. These don't."

"The college will want them back."

"They can file a transport claim."

That almost earned a laugh from him. Almost.

A berth alarm pulsed twice through the service passage. Sana's voice arrived over his wrist board. "Captain, Aphelion cargo is at the hatch. Their signal ecologist has threatened to unpack it on the concourse if we miss handoff."

"Ren threatens with enthusiasm, not authority."

"The distinction has not slowed him."

Ivo closed the channel. Min had already shouldered the repaired bag.

"How long are you staying?" she asked.

"We leave when the clock is aboard."

"A clock."

"Portable shared clock. Ren Adek built a physical timing frame for the contact trial."

"With the things outside the boundary."

"Nearer now."

Min studied him the way she examined a suspect filter, looking for the pressure history rather than the clean exterior. "Are you carrying it to them?"

"Close enough for both sides to measure."

"Under the phase lock?"

"Around its edge."

"That answer belongs in an Authority notice."

The second berth alarm sounded. Ivo took the hard case from her before she could object and fitted it back among her clothes. "Plant Three should log those sensors as private emergency contribution. If they vanish into stores, the next budget will pretend the plant never needed them."

"I know how maintenance records work."

"I taught you the interesting half."

"You taught me to distrust anyone who calls a debt interesting."

They returned to the concourse together. At the quarantine barrier, Sana stood with Ren Adek and two cargo handlers beside a cylinder taller than Min. The container wore six layers of impact mesh and a handwritten warning: DO NOT ALLOW SYSTEM CLOCK TO CORRECT INPUT. Ren had added a second label beneath it: THIS MEANS YOU, BERTH SERVICES.

Four squat mass cases occupied the floor behind him.

"Those weren't in the manifest," Ivo said.

Ren touched one case with his boot. "Correction mass. Tungsten and conductive glass, calibrated to the clock's pulse set."

"How much?"

"Eight hundred kilos total."

"You added eight hundred kilos after our burn plan cleared."

"I informed Sana."

Sana said, "He informed me while the cases crossed quarantine."

"Then the communication lag has improved," Ren said.

Ivo checked the seals. Each case had separate custody marks from Aphelion Array and Vela local control. Olan Brace's emergency office had signed transit authority without accepting loss liability. That omission took only one empty field.

"What does the mass do?" Min asked.

Ren turned to her. "We offer a correction they can move. They have responded to predicted motion twice. The shared clock lets us establish when a change happened relative to a witnessed pulse."

"And if they move the tug?"

"Then Captain Calder expresses professional irritation."

"We leave enough range that professional irritation can retreat," Ivo said.

Sana handed him the mission packet. "Not according to the exclusion order."

The document had updated during docking. A defense perimeter now enclosed the nearest incoming body and a spread of smaller structures behind it. Civilian vessels could enter only under Cantilever Authority contact designation. Crossing the boundary voided standard rescue guarantees. A military ship might assist at its commander's discretion, but no rescue craft would be dispatched if recovery risked breaking formation.

Ivo read the fuel appendix twice. The return plan used the cracked port fuel spine at sixty-one percent load, within the revised limit if no large correction became necessary. The new mass reduced reserve margin by four hours of maneuvering.

"When did this arrive?"

"Seven minutes ago," Sana said.

"After docking commitment."

"Before departure commitment."

Min watched the two of them. "You can refuse?"

"The ship can," Sana said.

Ivo looked at her.

"You asked me to make consent procedure real," she continued. "This qualifies. No rescue guarantee, altered fuel margin, military boundary, unknown contact response."

Ren's attention moved from one officer to the other. "I can take the clock back."

"No," Ivo said.

Sana did not raise her voice. "That answer covers your own card."

The berth clock reached four minutes. Cargo handlers waited with the trained patience of people paid to witness command mistakes without inheriting them.

Ivo wanted to decide. He could feel the old reflex arrive as a tightening around the mission: protect Min by finding transport, protect the crew by absorbing the order, protect Pelagos by making uncertainty fit inside one person's willingness. Maintenance had taught him better in every context except fear. No engineer repaired a loaded spine alone because courage did not distribute torque.

"Crew assembly in the forward hold," he said. "Berth control can bill us."

Sana keyed the ship channel. "All hands, consent review. Forward hold in six minutes. Remote stations join live. No departure burn until review closes."

Min reached for his sleeve before he turned away. "You don't get to make them save you from choosing."

"No."

"And you don't get to make yourself the condition they can't refuse."

He looked at the repaired strap on her shoulder. "You have become inconveniently specific."

"Plant Three encourages precision."

The forward hold could fit thirty-two people only if no one respected personal space. Twenty-six crew came in person. Six joined from reactor watch, thermal control, and the docked bridge through flat video panes clipped to cargo rails. The shared clock occupied the center deck, still wrapped in impact mesh. Its internal pulse traveled as a faint blue line around the cylinder, independent of Remainder's timebase.

Sana distributed the crew consent cards from a maintenance drawer. They resembled ordinary work authorization wafers because she had built the procedure from tools the crew already trusted. Each card displayed the changed conditions, expected exposure, abort authority, and any risk that command could not quantify. A signature did not waive liability. It recorded terms under which a crew member agreed to proceed. Conditions could be appended and forced review if violated.

Ivo stood at the rail console rather than above them. "Mission change since last assent: defense exclusion perimeter, no guaranteed rescue beyond it, eight hundred kilos extra correction mass, and a return burn that uses the port spine at sixty-one percent load. Contact response may alter our orbit. We do not have a probability worth saying aloud."

Palo Dene from thermal control lifted his card. "If we don't cross, who does?"

"Military says it can run contact remotely. Aphelion says distance corrupts the timing test."

"That wasn't my question."

"No civilian crew has accepted it."

Mara Hest, the fuel lead, sat on a tie-down lug with grease still along one forearm. "Port spine limit assumes no correction above point-two meters per second. Put that on the card."

Sana entered it. The risk field changed across every wafer.

A reactor technician asked whether refusal meant reassignment. Sana said no. The Authority mission order assigned the vessel, not individual labor beyond ordinary duty under materially changed hazards. Ivo watched three people relax enough to read rather than perform bravery for one another.

Conditions accumulated. Thermal control required a retreat if the scarred radiators lost a second circulation loop. Medical demanded one bunk remain powered independently of the clock rig. Fuel crew required the correction cases jettisonable without bridge permission if the port spine exceeded its strain curve. Two deckhands wanted any alien mass transfer treated as a new condition, not folded into the current assent.

"Agreed," Ivo said.

Sana looked at him. "The cards record proposals first. Command answers after everyone can see the cost."

"Right."

His correction stung because Min stood by the hatch, outside crew procedure and close enough to witness it.

The discussion took forty-one minutes. Vela reassigned two arriving medical shuttles and sent Remainder a berth-overrun notice. Ivo approved the charge. No one made a speech. People asked about shielding, message lag, the defense flotilla's firing authority, and whether the shared clock could contaminate navigation. Ren answered only what his tests supported. When he did not know, Sana entered unknown rather than a reassuring range.

At last the cards opened for signature.

Ivo signed first with one condition of his own: Sana held abort authority if his judgment became compromised by injury or communications isolation. Her mouth tightened when she read it, then she signed beneath him.

One by one the crew consent cards turned green. Twenty-eight. Thirty. Thirty-one.

The final unsigned wafer belonged to Mara. She studied the projected fuel curve, changed the automatic retreat threshold by two percent, and looked toward Ivo.

"That leaves less room for your elegant rescue improvisation," she said.

"I have never performed an elegant one."

"Documented history disagrees."

He accepted the lower threshold. Mara signed.

All thirty-two cards held conditions. All thirty-two authorized departure.

Min met Ivo at the inner hatch while the cargo crew cleared the berth. She had left her blue bag with a Plant Three runner. Without it she looked less like a stranded student and more like the technician she had insisted on being.

"I won't call the scholarship office," he said.

"Good."

"I will call if a legal seat opens without displacement."

"You can send me the notice. I decide whether to use it."

The distinction cost him nothing practical and more pride than he expected. "Agreed."

She checked his suit collar seal, pressing the tab until its indicator flashed. "Come back before Plant Three uses all twelve sensors."

"Wasteful maintenance schedule."

"We learned from ships."

The hatch closed between them before either reached for an easier sentence.

Remainder undocked under berth protest and rolled clear of Vela's ring. The station's lit spokes turned behind the bridge displays, carrying canceled students, reserve tanks, and one atmosphere apprentice into another local morning. Ahead, defense beacons marked a boundary no rescue plan crossed. Their red intervals counted down against the tug's closing range.

Sana called the consent conditions through each station. Mara confirmed the port spine curve. Ren freed the shared clock from transport restraint, and its blue pulse began counting intervals no system authority could revise.

Ivo put both hands on the propulsion rail.

"Correction mass secure," Sana said. "Thirty-two assents current. Exclusion boundary in six hours."

The nearest alien body appeared as a bent field of light beyond the plotted lanes, large enough to distort the stars and careful enough not to touch any certified route.

Ivo authorized the burn. Remainder turned its scarred radiators toward Vela, opened the serviceable fuel spines, and drove for the moving light.

Chapter 8 — A Clock Between Bodies

The first pulse left Remainder before Ansa could stop it.

Its blue marker crossed the contact plot in Cartography Bay Four, clean and irreversible. Nine seconds later Ren's voice arrived from the tug with the warning that should have preceded it.

"Clock rig has coupled to their pressure field. We did not transmit. Repeat, that pulse came from induced current."

Ansa held up one hand although Ren could not see her yet. Around the bay, operators froze their command gestures. The portable shared clock sat six hundred thousand kilometers away, bolted into Remainder's forward hold. Its recorded pulse had already reached the nearest Orison body. Any response would begin before Aphelion could advise Ivo what to do.

"No correction burn," she said. "Preserve the induced event."

Her instruction entered the tightbeam queue. It would reach the tug after eleven seconds, pass through Sana Vi's judgment, then return as action confirmation another eleven seconds later. The total delay remained trivial beside orbital motion and unbearable inside a conversation.

Ren's live telemetry spread across the central plane. The clock's pulse had followed its programmed interval of seven seconds, but the Orison field had drawn it twenty-three milliseconds early. That difference carried no obvious meaning. It might reflect plasma density, sensor error, or the involuntary twitch of a body larger than the Array.

Ansa marked the event as uncontrolled. "Witness only. Do not answer yet."

Poll Rask stood in the supervisory well with Meridian Control open across his private display. Three defense observers occupied the rear consoles, each under orders not to interfere with a contact experiment the military perimeter had made possible. Their silence required visible effort.

The contact plot rendered the alien body as a bent luminous sheet. No single boundary held. Dense mineral nodes traveled inside transparent biological sails, while magnetic arches linked structures farther apart than cities. Witness swarms moved over those arches in dark flecks, vanished into field glare, then appeared somewhere the model had not predicted.

Remainder maintained a twelve-thousand-kilometer separation. Against the Orison scale, the heavy tug looked like filings beside a magnet.

"Captain reports no thrust response," said an operator.

Another marker formed near the alien body.

The Orison pressure field tightened once, relaxed, then tightened again. Seven seconds separated the first pair. Eleven separated the next. A third change came after thirteen.

Ren's delayed voice reached the bay. "They have the prime sequence."

"They have three intervals," Ansa said.

"Four now."

Seventeen seconds after the third pressure change, the field tightened again.

No one in Bay Four spoke. The defense observers began sending messages to Meridia. Ansa watched their custody tags multiply along the data record, each office trying to become the first institution that had recognized intelligence after spending two weeks classifying its effects as hazard.

She opened the experiment protocol. The sequence allowed one reply: clock pulses at nineteen and twenty-three seconds, then silence. Those intervals had never appeared in the observed alien field. If the Orison mirrored them, the shared clock would establish an ordered set without assuming units beyond witnessed duration.

"Authorize response one," she said.

The instruction left Aphelion. During its travel, the Orison emitted no further pressure changes. Remainder held position without thrust. Heat accumulated in the locked outer Cantilevers, widening the noise floor along every line Ansa needed to measure.

Cantilevers 61 through 72 remained under Olan Brace's emergency phase lock. Their fixed relation created a hard wall in the resonance model and a real one in space: phase pressure strengthened behind the network, narrowing the route between Asteron and the survey boundary. The Orison body kept its outer sail clear, but smaller forms crowded into its wake. Ansa could not tell whether those forms belonged to machinery, symbionts, or persons.

Nineteen seconds after Remainder's next pulse, the alien field changed.

Twenty-three seconds later, it changed again.

Ren laughed once over the delayed channel. The sound arrived stripped of triumph. "Ordered interval acknowledged."

Rask leaned toward Ansa's station. "Meridian wants a verbal status."

"They can read the plot."

"They want to know whether contact has occurred."

"A mirrored interval has occurred."

"That distinction will not survive public release."

"Then the release should include the distinction before it includes a conclusion."

Rask relayed her wording. Behind him, one defense observer left the room, perhaps to report where uncertainty could no longer embarrass him.

Ansa brought up the three prepared predictions. They had chosen bodies too small to threaten settlements and large enough for both sides to measure. Each event asked a different question through motion.

The first used a maintenance puck expelled from Remainder. Its release would create a new object with a known starting time. The clock would mark one pulse before release and another after. No correction mass would be offered. If the Orison repeated either interval around the puck's movement, they might establish order: before and after.

The second placed a tungsten case on a trajectory that crossed a thin outer filament of the alien sail. Human models could not know whether contact would damage it. Remainder would predict the crossing, then offer a safer alternative path by releasing conductive glass at a different angle. The Orison response could distinguish harmless passage from unwanted contact, though Ansa disliked how much interpretation they had already packed into two pieces of mass.

The third returned to Cantilever 68 and the shepherd moonlet. Ansa would transmit two future tracks. One preserved the certified orbit. The other reproduced the unexplained early crossing first measured on SD0. If the Orison chose between them, the original anomaly might acquire a reference.

"Begin prediction one," she said.

Ivo's tug received the order after the light delay. A port launcher pushed the maintenance puck into open space. The shared clock marked a seven-second pulse before release and an eleven-second pulse afterward. Range returns drew the puck's slow divergence from the tug.

For forty seconds, nothing changed near the Orison body.

Then witness swarms gathered along one magnetic arch. Their movement thickened the field on the side facing Remainder. The clock sensed a pressure change and emitted its own blue pulse at the programmed threshold.

"Feedback," Ren said. "The rig is responding to them responding to it."

Ansa cut the automatic threshold from the next command packet. A shared instrument could become a loop no participant controlled. "Record only. Manual pulse authority from now on."

Before her instruction reached the tug, the Orison field changed twice. Seven seconds divided the changes. A third arrived eleven seconds later. The sequence repeated, but one pressure peak aligned with the maintenance puck's release and another with its present position.

Ren overlaid the clock record. "They bracketed the event."

"Or measured its duration."

"Duration from what?"

"That is what the second prediction has to constrain."

The puck drifted untouched. The alien body had not corrected it, sampled it, or drawn it into the witness swarms. Ansa recorded the first result as ordered relation probable; semantic assignment unproven. Rask read the label and chose not to object.

A new priority order appeared from Olan's emergency office. DEFENSE PERIMETER TO HOLD CURRENT GEOMETRY. NO ALIEN MASS APPROACH WITHIN FIVE THOUSAND KILOMETERS OF CIVILIAN VESSEL.

"That invalidates prediction two," Ansa said.

Rask checked the order. "The tungsten case is not a civilian vessel."

"The Orison must approach the case to alter it. Their field can extend across the limit without their dense mass crossing. Defense may read that as approach."

"Then stay outside five thousand."

"We are trying to learn what they consider harmful. A weapons perimeter makes our own answer impossible to distinguish."

Rask's expression held weariness rather than disagreement. "Brace's office says the exclusion geometry remains a condition of the experiment."

Ansa opened a direct channel request. Olan could not answer within the contact window. Even if he watched live from Meridia, the combined network delay and review staff would turn one question into minutes.

She looked at Ivo's trajectory. Remainder could release both cases away from the tug, beyond the five-thousand-kilometer limit, while remaining close enough for the clock to witness the motion. That shifted physical risk onto the Orison. It also put the decision outside the defense perimeter's claimed protection.

"Move release point twelve thousand kilometers fieldward," she said. "Tug holds. Cases cross without escort."

Sana's answer came back twenty-four seconds later. "Captain accepts if both cases remain jettisonable from all navigation models. Crew conditions prohibit any chase after release."

"Accepted."

The tungsten case left first. A burst of cold gas placed it on the predicted crossing path. Its surface carried no sampler, transmitter, or explosive bolt. The shared clock pulsed at release. Conductive glass followed on the alternative track, slower and wider.

Ansa watched the projected tungsten line enter the Orison sail. Her model painted the crossing amber because it had no damage threshold. A defense system painted it red. The Orison field offered no color at all.

Witness swarms moved.

They did not approach the tungsten. A cluster gathered around the conductive glass and changed its charge through induction. The glass curved inward by less than a degree. That tiny correction altered its arrival time at the alien field.

At the same moment, pressure rose against the tungsten case. Its path bent away from the sail.

"They selected the alternate," Ren said.

The correction did more than prevent contact. It placed both cases into parallel trajectories, separated by three hundred meters. The witness swarms passed between them. Pressure pulses marked the tungsten's former crossing time, the changed crossing time, and the moment the swarms occupied the gap.

Ansa built three event brackets. "Before correction. After correction. Witness between paths."

"Harm and passage," Ren said.

"Possible harm. Accepted passage."

"You can keep adding caution until the message ends."

"And you can end the message before we know who said what."

His image arrived on a side pane after the video delay. He had strapped himself to a hold rail beside the clock, face gray under emergency lighting. Behind him, the blue pulse circled the cylinder. "They moved the dangerous mass away and put themselves on the safer line. If we cannot call that a distinction, no experiment can teach us one."

Ansa enlarged the witness-swarm track. The cluster had not merely occupied the gap. It entered where the conductive glass would pass, then withdrew before contact. A demonstration. Passage without collision, framed by the same clock.

She changed the result label: before; after; harmful crossing refused; passage demonstrated. Each term remained a human approximation, but the actions gave them shared edges.

A defense observer opened his channel without requesting the floor. "Your second term assigns preference. The field may have diverted an uncontrolled projectile while drawing useful material toward itself. That supports resource defense, not safe passage."

Ansa turned the two case tracks until the represented sail lay edge-on. "If resource defense explains it, the swarms should remain behind the protected boundary. They crossed the alternate path at the predicted time, then withdrew."

"They may have been collecting charge."

"The conductive glass lost less than a tenth of one percent."

"Enough for a sample."

Ren sent a new measurement from the tug. The glass case carried a shallow magnetic imprint where the witness swarms had surrounded it. No material had left either surface. The imprint reproduced the clock pulse before the swarms withdrew, an echo written into a human object without taking possession of it.

Ansa placed that measurement beside the demonstrated crossing. "Then their collection method returned the object and preserved our timing mark. We still cannot assign motive. We can assign sequence."

The observer tagged the record with contested interpretation. Rask glanced at the custody pane as if the phrase had damaged his equipment.

"Leave the tag," Ansa said. "If we strip disagreement now, the first public explanation will harden into doctrine."

"It may harden anyway," Rask replied.

"Then give later readers the fracture line."

She added the observer's alternative account beside her own. The contact record now carried two models tied to the same motion: threatened crossing answered by demonstration, or resource defense answered by sampling. Prediction three would have to distinguish warning from acquisition. Ansa revised its release sequence so the Orison could choose a track without receiving either object. The change removed a useful-material explanation at the cost of making the geometry harder to read.

Rask read over her shoulder. "Meridian will publish those words as translation."

"Publish the trajectories beside them."

"Most people do not read trajectories."

"Then most people should know the words depend on something they have not read."

The phase lock flared across the plot. Cantilever 66 dumped heat into its radiator field, adding a broad distortion. For six seconds, Ansa lost the weaker Orison pressure marks. The shared clock held its own pulse sequence, physically separate from the network timebase. When the field cleared, the alien body had shifted farther from the locked corridor.

The movement cost it. One luminous arch collapsed against the central sail and reformed at a tighter angle. Smaller bodies behind it compressed into a band along Asteron's outer magnetic flow.

"They are avoiding the Cantilever wall," Ansa said.

A defense analyst sent a text objection: Or maneuvering for leverage.

She attached the sail-deformation estimate. Intent remained uncertain. Cost did not.

Prediction three opened with two tracks for the shepherd moonlet. The first showed its certified orbit crossing Cantilever 68's reference plane at standard time. The second shifted the crossing earlier by exactly seventeen seconds, reproducing the SD0 anomaly. Both tracks carried uncertainty layers, clock references, and a future observation point. Ansa removed every label that only a human institution could understand.

"Transmit both," she said.

The tracks left Aphelion as timed pulses routed through the tug's shared clock. Prime intervals indexed the two options. A short mass release represented the plane. The maintenance puck represented the moonlet. Ren and Sana performed the sequence inside the hold while Ivo kept Remainder passive.

The Orison body responded before the full pattern completed.

Pressure gathered along the field line touching Cantilever 68's outer resonance. The same broad coupling Ansa had reconstructed on SD0 returned in miniature. It moved the maintenance puck across the represented plane on the early track.

Seventeen seconds separated the demonstrated crossing from the certified alternative.

Ansa's hands left the sensor rail. For two weeks she had treated the first wrong orbit as a statement aimed at a receiver. Now the Orison repeated it inside a negotiated reference frame. The anomaly did not announce arrival. It referred to a relation involving Cantilever 68.

"Warning," Ren said quietly.

This time she did not refuse the word. "Warning about what?"

The Orison field began another sequence.

Witness swarms spread across the luminous arch. They marked before and after using the brackets from prediction one. A dense portion of the sail crossed between two paths, repeating passage. Then the field pressed against the modeled Cantilever line. The maintenance puck lost coherence on the clock's sensors: not destroyed, but made impossible to locate within the agreed error.

"Harm," Rask said.

Ansa layered the three witnessed predictions over one another. Before. After. Harm. Passage. Cantilever 68 connected all of them, yet the sequence pointed beyond the station toward empty deep field.

No, not empty. The Aphelion Array's long survey held weak disturbances there, dismissed for decades as baseline drift. Ansa called up the oldest unassimilated field records. Each Cantilever activation had released a faint phase wake beyond Pelagos. Local models ended after the wake dropped below infrastructure thresholds. The disturbance itself continued.

She extended the lines without claiming a physical law the data had not proved. The accumulated front lay far outside the system, moving inward along the route from which the Orison migration had come.

Ren saw the construction over the contact channel. "Can we ask when?"

The shared clock could mark duration, but not ninety-four days through a single wait. Ansa needed a scale both sides had witnessed. Cantilever 68 supplied one: its phase cycle repeated every seventeen seconds at current lock. The number matched the SD0 moonlet's early crossing closely enough to turn anomaly into reference. She built nested groups of cycles, then indexed Pelagos rotational days against Aphelion's survey clock. The pattern grew long enough that light lag became the smallest complication.

"They may read the grouping as mass," an operator warned.

"Use empty pulses between day groups. No correction cases."

"Defense wants the trial ended."

"Defense can file its request against the experiment record."

Rask did not countermand her. That omission granted less authority than courage and more than procedure.

Remainder transmitted the time-scale query. The Orison body went still in a way no inert object could manage. Pressure fluctuations continued across its sail, but none touched the shared clock. Internal motion increased. Witness swarms rearranged into separate concentrations, joined, then separated again. Ansa could not tell whether the delay meant confusion, calculation, or disagreement.

Eight minutes passed. Cantilever heat climbed. The defense perimeter tightened behind the tug, changing its safe retreat vector. Sana sent a condition notice: another six minutes without response and Remainder would withdraw under the crew cards.

At five minutes, the oldest visible Orison arch moved.

It drew pressure from three surrounding bodies and impressed a sequence on the clock. Seventeen-second cycles formed units. The units gathered into days. The total stopped at ninety-four.

"SD94," Ren said.

Ansa verified the count twice. The predicted arrival aligned with the extrapolated phase front within nine hours, an error small enough to terrify her and large enough to preserve doubt.

The alien pattern continued. It placed the harm bracket around the approaching front. Then it traced the front backward, not toward the Orison bodies, but along the accumulated wake lines in Aphelion's oldest records.

One by one, twelve pressure marks appeared.

They matched Cantilevers 61 through 72.

The shared clock pulsed between the alien body and the locked stations. The Orison field repeated passage on its own inward trajectory, then harm on the Pale Interval's. Finally it pressed every wake line back to the Cantilevers that had made them.

Ansa saved the raw sequence before any office could name it. On the contact plot, SD94 burned beside the approaching front. Twelve bright lines connected that future arrival to Pelagos's own machines.

Chapter 9 — First-Arcs

Little Weight lost the juvenile while crossing the first magnetic fold.

One moment the first-arc rode inside the body's pressure shelter, close enough for its unfinished correction loops to borrow timing from Kehr. The next, Cantilever noise flattened the fold. The juvenile slid toward open shear with its mineral seeds exposed and its two young sails trying contradictory turns.

Kehr separated around the danger.

Ember-Side pushed outward, enlarging the shelter. Bent Morning took the debt, drawing the loose juvenile across a gradient harsh enough to blister three witness swarms from its outer arch. Silt Crown held the remaining first-arcs close. Harrow of Rain reached through all five bodies with the remembered shape of an older rescue: slow pressure, no pursuit, leave room for the frightened person to correct.
