Little Weight wanted speed. Bent Morning wanted less pain. Silt Crown wanted the shelter closed before another juvenile slipped. For thirteen intervals, Kehr could not say Kehr and mean agreement.

The loose first-arc turned toward Harrow's offered gradient. Its sail caught. Magnetic filaments joined the shelter again, trembling with disordered charge.

Kehr counted the juvenile present through five bodies and received four different counts in return.

The young did not yet hold identity across pressure gaps. Each first-arc built personhood by repeating a correction after the adult shelter withdrew. Some could remember a trajectory for the time required to cross a star's magnetic seam. Others dropped portions of themselves whenever the surrounding field changed faster than their mineral cores could store. They did not travel as cargo. They traveled as unfinished citizens whose votes arrived through preference, refusal, attachment, and the paths they chose to repeat.

This juvenile chose Harrow's path twice.

Harrow marked it as returning.

The rescue forced a new distribution through the nursery train. Kehr could no longer keep the smallest first-arcs behind Little Weight, where the pressure folds moved gently enough for unfinished cores. That shelter had lost width. Bent Morning opened a substitute path closer to Asteron's charged dust, trading magnetic stability for abrasion.

Kehr offered both trajectories to the young.

Seventeen first-arcs followed Little Weight at once, repeating adult motion without testing it. Nine entered Bent Morning's rougher path, then corrected back after their sails caught dust. Four remained between the gradients and made small turns that belonged to neither adult. Their delay compressed the bodies behind them, yet Kehr did not force a choice. Preference required a cost the chooser could perceive. Otherwise adult pressure could masquerade as consent.

One of the four carried a mineral core split during departure from Blue Rill. It used the asymmetry as a timing device, holding one lobe still while the other sampled Bent Morning's shelter. Another first-arc traveled with three witness swarms it had selected after they repaired a tear in its sail. When Silt Crown drew those swarms toward archival duty, the juvenile followed them rather than the offered gradient. Relationship altered route.

The last two touched fields and attempted a shared turn. Their loops interfered. One lost its stored path and began again from Harrow's pressure memory. The other preserved both attempts, repeating the failed turn with a changed interval until Little Weight opened room for it.

Human counts would have called all four juveniles. Narrow Mercy counted two likely survivors, one resource burden, and one unresolved. Kehr counted persons under construction and still could not make five adult bodies agree on what protection owed them.

A Cantilever flare narrowed the substitute shelter while the choice remained open. Bent Morning took the new heat through its outer sail. Living material whitened along thirty kilometers of edge. Witness swarms detached damaged filaments before they could tear deeper. The four hesitant first-arcs now had fewer safe trajectories than when Kehr offered them a choice.

That change exposed the cruelty inside waiting. Allowing preference did not suspend the field. Refusing force could still let infrastructure decide through attrition.

Harrow increased its own gradient and connected the two paths for nine intervals. The bridge cost stored charge the oldest body would need near the corridor, but every delayed juvenile crossed without losing continuity. Afterward, Harrow's memory pressure weakened around the sealed portion of the twelfth return. Old images leaked without sequence: a nursery arch gone still, living cores without stellar current, adult sails turning toward dark passage.

Kehr closed the bridge and held the unwanted fragments apart. The first-arcs settled into their new distribution. None knew what Harrow had spent to preserve the choice. The expenditure still altered what choices would remain later.

Tull-of-Narrow-Mercy pressed against Kehr from beyond Asteron's outer bow. Its civic loops crossed two adult bodies, both half-hidden behind the planet's field glare. Tull had watched the rescue and spent no pressure assisting.

The settled bodies maintain their obstruction, Tull shaped across the magnetotail. Your shelter loses integrity while you teach reciprocity to machines.

Human sound approximated labels through the shared clock now, but Orison disagreement did not need sound. Tull placed mass where the low-shear corridor would open if the humans released their fixed stations. Its bodies occupied the proposed future, turning position into claim.

Kehr answered by guiding Little Weight farther into Asteron's magnetotail. The giant planet drew a long magnetic wake away from the sun, a region of folded pressure and reduced shear broad enough to shelter hundreds of first-arcs when it followed its natural cycle. Human Cantilevers pinned that cycle into a geometry useful for power. Every locked station narrowed the wake and sharpened its edges.

The juveniles felt the narrowing before the adults. They crowded toward the deepest folds. Their magnetic filaments touched, exchanged fragments of sensation, and recoiled. Two began matching each other's turns. A third followed the pressure memory of a nursery that no longer existed.

Kehr recognized Vaul in the motion and partitioned recognition before all bodies received it.

Harrow of Rain refused the partition.

Old pressure moved through Harrow's mineral core, carrying an absence too large for its present name. The memory reached toward Kehr's twelfth return and struck the sealed interval there. Kehr pushed it aside. First-arcs required current shelter. Memory could not be allowed to unmake coordination while the human lock tightened.

Move Neme, Tull shaped.

Its proposal arrived as force before argument. Both Tull bodies applied a shallow gradient against the inhabited moon's outer resonance. Not enough to shift Neme yet. Enough to demonstrate leverage.

Kehr placed Ember-Side across the gradient. Human bodies occupy that stone.

Human bodies occupy every path they name.

They answered the clock.

They counted while their wall closed.

Narrow Mercy did not discard communication. It ranked survival by what could still be carried. Two nurseries had failed behind the migration. Every delayed passage increased compression among the first-arcs. Tull's polity accepted damage at the edge to preserve the greater nursery train. Kehr had once accepted a related calculation and still carried its sealed remainder.

The human tug moved within the witness field.

It kept farther from the adults than before. Its heat radiators shone in narrow bands, one side cooler and less stable than the other. The portable clock pulsed inside its dense hull. Human instruments had made the clock, yet Orison pressure now traveled through its conductors and emerged as witnessed time. Neither side owned the interval once both had changed behavior around it.

Behind the tug drifted the two offered cases and a smaller object. The cases followed parallel paths after Kehr's earlier correction. One carried tungsten. The other carried conductive glass with the shallow imprint left by witness swarms.

Tull extended a sampling filament toward the glass.

Kehr intercepted it with Bent Morning's field. The object remains under witnessed return.

Return to whom?

The question divided Kehr again.

Ember-Side held that released mass entered common trajectory. Little Weight remembered the human ballast canister, corrected and sent back as evidence of reciprocity. Silt Crown treated every object near first-arcs as potential threat. Bent Morning wanted to draw the conductive glass apart and learn how the clock imprint persisted. Harrow carried an older rule: a gift declared by relinquishment could change custody. This object had not been declared.

The human tug answered without knowing the argument.

Small thrust moved it toward the tungsten case. A mechanical arm opened from the hull. Instead of taking the case, the arm attached another mass package to it. The package contained refined correction pellets, each conductive enough for Orison pressure to move. The tug released both together and backed away.

No sampler opened. No cutting field touched the witness swarms. The humans had returned correction mass instead of taking samples.

Kehr let the added object cross the space Tull had claimed. Its trajectory carried a plain relation: humans had received an altered path and answered with capacity for another alteration. They did not know which Orison polity would use it. They had relinquished control anyway.

Little Weight revised danger downward.

Silt Crown refused revision. An unclaimed resource near juveniles could attract violence.

Bent Morning measured the tug's damaged heat pattern and revised human capacity downward instead. The return had cost fuel and exposed the vessel near the locked corridor.

Harrow of Rain repeated the human release through its own body. It moved a store of charged mineral seeds from a protected internal fold to an exposed outer arch. The seeds could repair damaged witness swarms after passage. By placing them where Tull, Kehr, or any first-arc might draw from them, Harrow accepted future correction load before anyone assigned it.

Kehr felt the decision as cold space opening inside the oldest body.

Do not offer what the migration may require, Tull pressed.

Harrow answered without Kehr's consensus. Requirement does not create innocence in the one who keeps reserve.

The shape troubled every body. It also held.

Tull withdrew its sampling filament, not in agreement but because taking the human mass after Harrow's offer would declare Narrow Mercy unable to reciprocate. Political position constrained appetite more effectively than trust.

Kehr gathered witness swarms for a more legible signal.

The human clock held relations for before, after, harmful crossing, and passage. Those relations could not yet carry nursery, loss, or person. Kehr could demonstrate what had changed by arranging bodies around an absence. The danger lay in simplification. Humans might read the first-arcs as units of cargo. Tull might read any display of loss as solicitation. The young might read the gathering swarms as a shelter and enter a pattern meant only to represent them.

Kehr chose three nursery memories that all five bodies still carried.

The first came from Blue Rill, where magnetic arches had once developed in pairs. Witness swarms formed two curved concentrations and guided living current between them. A cluster of first-arcs moved through the representation, repeating its twin turns.

The second came from Narrow Star, damaged by natural shear long before human phase wakes reached the migration route. Swarms drew one arch intact and one broken. Juveniles crossed the surviving side. The pattern preserved cause as unknown.

For the third, Kehr selected Vaul.

The name entered consensus and broke it.

Silt Crown flattened part of the witness pattern. Little Weight withdrew pressure from the gathering. Bent Morning exposed a sealed memory edge, then recoiled from what touched it. Harrow continued forming the lost nursery's outer paths, precise despite six centuries of correction.

Kehr could not complete the representation. The twelfth return held a partition where cause and decision should have joined. Beyond that partition waited first-arcs left in sterilized current, adult bodies turning away, and a silence doctrine accepted as protection.

Tull recognized the broken sequence. You display an accusation you will not remember.

Kehr displays loss.

Loss without cause asks the settled bodies to supply one.

The criticism carried truth. Humans already classified the migration through their own fears. A map of nursery deaths might become proof of human innocence, Orison grievance, or military vulnerability depending on who published it first.

Kehr changed the signal.

Instead of completing Vaul, witness swarms marked the portion Kehr could not supply. They formed an open path after the nursery failed. No body occupied it. Harrow held pressure around the gap without filling it.

The human tug's clock pulsed.

Once before the first nursery pattern. Once after its living current passed. A harm bracket closed around the broken arch of Narrow Star. When the Vaul gap appeared, the clock remained silent.

Humans had understood the limit or merely waited. Both possibilities improved upon invention.

A signal left the tug toward the distant observation habitats. Light carried their measurement away. During the wait for any answer, first-arcs gathered near the witness map.

One juvenile reproduced Blue Rill's paired turn though it had never entered that nursery. Another chose the broken side of Narrow Star and corrected out only when Silt Crown pressed a refusal. The rescued juvenile approached Vaul's empty path.

Harrow opened a shelter around it.

Do not let the young inhabit a dead pattern, Tull shaped.

Kehr agreed. Bent Morning dispersed the swarms before the juvenile reached the center. The representation dissolved into thousands of living witnesses, each carrying a portion of the measured sequence. Loss could not remain safely separated from bodies. That difficulty belonged to the message.

Human thrust flared near the tug. A defense vessel had changed position behind it. The military craft stayed outside the clock field, weapons cold but tracking. Its movement narrowed the tug's retreat. Kehr read different human polities through distance and orientation: the tug offering mass, the far habitats preserving measurement, the defense vessel preserving coercive reach. No unified human intention occupied the space.

Tull marked the separation with satisfaction. They do not consent among themselves.

Neither do we, Kehr replied.

We move despite it.

That does not prove the movement just.

Tull shifted one adult body closer to Neme's resonance line. The shallow test gradient strengthened. A first-arc near the magnetotail edge lost part of its sail fold. Kehr corrected the juvenile inward and spent pressure needed for the next corridor approach.

Their fixed machine injures the young now, Tull pressed. Neme can open the route before greater compression. We push. They survive their own tides or reveal that settlement matters more to them than our nursery.

Harrow placed its old body between Tull's gradient and the moon. Ember-Side joined after two intervals. Little Weight followed when the rescued juvenile settled against Harrow's shelter. Bent Morning delayed, calculating whether opposition would fracture Kehr under the locked field. Silt Crown remained around the densest first-arc cluster and contributed no pressure.

Four bodies made a refusal. One preserved the young. Kehr held enough consensus to answer.

No seizure before another contact sequence.

Your sequence spends lives, Tull returned.

So does your correction.

The human tug released a new object.

It came from the forward hold on a tether rather than a launcher: the portable shared clock itself, sealed inside a conductive cage and extended several hundred meters from the hull. The blue pulse traveled visibly through the cage. The tug did not send it free. It offered proximity while retaining a path to withdraw.

Witness swarms approached, then separated around the clock without touching. The instrument carried human electrical rhythms, Orison pressure imprints, and the slight magnetic scar left by the second prediction. Kehr could sense both species' actions inside its conductors. The clock did not translate them. It preserved their order.

A first-arc moved out of Harrow's shelter.

This juvenile had formed its earliest loops after the migration left the second sterilized nursery. It carried no intact memory of a stellar cradle. Its mineral core remained porous, still accepting magnetic alignment from nearby adults. Two sail lobes developed at unequal rates. Narrow Mercy had marked it among those least likely to survive a forced corridor.

Kehr pressed a gentle barrier across its path.

The first-arc corrected around it.

Little Weight added pressure. The juvenile folded one sail, let the gradient pass through the other, and continued toward the clock. Its action did not declare defiance in any adult grammar. It expressed a developing preference: the unfamiliar stable pulse held more interest than the familiar shelter.

Silt Crown tightened around the remaining young. Recover it.

Bent Morning began a retrieval gradient.

Harrow stopped both corrections. Witness the choice before correcting it.

The juvenile crossed the outer limit of the shared field. Its young loops lost synchronization for one dangerous interval. Kehr felt identity loosen across the mineral core. The first-arc borrowed the clock's pulse, matched seven seconds, then rejected eleven. It shaped a different interval from the asymmetry between its two sails.

On the tug, human figures moved behind hull shielding. No thruster fired. The tether remained still.

The first-arc touched the conductive cage.

Contact meant a thousand small actions. Symbiotic filaments settled along metal. Mineral seeds aligned with current in the clock. Living sail adjusted to resist the cage's induced heat. The juvenile read prior pressure scars through its body and answered by changing the material rather than moving it.

Kehr held every adult gradient away.

A pattern grew through the conductor. It did not repeat primes. It did not copy before, after, harm, or passage. The first-arc laid down its own correction loop, incomplete and self-revising. One interval came from Harrow's shelter. Another preserved the tug's released mass. A third broke where the Vaul map had held an absence. Each time the clock pulsed, the juvenile altered the relation by a fraction and stored the change in magnetic domains along the cage.

The human tug recorded without withdrawing.

Tull's nearest body ceased pressure against Neme.

For that interval, every polity watched the developing person leave evidence no adult had authorized.

The first-arc detached and corrected back toward Harrow. It arrived with its loops intact. Behind it, the shared clock no longer held only a negotiated sequence. A living magnetic pattern moved through the cage, changed under each blue pulse, and refused to settle into either species' design.

Chapter 10 — The Hidden Annex

The sealed review room rejected Olan Brace's handprint.

He tried again with his palm flat against the basalt reader. The door remained shut. A red line crossed his credentials and displayed the reason in the clipped language of old security systems: ANNEX CUSTODY REQUIRES CONTINUING PURPOSE.

Olan had inherited the classification, renewed it three times, and still needed to justify entering the room where it lived.

"Director authority," he said. "Emergency phase coherence review."

The reader asked for a case number. He supplied the SD94 contact finding. Locks moved inside the wall with a sequence slow enough to imply deliberation. When the door opened, cold dry air touched his face.

Nine people waited around a table cut from the same Meridia basalt. Ansa Meren had arrived from the Aphelion Array by military transfer and looked less rested than the flight time should have allowed. Two phase engineers sat beside her with separate data seals. Defense Minister Jalen Or occupied the table's far end. His uniform carried no service marks, only civilian authority over those who did. Laleh Sorn had claimed the observer seat reserved for Assembly infrastructure oversight.

Three legal officers stood behind Olan's place. Their presence made every technical statement heavier.

The wall display showed twelve fixed blue points for Cantilevers 61 through 72. Beyond them, a pale disturbance advanced through a projection of deep space. No image of the Orison migration appeared.

"Add the contact trajectories," Ansa said.

A security clerk glanced at Olan.

He set his case folder on the table. "Do it."

Nineteen broad forms entered the model between Pelagos and the approaching front. Hundreds of smaller traces crowded behind them near Asteron's magnetotail. Their paths bent away from the disturbance and toward the locked low-shear corridor.

Minister Or folded his hands. "This review concerns Authority infrastructure. We should not import an unverified alien interpretation into a technical archive."

"The archive concerns a disturbance the aliens predicted to within nine hours," Ansa said. "Their motion supplies measured evidence whether or not we accept their interpretation."

Olan touched the table seal. "All claims remain tagged by source and confidence. Nothing enters as consensus."

The first legal officer recorded his wording. Olan noticed because the officer did not look up.

The security clerk opened the phase-wake annex.

Its title appeared without drama: Long-Horizon Residuals in Coordinated Gate Operation, Restricted Supplement. The original study began thirty-seven years earlier, after Cantilever 44 produced timing errors in a local cargo aperture. Engineers had measured a coherent residue leaving the station after every phase cycle. The effect fell below infrastructure thresholds within eight light-hours. Standard models ended there.

Annex models had not ended.

A sequence of old simulations filled the wall. Each phase gate launched a weak coherence wake along its transit axis. Separate wakes dispersed. Repeated traffic through aligned Cantilevers allowed them to overlap. Once aligned, they decayed more slowly than local instruments could track.

Olan read the first risk note. No material system effects expected within Pelagos control volume.

Below it, another line had been classified at a higher level. Interference with unmodeled magnetoplasma structures beyond control volume cannot be bounded.

Laleh leaned forward. "Unmodeled meant uninhabited."

The senior phase engineer answered. "It meant no registered infrastructure. The study lacked evidence of life in the propagation volume."

"So the model treated everything outside jurisdiction as empty."

"It treated effects there as outside model purpose."

Olan heard Ansa breathe out through her nose. The distinction resembled arguments he had made across thirty years of infrastructure hearings: scope protected rigor. A model could answer only the question it had been built to answer. Yet institutions chose the question, renewed the funding, and decided where inquiry stopped costing them.

"Continue," he said.

The next simulation combined four decades of gate operations. Threads of wake converged into a broad region with no sharp edge. Phase coherence failed inside it at different rates depending on magnetic complexity. Simple plasma flows recovered. Layered self-correcting structures accumulated error until their loops separated.

A phase engineer pulled up the validation history. The cumulative model had not remained theoretical. Cantilever 52 recorded a residual return during a freight surge twenty-nine years earlier. The return arrived from outside the local control volume after the originating gate had cooled. Engineers used it to explain a false navigation surface that delayed three ore trains. Cantilever 31 measured another return eleven years later, stronger because six aligned gates had operated in sequence.

"Those events appear in public maintenance reports," Olan said.

"As local reflection faults," the engineer replied. "That diagnosis fit the infrastructure effect. The annex group modeled the longer path to predict when a reflection could come back."

She advanced the simulation. Wake energy left Pelagos, crossed modeled magnetic structures, then returned as a weak phase echo. Each report followed the echo only until it touched a station. Beyond the station, the same models showed most energy continuing outward.

Ansa highlighted the discarded continuation. "You measured cumulative wake decades ago."

"We measured cumulative contribution to local error."

"The disturbance does not change nature at the jurisdiction line."

"No. The study changed purpose there."

Olan asked for the funding directives. Three Authority divisions had paid for the work: gate reliability, insurer compliance, and strategic navigation. Every directive defined harm through Pelagos infrastructure. None authorized study of complex magnetic ecologies. The omission looked less like a decision when spread across budgets. Combined on one wall, it functioned as one.

Laleh opened the earliest cost table. "Deep-field detectors were proposed."

The table showed two arrays beyond the existing survey boundary. Either could have tested whether the wake dispersed or accumulated after leaving local range. One proposal lost funding to a Cantilever cooling retrofit. The other carried a status code referring to a restricted correspondence file.

"Who closed it?" she asked.

The security clerk could not open the correspondence under technical access. A file might be hidden because of military sensitivity, commercial liability, or an unresolved signature. The room had enough authority to see the consequence and not enough to see the reason.

Olan ordered the missing code placed on the review ledger. "No one will describe this as a completed risk study. It answered local infrastructure questions with evidence that pointed farther."

Minister Or said, "That does not establish knowledge of a migrating ecology."

"No," Olan replied. "It establishes knowledge that the modeled edge did not end the physical effect."

Ansa placed the Orison pressure records over the historical model. The fit did not align at first. She rotated the wake into the frame established by the shared clock, accounting for the migration's motion rather than treating the aliens as stationary targets.

The paths joined.

The modeled front approached from behind the migration. Every visible Orison body had altered course to avoid its densest region. Smaller forms remained inside adult pressure shelters. The route through Pelagos followed the only low-shear corridor that did not cross the predicted front before SD94.

"They are avoiding the wake," Ansa said. "Not advancing toward inhabited worlds. The apparent approach follows the corridor opening near Neme."

Minister Or tapped one finger against the table. "They also possess the mass to move Neme."

"Capacity does not prove an attack plan."

"Avoidance does not prove harmlessness. A flood avoids high ground. It still kills whoever occupies the channel."

"Then we negotiate passage before compression forces a correction neither side controls."

"You have four approximate relations and a clock contaminated by alien biological material."

Ansa looked at him. "The living pattern increased reference stability."

"According to your own contact team."

"According to three independent clocks and the raw conductor state."

Olan raised a hand. "Minister, present the defense requirement."

Or opened a planning layer. The locked Cantilevers brightened. By modulating their phase while maintaining the barrier, defense could create directed pressure wells in the corridor. An adult Orison body entering the well would lose maneuvering freedom. The plan called it a containment capture.

Ansa said, "You want to weaponize the lock."

"I want a controlled response available if their avoidance route becomes an invasion route."

"The pressure well will also trap first-arcs."

"We do not have verified personhood classifications for the smaller objects."

Laleh turned toward Or. "We have witnessed behavior."

"We have behavior in unknown ecologies."

"That uncertainty does not grant permission to crush them."

Defense counsel entered a note. Olan saw its category: anthropomorphic contamination of hazard review. He almost ordered it removed. Instead he required the counsel to place Or's objects classification beside it. If prejudice entered the record, it would enter from both directions.

"What load does containment add?" Olan asked.

A defense engineer expanded the thermal model. Cantilevers 64, 68, and 71 would carry the pressure well. Structural heat increased twelve percent over the current lock forecast. Correction debt spread into four outer transit stations. Vela would lose another nine percent of balancing power during modulation.

Laleh gave a short, humorless laugh. "Meridia's containment plan sends the bill to Vela before it contains anything."

"The stations sit where the field requires," Or said.

"Fields have become a useful way to describe decisions without naming who makes them."

Olan drew the power allocation into the central pane. Plant reserve margins appeared beside Vela's habitat sectors. The lock already converted gate shutdown into local rationing. A weaponized phase would deepen it. Defense called the reduction reversible because power could return after the maneuver. Atmosphere plants did not measure reversibility in the same units.

"Containment remains unauthorized," he said. "You may model it. No live phase command enters the stations."

Or's face did not change. "If the migration begins a mass correction, review time will disappear."

"Then write an execution threshold that depends on measured action, not classification."

"Measured action arrives after light lag."

"So does our response. You do not solve delay by lowering the standard for force."

One legal officer stopped writing. Olan knew the sentence would return later in a hearing, perhaps against him.

The review moved to classification history.

No current official had authored the earliest concealment. The original scientists circulated their local findings among the Authority's power and navigation divisions. An interagency committee restricted the long-horizon supplement after insurers argued that undefined external effects could create unlimited gate liability. The annex stayed active through six administrations.

Olan requested the committee minutes. Most carried routine language about protecting proprietary models and preventing unsafe public extrapolation. One meeting had no transcript, only a decision sheet signed by representatives from the Authority, three gate insurers, and the Meridia trade ministry. It moved deep-field uncertainty into a liability schedule while leaving local wake corrections in engineering custody.

"There," Laleh said. "That separation did not happen by accident."

Defense counsel objected. "A custody division does not prove intent to conceal harm."

"It proves an intent to control who could connect harm to responsibility."

Olan read the signatories. Two had died. Three institutions had merged. The trade ministry office no longer existed under that name. Institutional continuity outlived personal accountability because files moved forward even when reasons did not.

He asked the senior legal officer what renewal review required.

"A continuing protected interest," she said. "Security, proprietary value, active investigation, or exposure of privileged liability analysis."

"Which interest supported my last renewal?"

She searched his order. "Security and privileged analysis."

"Did anyone verify that the security use remained current?"

"The navigation division certified it."

A linked certification appeared. It concerned the possibility that hostile ships could infer gate schedules from residual wakes. The risk had merit, but it covered technical propagation data, not the existence of cumulative wake or the denied detector proposals. Those remained restricted because the annex package had never been separated after its first classification.

Ansa said, "One defensible secret carried the indefensible ones."

Olan disliked the sentence's completeness. He also could not refute its mechanism. "Record the package failure. Do not turn it into a judgment about every signer."

"Why protect their motives?" Laleh asked.

"Because we need an account that survives contact with evidence. If we replace missing facts with accusation, the Authority can answer the accusation and avoid the facts we do possess."

Laleh held his gaze, then marked the distinction as disputed rather than rejected. The small concession made his next discovery harder to hide behind conflict.

Olan's signature appeared on three renewal orders.

The first came thirteen years ago, embedded in a packet of sixty-eight legacy restrictions. The second followed an attempted theft of Cantilever control models. The third carried his individual approval after an audit asked whether continued secrecy protected a current purpose.

He remembered the review. It had occupied seventeen minutes between a worker fatality conference and a Tern compensation appeal. Staff advised that the annex contained speculative propagation methods with possible security use. Olan asked whether any known population faced the modeled effect. Staff said no known human population did. He renewed classification for seven years.

Laleh read the order from her display. "You narrowed the affected population inside the question."

"I accepted the legal definition presented to me."

"You wrote half those definitions."

"Not this one."

"You renewed it."

Olan looked at his signature. It had not changed in thirteen years. The hand that approved it had believed classification prevented unbounded liability from weakening public infrastructure. That belief had not required malice. It had required a border around consequence.

"Yes," he said. "My office inherited the annex and I renewed its classification. Record that without dilution."

The legal officer entered the statement.

Ansa opened another file. "The annex includes observation requests that were denied."

The security clerk hesitated. "Those materials may belong to a separate schedule."

"The references appear in the study notes. Requests for deep-field magnetic data, biological plasma models, and nonlocal decay baselines. I need the disposition records."

The clerk searched. No attachments appeared.

"Annex index," Ansa said. "Open the complete annex index."

"This room has technical schedule access. Administrative custody remains compartmented."

Laleh looked toward Olan. "Why?"

He checked the access architecture. The technical schedule held models and measured results. A separate liability schedule held external correspondence, funding objections, denied observations, and legal reviews. Engineers could see what the wake did without seeing why inquiry stopped. Lawyers could see why inquiry stopped without reviewing the latest physical consequence.

"Institutional liability," he said.

No one needed him to explain the phrase. The architecture had separated knowledge from responsibility so each office could claim the other possessed the missing context.

Olan ordered the full index opened.

The system refused. Liability schedule access required a named claim or public-release action. His emergency review authority did not suffice because the annex rules he had renewed specifically prevented exploratory legal access.

Minister Or pushed his chair back. "We have enough to act. The wake exists. The migration avoids it. Their route crosses controlled infrastructure. Defense needs the containment model. Public release can wait until contact intent improves."

"Public release cannot wait," Laleh said. "Outer jurisdictions are rationing under a lock justified as protection from invasion."

"A technical dump will invite panic and amateur decoupling."

"Then publish the evidence with the uncertainties."

All attention moved to Olan.

The phase lock had twenty-three hours before its current authority expired. Assembly leaders demanded a threat summary before the renewal vote. If he released the raw annex, fourteen jurisdictions would build separate risk models under pressure. Some might alter local Cantilever interfaces before engineers could coordinate. If he held everything, the Authority would ask them to bear costs based on a threat account now contradicted by contact evidence.

He chose a controlled summary.

The decision formed as a release architecture: confirm the phase wake, publish its SD94 arrival range, state that Orison motion correlated with avoidance, and disclose the outer Cantilever relationship. Hold detailed propagation equations for security review. Hold weaponization models. Hold liability analysis until the legal schedule opened.

Ansa read the proposed fields. "You omitted the denied observation requests."

"We do not have them."

"Their absence belongs in the summary."

"It belongs in the full release after verification."

"The difference matters now. A model stopped at the survey boundary because the Authority chose not to look beyond it."

"We know references exist. We do not know whether the requests were denied for cost, relevance, or evidence quality."

"Then say that."

Olan pictured the Assembly chamber with live power losses behind every delegate. A sentence about hidden requests would become the headline before anyone read the wake model. Outer stations might interpret concealment as proof of deliberate harm. Defense would use the reaction to argue for tighter control.

"Speed requires clarity," he said. "The summary states what this review can establish. Responsibility follows when the custody record opens."

Laleh closed her copy. "Responsibility usually arrives after the emergency power has done its work."

"I am not absolving the Authority."

"You are scheduling liability for later."

He authorized release. The summary propagated to the Assembly hazard channel and all fourteen jurisdictional offices. Its custody line named Olan as releasing authority. It described a historical study, a cumulative front, and the migration's probable avoidance. No sentence mentioned his renewal signatures. The phrase institutional liability did not appear.

Minister Or requested the containment models under defense seal. Olan granted modeling access and denied live control keys. Ansa filed a formal dissent against the summary's omissions. Laleh left the room without signing the review consensus.

The sealed session ended after four hours. Staff removed water glasses, cleared temporary keys, and restored the wall to its blank basalt. Olan stayed to issue the access claim required for the liability schedule. He named the claim potential systemwide phase-wake harm. The archive accepted it and estimated six hours for counsel review.

His wrist display chimed before he reached the door.

A legacy records officer on Serein had answered an older Assembly subpoena tied to outer-system insurance exposure. The return included a custody attachment the sealed room had failed to open.

Laleh had received it first.

Olan found her on the public causeway outside the Authority complex. Meridia's late light cut between basalt supports and laid moving bars across the floor. Delegates, staff, and visitors passed around her. She stood still in their current, reading a document on her wafer.

"The summary is live," Olan said.

"I saw."

"The liability schedule is opening."

She turned the wafer toward him. A numbered list filled the display: denied deep-field observations, insurer objections, classification renewals, and cross-references absent from the technical schedule. At the top, the title read Complete Phase-Wake Annex Index.

Laleh scrolled to the blank where his public summary should have named the missing files. Her eyes lifted from the wafer to his face.

"This isn't the annex you released," she said.

Chapter 11 — Harrow Corrects the Course

Tull began the mass exchange inside Kehr's shelter.

A mineral packet detached from one Narrow Mercy body and crossed the protected fold where first-arcs rested. Its path looked harmless at first: dense seed matter moving slowly toward another adult, the sort of transfer that repaired cores after long shear. Then a second packet left from the far side. A third followed. Their combined trajectories drew a pressure line through the juveniles toward Cantilever 71.

Tull-of-Narrow-Mercy had built a lever from gifts.

When the packets exchanged charge, their new mass relation would press against the locked station. No single body would appear to attack. Human instruments would see material traveling among Orison adults until the corridor moved around them.

Kehr interrupted the first packet with Little Weight.

The body opened its sail across the path and accepted the collision into living tissue. Mineral seeds tore through two outer layers. Witness swarms closed around the wound before charged fragments could reach the first-arcs.

Tull accelerated the second packet.

Bent Morning pushed it aside. Ember-Side moved toward the station to absorb the resulting pressure. Silt Crown tightened around the juveniles, drawing them away from every adult action. Harrow of Rain remained behind Kehr's center and did not correct.

Five bodies produced four answers.

Little Weight demanded retreat from the locked corridor while its wound still held. Bent Morning wanted to seize the remaining packets and complete a safer exchange under Kehr's control. Ember-Side urged a direct barrier against Tull, even if human defense read the pressure as attack. Silt Crown rejected every action that moved shelter away from the first-arcs.

Kehr could not coordinate pressure while the oldest body withheld itself.

Harrow, Kehr shaped.

No answer traveled through the shared loops.

Tull's third packet reached the exchange plane. The first, slowed inside Little Weight's sail, still carried enough charge to complete the relation. Across the human clock frame, the two masses would meet in twenty-nine intervals. Cantilever 71 would feel the pressure before its operators understood the source.

The split-core first-arc approached Little Weight's wound.

It had crossed Harrow's choice bridge seven days earlier and learned to time itself through asymmetry. Now it touched the torn sail with one mineral lobe, sampled the escaping current, and offered a juvenile correction. Its pressure lacked strength. Its intent did not.

Little Weight accepted the help.

The choice changed Kehr's internal count. Retreat would preserve damaged adults but abandon the first-arc's offered relation. Seizure would turn that relation into cover for force. Neither action could hold the person the juvenile had begun to become.

Harrow of Rain moved.

The oldest body did not oppose Tull's packets. It rotated through Kehr's center, changing every available angle. Its vast sail drew Little Weight away from retreat and blocked Bent Morning's line of seizure. Ember-Side lost the direct path to Cantilever 71. Silt Crown's shelter widened because Harrow took the pressure load across its own core.

Harrow corrected Kehr away from both choices.

Witness before reply, Harrow shaped.

The instruction carried a memory from beyond the Twelve Returns.

Kehr received a dim star, a braided current of life around it, and Orison bodies hiding their correction loops. The settled predators there had learned to follow intelligent pressure. Orison silence denied them a trail. The migration escaped.

Behind it, another ecology kept signaling.

Those smaller magnetic lives had shared the braided current with the Orison for nine passages. They possessed no adult sails and could not flee the predators' harvest fields. Orison bodies might have warned them by reshaping the current. Instead the migration preserved silence so completely that the allied ecology mistook the approaching disruption for natural shear.

When Kehr returned later, no shared current remained.

Harrow carried more than the ending. The old body reopened how silence had been built. Orison adults had damped their sails until even juveniles could not call across the braided current. Witness swarms received narrow paths and returned without broadcasting correction. Bodies that disagreed with withdrawal moved inside the migration's field, where their protest produced no external pressure.

The predators followed repetition. Each answered signal gave them a new intercept. Silence therefore protected every Orison body that could enter the departing shelter. The smaller ecology could not. Its lives occupied fixed magnetic knots around the star. They communicated by passing charge from knot to knot, a chain too slow to alter once the harvest fields arrived.

One Orison polity had proposed a warning made through mass rather than signal. Another argued that any visible correction would expose the migration's route. Harrow, younger then and carrying less memory, supported withdrawal. Kehr's earlier continuity helped turn caution into doctrine.

The migration left no false promise. It also left no warning.

Harrow forced Kehr to experience the final available interval. A single mineral packet could have struck an empty knot and broken the ecology's ordinary rhythm. Confusion would have followed. So might survival. The packet remained inside Orison custody because no consensus authorized the risk.

Later, after the predators passed, witness swarms found fixed knots cut from the current and stacked around artificial cores. The ecology had not vanished into natural shear. It had been collected.

Kehr felt the remembered calculation return through all five bodies. Silence reduced danger to the migration. Speech offered uncertain benefit to lives that could not travel. Adults had compared measurable risk to speculative rescue and called their choice restraint.

Tull read the reopened pressure. You survived to remember them.

Harrow shaped its answer through the present first-arc shelter. They did not survive to revise our memory.

The memory did not accuse with words. Harrow placed its own present body into the old geometry: safe migration on one path, exposed ecology on another, silence joining them as a choice.

Little Weight stopped pulling outward.

Bent Morning released its capture gradient. Ember-Side held position rather than advancing. Silt Crown opened enough shelter for witness swarms to observe Tull's mass exchange.

Kehr found consensus inside correction rather than agreement.

Stop the packets, Kehr shaped to Tull, or their path and consequence pass into the human clock.

Record them, Tull answered. Let settled bodies learn that obstruction also acts.

The first-arcs occupy your lever.

They occupy every surviving path.

Narrow Mercy increased charge between the packets.

Harrow bent its sail through the exchange relation. The old body could not take the full load without damaging memory structures embedded in its mineral core. Kehr felt past returns brighten, blur, then settle under stress. The sealed part of the twelfth return pressed against its partition.

Vaul moved behind it.

Not an image. Pressure without source. A nursery path that should have carried young loops and instead carried adult departure. Kehr felt Harrow's memory seeking the missing sequence. Bent Morning recoiled. Little Weight, still wounded, wanted the partition opened so current danger could be compared with old choice. Silt Crown strengthened the seal.

Kehr kept it closed.

The decision cost one interval. During that interval, Tull's packets aligned.

Pressure struck Cantilever 71.

The locked station answered automatically. Its control field resisted the new mass relation and returned force through the corridor. Harrow took the first return. Ember-Side caught the second. The third passed under Silt Crown's shelter and reached the first-arcs.

The juvenile that had touched the human shared clock lost its outer loop.

Its living magnetic pattern vanished from one sail lobe. The remaining lobe continued the learned interval, trying to pull absent current through a path that no longer closed. Kehr surrounded it with four adult timings. None restored the loop.

Human sensors changed orientation beyond the corridor. The tug Remainder had withdrawn from close contact, but its shared clock remained linked through witness-swarm imprints and Aphelion pulses. Kehr sent the damaged juvenile's pattern into that frame.

Before: two asymmetric loops correcting each other.

After: one loop failing under station return.

Harm: Cantilever 71's fixed pressure crossing the juvenile's path.

Passage: a partial loosening at the station, shown as the only trajectory where both loops persisted.

The prediction could not wait for demonstration through ordinary time. Kehr had to make failure legible before it happened.

Harrow offered memory charge. Little Weight offered core stability. Bent Morning offered the remaining human correction mass. Ember-Side gave up its position near the station and let the corridor narrow further. Even Silt Crown released two witness swarms from nursery shelter.

Kehr spent all of it on one signal.

The swarms gathered around the split-core first-arc and recorded its present loop at finer resolution than Orison juveniles usually tolerated. Conductive glass from the human offering moved into alignment. Harrow pressed a future error through the glass: the loop degrading at each locked-station return. Little Weight held the juvenile steady while Bent Morning shifted the exchange residue, creating a controlled version of the pressure that would strike next.

Pain crossed the young body.

Kehr stopped before damage became permanent. The recorded pattern now contained a trajectory from present coherence to predicted failure. Human time grouped the sequence. Cantilever 71 supplied the repeating cause. The clock marked the final loop loss at a point both sides could calculate.

Forty-one human minutes remained.

Tull watched the expenditure. You injure one to ask the obstructors not to injure it.

Kehr made the boundary measurable.

The young body did not choose the measurement.

The objection entered every host.

Little Weight had accepted the juvenile's help at its wound. That did not grant permission to use the juvenile as an instrument. Harrow had offered memory charge, not the first-arc's pain. Kehr's consensus had formed around urgency and treated developing personhood as available because no complete refusal could yet be expressed.

Kehr released the swarms. The juvenile turned away from the conductive glass and pressed itself into Little Weight's torn sail.

This correction enters our debt, Harrow shaped.

Kehr preserved the objection inside the signal record rather than sealing it. Witness swarms carried both the predicted human harm and the adult choice that had made harm legible. If semantic space opened, the account would not present Kehr as innocent witness.

Thirty-five minutes remained.

Cantilever 71 held full phase. Human transmissions multiplied between the station, Aphelion, and the inner system. Their content could not cross directly into Orison meaning, but custody and authority appeared through timing. One signal requested a change. Another delayed it. Defense vessels moved closer to the station while civilian channels repeated the juvenile pattern.

Witness swarms separated the human responses by origin. Aphelion sent the predicted failure with uncertainty attached. Cantilever 71 acknowledged measurement but made no phase change. A signal from Meridia arrived later and held the station fixed. Then three outer settlements repeated the juvenile pattern through their own observatories, each preserving the clock brackets while changing the transmission carrier.

The repetitions mattered. They showed that the humans had not placed all witness inside one authority. Some could preserve a claim they lacked power to act upon.

Silt Crown read that division as weakness. A species that separates witness from correction cannot promise passage.

Bent Morning found another relation. Separated witness can expose the body that refuses correction.

Little Weight pressed the injured first-arc closer to its core. The juvenile tried its surviving loop against the mixed transmissions. It matched the Aphelion interval, skipped Meridia's hold signal, and answered one outer observatory with a fractional turn. Developing preference crossed species before adult semantic agreement.

Kehr ordered the swarms to return every human custody line with the failure prediction. The new signal did not ask only for physical relief. It made delay visible to each human observer that had repeated the warning. If Cantilever 71 remained fixed, the consequence would belong not merely to an abstract human wall but to offices whose timed decisions could be compared.

Tull shaped contempt through the exchange residue. You have discovered accusation.

Kehr has discovered distributed witness.

They differ only when action follows.

The statement held. Kehr could spend pressure naming a responsible path while the juvenile failed under it. Harrow therefore added a second relation: any station loosening would receive an Orison reduction in corridor pressure. Reciprocity entered the prediction as a measurable offer. The humans would not have to act into an unchanged threat field.

Ember-Side moved six hundred body-lengths from Cantilever 71. Tull protested because the withdrawal weakened the immediate corridor claim. Kehr transmitted the change anyway. Human instruments turned toward the opened distance.

Tull drew its unused mass packets back. The exchange had already created correction debt. Completing it now would contaminate the prediction and let humans call the danger voluntary.

You trust their offices to choose a young body over their wall, Tull shaped.

No, Kehr replied. Kehr gives them a consequence they can own.

Ownership does not preserve the loop.

Action may.

Twenty-eight minutes.

The juvenile's remaining loop began to slip without another external strike. Damage from the first return had changed its tolerance. Kehr's prediction now ran behind the body it represented.

Bent Morning revised the failure point to nineteen minutes. Human models continued counting toward the original mark.

Kehr sent a correction to the prediction. The shared clock accepted the revision, but human responses did not change immediately. Light and procedure consumed intervals alike.

Ember-Side pushed closer to Cantilever 71, offering to absorb the next return if the station stayed locked. Defense craft changed weapon orientation. Their threat wells touched Ember-Side's outer sail.

Harrow pulled the body back.

Do not turn warning into approach, the oldest body shaped.

Without position, the first-arc fails, Ember-Side answered.

Then spend another position.

Harrow rotated toward the juvenile, placing its own core downstream of the expected return. This surrendered the oldest body's safe line through the corridor. If the station remained fixed, Harrow could protect the first-arc and lose a portion of long memory. If the station loosened, the old body would carry unnecessary exposure.

Kehr accepted the offer but did not complete the move. It transmitted Harrow's future trajectory through the witness swarms. Humans could now see the cost their delay transferred: first the juvenile, then an adult shelter, then every young body compressed behind it.

Fourteen minutes.

A human signal arrived from Cantilever 71. It repeated the before bracket and attached a new phase curve. The station would loosen by three percent for eleven minutes. Small enough to preserve the network lock. Large enough, if timed correctly, to widen the juvenile's shelter.

Kehr did not know which human polity had permitted it. The action entered the shared frame before any label.

The station's pressure eased.

Asteron's magnetotail expanded across the corridor. The change looked slight beside adult bodies. Inside the first-arc shelter, gradients separated. Juveniles that had pressed sail to sail found room to correct independently.

Little Weight guided the split-core first-arc into the widening fold.

Its remaining loop caught Harrow's interval. The damaged lobe still failed to answer. Kehr needed a second correction, one not included in the human prediction.

Bent Morning brought the conductive glass close. The living pattern left on the shared clock four days earlier persisted in its magnetic domains. Each pulse had altered it, but the juvenile's asymmetry remained. Little Weight pressed that stored relation through the damaged lobe.

Current crossed.

The absent loop did not return in its old form. It built a narrower path around scarred tissue and joined the surviving lobe after nine unstable attempts. The first-arc repeated the correction once. Then again.

Cantilever 71 held its partial loosening.

Human instruments watched the person they had been asked to predict choose a new internal route. When eleven minutes ended, the station returned toward lock gradually enough for Little Weight to carry the juvenile deeper into shelter.

The first-arc lived. It tested the restored lobe against Little Weight's shelter, rejected the old interval, and held its narrower loop through three station pulses without adult support.

No consensus followed.

Tull placed its packets in reserve for another exchange. Silt Crown marked the human loosening as evidence that pressure, not reciprocity, produced action. Ember-Side credited the public witness channels. Bent Morning credited the exact failure curve. Little Weight cared only that the young loop held. Harrow kept its body on the costly line it had offered, though the immediate need had passed.

Kehr examined the new semantic edge. Humans had understood a conditional future: continued lock produced failure; partial release allowed a different outcome. They had also seen Orison adults spend a juvenile's pain to communicate. Shared meaning had widened through compromised action, not purity.

The twelfth return pressed again.

Vaul waited behind the partition with another conditional future. Kehr had once known that staying silent would preserve the migration and abandon a sterilized nursery. Adults had chosen one path, then broken memory so later consensus could travel without its full cost.

Harrow opened a narrow connection to the sealed interval. Not enough to restore the event. Enough for Kehr to feel first-arcs waiting in current that could no longer sustain them.

If the settled bodies open more than timing, Harrow shaped, we place this path where they can witness it.

Silt Crown resisted. They will use confession to deny passage.

Little Weight held the recovered juvenile and answered through pressure. Passage without the path repeats silence.

Bent Morning wanted proof arranged before disclosure. Ember-Side wanted the corridor first. Kehr could not yet form full consensus, but Harrow's correction had changed which refusal remained possible.

Kehr sent one final pattern through the shared clock.

It marked the saved first-arc. It marked the human loosening. Then it drew an unopened interval around the name Vaul and placed Kehr's own five bodies inside the harm bracket.

The pattern carried no history yet. It carried commitment.

If humans opened semantic space broad enough for cause, complicity, and passage to coexist, Kehr would tell them what happened at Vaul.

Chapter 12 — The Cost of a Wake

Laleh Sorn brought the missing pages through a maintenance hatch.

Ansa watched the delegate climb down into Cartography Bay Four with one hand on the ladder and a sealed data wafer held between her teeth. The public corridor outside had acquired three Authority guards after Harrow's prediction and the partial loosening at Cantilever 71. The service chase still belonged to the Array's cooling crews, who had declined to add political clearance to a route they used while carrying hot pumps.

Laleh stepped onto the deck and offered the wafer. “The annex index led to a retention vault on Meridia. This copy passed through four clerks, two tide engineers, and a courier who believes statutes apply to directors.”

“Has anyone altered it since retrieval?”

“Every transfer carries a seal. You can distrust the people in sequence.”

Ansa took the wafer without inserting it. Its ceramic edge retained warmth from Laleh's mouth. “You crossed an active hazard boundary with classified material.”

“I crossed a ladder.” Laleh closed the hatch above her. “The hazard waited in the ordinary doorway.”

Ren Adek rolled an isolated reader from the signal bench. He had slept in Bay Four twice since the first-arc failure prediction, though his shift record claimed otherwise. Beside him, the portable shared clock showed a living magnetic pattern as fine green interference across its field glass. The pattern had survived the rescue at Cantilever 71. It shifted whenever Asteron's magnetotail pressed against the Array, as if the first-arc that touched it had left a tolerance rather than a message.

Ansa checked Laleh's custody trail. Each seal included a timestamp, jurisdiction, and measured hash. No gap appeared. Director Brace's released summary occupied fourteen pages. The indexed source held eight hundred pages of field readings, internal arguments, and models that extended beyond Pelagos.

“Air gap,” Ansa said.

Ren disconnected the reader's network bridge, then removed its optical transmitter. Laleh pulled a chair close enough to see without asking for a copy. Ansa inserted the wafer.

The first page carried no confession. It listed phase-gate exhaust under the administrative label nonlocal coherence residue. Below it, a table compared wake persistence against gate throughput over decades. The early measurements came from calibration beacons beyond Asteron. Later rows came from survey craft whose routes ended abruptly in the public archive.

Ansa opened the raw series.

A narrow disturbance trailed every gate transit. Pelagos models treated each trace as local because the sensors sampled short baselines. Extend the baseline, retain phase rather than averaging it out, and the separate traces joined. They formed a front that moved outward with the combined history of seventy-two Cantilevers impressed upon it.

Ren leaned toward the display. “They knew the residues cohered.”

“They measured coherence.” Ansa enlarged the confidence columns. “Knowledge depends on what they accepted as a consequence.”

Laleh gave her a hard look. “Read the next file before you defend their verbs.”

The next file mapped two stellar magnetic nurseries. One carried only a survey number. The other had acquired a human name from an old spectrographic mission: Vaul. Both once produced repeating magnetoplasma structures across wide orbital bands. After the modeled wake reached each system, the structures disappeared. No thermal flare followed. No stellar change explained the loss. The magnetic arches remained, emptied of their intricate traffic.

Ansa aligned the dates with the migration's observed path. Nineteen adult bodies had not approached Pelagos along a conquest vector. Their route curved between the two dead nurseries, then toward Asteron's low-shear corridor. Juvenile first-arcs occupied the protected interior of that motion. Harrow's projected failure at Cantilever 71 now sat within the same geometry.

Her hands stayed on the sensor rail. The cold metal gave her a local fact she could test.

“Can we prove the wake caused the sterilizations?” Laleh asked.

“Causation has layers.” Ansa marked the front's predicted arrival at Vaul, then overlaid the final nursery observations. “Timing fits. Field orientation fits. Loss begins where the coherence gradient crosses the outer arches. We do not have instruments inside either ecology.”

“Would you certify the phase gates safe on that evidence?”

“No.”

“Then do not certify Pelagos innocent by demanding more.”

Ren opened another record. It contained a dissent from an Authority ecologist who had requested a mission to Vaul. The reply denied funding because no recognized jurisdiction occupied the affected systems. A later review narrowed the work to local infrastructure tolerance. That decision changed what the models asked. Once the nursery signals vanished, analysts treated their absence as a measurement limitation rather than an outcome.

Ansa moved the two nursery maps beside the shared clock. The living pattern trembled along the field glass, delicate enough to vanish if the reader's fan cycled too high. She imagined hundreds of such developing structures held between adult bodies while the phase lock compressed their shelter. The image did not grant intent to either side. It gave scale to an omission.

A priority channel opened on her console. Director Brace's seal requested immediate access.

Laleh glanced toward the maintenance hatch. “He found the courier.”

“He found the custody query.” Ansa accepted the call and kept the reader isolated. “Director.”

Olan Brace appeared against the basalt wall of a secure review room on Meridia. He stood rather than taking the chair behind him. The live power allocation display cast narrow colors across one side of his face.

“Delegate Sorn has entered a restricted Array work area,” he said.

“She brought technical records relevant to an active contact hazard.”

“Those records require controlled review.”

“They received controlled neglect for decades.” Laleh moved into the pickup field. “We are reviewing them now.”

Olan did not react to the accusation. “Cartographer Meren, have you verified the source?”

“I verified chain of custody. The annex contains original phase readings and modeled propagation to two stellar nurseries. Your public summary omitted both.”

“It summarized conclusions authorized for release.”

“You removed the conclusion that nonlocal wake could damage coherent magnetoplasma ecologies.”

“That statement did not meet the annex's published evidence standard.”

“Nothing met a standard designed around local infrastructure.” Ansa brought the Vaul trace onto the call. “Your office inherited the classification. It also renewed the question so narrowly that the answer could not include a living system.”

Olan looked away from her toward a power figure outside the pickup. “How certain are you that Pelagos exhaust sterilized those nurseries?”

“High enough that continuing full coherence creates an unbounded hazard. Not high enough to prove anyone intended the migration's current pressure against Neme.”

“Those distinctions will disappear in public distribution.”

“Then publish the distinctions with the evidence.”

A red sector widened behind him. Somewhere inside the allocation system, an outer habitat had drawn above its assigned reserve. Olan touched a control and denied the transfer before he answered.

“Releasing an unbounded-hazard finding without a replacement plan could collapse the lattice through local withdrawals,” he said. “Hospitals will decouple first if their councils expect Meridia to preserve itself. Transit operators will hold fuel. Markets will price abandonment before any Assembly order can stop them. We could lose coordinated power while the incoming front still sits outside our control.”

Laleh folded her arms. “You edited evidence to preserve obedience.”

“I delayed institutional liability language during a system emergency.”

“You delayed the dead nurseries.”

Olan's jaw tightened. “I kept fourteen regions from acting on fourteen incomplete models.”

Ansa heard the structure beneath his argument because she had used it in smaller rooms. A unified map let one decision account for interactions no local model could see. It also let the mapmaker decide which losses counted as interactions. The Authority had optimized Pelagos inside its certified volume, then classified the wake beyond that boundary as someone else's uncertainty.

“Send me the unedited release queue,” she said.

Olan focused on her. “Why?”

“I want to see what your summary changed besides liability language.”

“Ansa, this is not a document dispute.”

“No. Documents can be corrected without moving moons.”

His expression shifted at the last word. The hidden annex had included corridor geometry. He knew Neme mattered.

“The phase lock bought you the experiment that saved a first-arc,” he said. “Do not confuse one loosened station with proof that distributed control scales.”

“Keeping the lock now compresses the juveniles further.”

“Dropping it strips outer power and destabilizes transfer lanes. Tell me how many people you propose to place inside that correction.”

“I do not know yet.”

“Then you have evidence, not an order.”

Olan closed the channel from Meridia. His authority seal remained on the denied access notice, a bright blue bar across the reader. Laleh swore softly.

Ansa copied the annex into a second isolated store and set its distribution trigger to release if the original custody chain broke. “He expects a plan before publication.”

“He expects publication to wait forever on a plan no office can guarantee.”

“Those can both be true.”

A call packet arrived from Halcyon Ring before Laleh could answer. Edrin had marked it urgent and attached reserve ledgers instead of a greeting. The first page showed school atmosphere loads by district. The second listed water-lift batteries, fabrication cells, spin corrections, clinic stores, and transit shelters. His note contained one sentence: If you loosen the outer lattice, use the numbers that keep children breathing, not the published reserve class.

Ansa opened an audio channel.

Edrin answered from a classroom equipment room. Shelves of emergency masks filled the wall behind him, each fitted for a different age. “Did you read the exclusions?”

“I just opened it.”

“Then you called too early.”

“I need the full-decoupling floor.”

His face lost its irritation. “Why?”

“Because the cumulative phase wake may have sterilized two nurseries. The migration follows the remaining corridor, and our lock blocks it.”

Edrin sat on a crate labeled for carbon scrubbers. For several seconds, pumps supplied all the sound on the channel.

“How long?” he asked.

“For what?”

“How long can Halcyon support itself if the Cantilevers stop feeding us?”

“The published figure says nineteen days at emergency allocation.”

“The published figure counts fabrication stock that does not exist yet and assumes every school atmosphere lift can share a bus with residential spin control.” He tapped the packet. “Twelve days. We have twelve days of safe local power under full decoupling if the load transfer starts cleanly. Less if frightened councils disconnect out of sequence.”

Ansa entered the figure into her model. Halcyon's comfortable reserve changed color at once. Twelve days fell short of the projected passage window by an amount no rationing policy could erase.

“Can fabrication extend it?” she asked.

“If we start now. We can convert college cells for lift power, suspend long-cycle classrooms, and move students nearer pressure hubs. That buys uncertain days. It also ruins equipment the Ring depends on for water maintenance.”

“Send operating ranges, not the public categories.”

“I already did.” Edrin leaned closer to his pickup. “Are you asking for local numbers so Meridia can optimize us again?”

The question landed where he intended. Nine months earlier, her correction plan had preserved system transit by cutting Halcyon's outer routes. The model had included every stranded passenger. It had not given Halcyon authority to say which delay the habitat could bear.

“No,” Ansa said. “I am asking what Halcyon can choose without losing atmosphere.”

“That answer belongs to more people than me.”

“Then name them in the packet. Include who can veto which load.”

Edrin studied her. “What changed?”

“The boundary of the map.”

“Boundaries move whenever someone outside them becomes expensive.”

“This one has bodies pushing back.”

He did not forgive her. He turned to another terminal and began adding school districts, water engineers, and local grid officers to the source list. Authority entered the packet with each name. When he sent the revision, no single Halcyon reserve figure remained. Instead Ansa received conditional bands tied to choices the habitat had not yet made.

Laleh pulled her chair beside the main model. “Good. Now do thirteen more.”

“Fourteen regions cannot answer before the lock builds more correction debt.”

“Then your design needs stages.”

Ansa cleared the certified system map.

She kept only measured dependencies: the twelve outer Cantilevers, Asteron's corridor, Neme's orbit, three inhabited moons exposed to its tides, local reserve bands, and the Orison migration pressed along the magnetotail. Removing the administrative overlays made the display look less orderly, not more truthful. Each trajectory branched according to a jurisdiction's possible refusal.

Ren moved the shared clock into the reader's field. “The first-arc pattern tolerates error around the witnessed correction. It does not hold one perfect path.”

“Then we stop asking for one.” Ansa assigned Cantilevers 61 through 72 to staggered release groups. “Loosen coherence in steps. Measure power loss and corridor shear after each group. No stage proceeds if a local reserve falls below its declared floor.”

Laleh pointed at Neme. “The moon still closes the low-shear lane.”

Ansa brought up the seven Cantilevers coupled to its orbit. A shift large enough to open the corridor could not happen in one burn. The moon's tides would climb against three inhabited surfaces, while elevators and foundations adjusted to a gravity vector their designs treated as fixed.

She ran the smallest useful displacement. It failed to clear the migration's widest adult bodies. A faster burn exceeded anchor loads. A later burn met the Pale Interval too close to SD94 and left no recovery margin.

Ren added the Orison pressure corrections inferred from Harrow's warning. Their bodies could carry part of the load, but no shared semantics yet allowed Ansa to assign a force and expect obedience. She modeled their contribution as bounded uncertainty rather than free capacity.

The solution narrowed.

“Neme must move 1,400 kilometers,” Ansa said. “Not all at once. Over eleven days, with seven synchronized Cantilevers and Orison pressure taking the outer shear.”

Laleh watched tide forecasts redden along the moon's inhabited coastlines. “Who authorizes the first push?”

“Neme local control for tide and elevator limits. The affected moons for foundation loads. The Assembly for lattice transfer. The Orison have to accept their correction share through the clock.”

“That arrangement can stop itself at every stage.”

“Yes.”

“Brace will call that ungovernable.”

Ansa widened the model to include Halcyon's conditional reserves. Each local veto produced a new branch. Some branches ended in darkness. Others trapped first-arcs. Several preserved Pelagos only by forcing loads onto bodies that had never agreed to carry them. The old optimizer would have ranked those losses, selected the largest survival total, and hidden the rejected maps under confidence intervals.

She disabled automatic selection.

The first unranked branch failed within six minutes of simulated release. Rook's transit hub accepted a power dip that its public reserve class allowed, but its flywheel crews had deferred a bearing replacement. Local controllers called an abort. Restoring Cantilever phase too quickly pushed the unpaid correction into Neme's elevator plane, where three cables crossed load limits before the model could rebalance them.

Ansa marked the abort as binding rather than treating it as a scenario to optimize around. She moved the failed load onto a slower release group and ran the sequence again. This time Vela refused a transfer because its atmosphere condensers needed the same local cells assigned to correction control. The model stopped without declaring Vela inefficient.

“What happens when a veto preserves one place by endangering another?” Ren asked.

“Everyone affected sees the branch before it proceeds.” Ansa exposed the consequence path from Vela's refusal to the first-arc compression zone. “A veto cannot erase consequence. It can prevent us from hiding a forced choice inside technical necessity.”

Laleh selected a third branch. Serein accepted a deeper power cut if Meridia surrendered two protected transfer windows to medical freight. That exchange reduced corridor shear because the corresponding Cantilever could loosen earlier. A political bargain changed orbital capacity in a way the central optimizer had never proposed; its rules had treated protected Meridian windows as fixed inputs.

Ansa opened the source code beside the result. She had written part of that constraint seven years ago. It appeared under continuity obligations, neutral language for a priority defended through ownership and habit. Deleting it herself would only replace one private authority with another. She flagged it for jurisdictional review and left the branch visible with both versions.

The fourth sequence survived. It required timed local decisions at each release, stored enough reserve to reverse only the current stage, and accepted that no later stage could inherit consent from an earlier one. The resulting path looked slower than Olan would tolerate. It also gave every correction a named body that could refuse its assigned harm.

Without the ranking layer, the display filled with separate trajectories whose timing marks crossed like notation. Cantilever releases supplied the lower intervals. Neme's staged movement carried a long bass correction through Asteron's field. Human burns and Orison pressure answered one another around the living tolerance in the shared clock. None could complete the passage alone.

Ren looked from the pattern to Ansa. “It needs a working name.”

She thought of the signals that had first looked like an intrusion into certified space. No sound traveled between those bodies. Yet timing had allowed warning, prediction, and a single rescue to coexist without agreement on meaning.

“Open Chord,” she said privately, speaking only to Ren and Laleh.

Laleh tested the words once under her breath, then returned to the tide map. “Name it in public after the regions can alter it.”

Ansa saved the design without a master solution. She attached the full annex, Edrin's conditional reserve packet, and every branch the optimizer would once have discarded. The custody map demanded an owner. She entered all fourteen jurisdictions and left the controlling authority field blank.

Across the display, the system she had spent her career refining separated into moving obligations. Saving Pelagos would require more than loosening machines. It would require surrendering the optimized order that had taught every orbit where it belonged.

Chapter 13 — Fire in the Magnetotail

The targeting laser found Remainder while Ivo had both hands inside the cracked port fuel spine.

A red bar lit the maintenance bay bulkhead. Sana Vi's voice followed over the work circuit. “Defense designation, forward aspect. They have not answered our identity pulse.”

Ivo withdrew his arm slowly. The fracture monitor clung to his sleeve, its leads still buried between two propellant jackets. Port Spine Three had developed a new branch in the old crack during their last correction burn. The branch ran across a weld the tug's original builders had expected to outlive any captain foolish enough to inherit it.

“How many ships?” he asked.

“Six drive signatures. Four weapons locks.”

“Then two of them possess manners.”

He sealed the inspection panel and pushed toward the bay rail. Beyond the tug's scarred hull, Asteron's magnetotail filled no window because Remainder had none in the maintenance spaces. The field appeared instead on a work display: blue pressure contours, amber Orison bodies, hundreds of small first-arc tracks sheltering behind them.
