Human stations sat farther sunward as white mechanical knots along the Cantilever lattice.

A mass transfer moved between two Orison adults. Witness swarms gathered charged mineral grains from one veil and carried them toward another in a broad luminous stream. The maneuver had repeated twice since the shared clock established a tolerated interval. Ivo understood only its logistics. A body short of conducting mass borrowed from a body with enough. Nothing in the motion aimed toward a human station.

The defense channel opened without waiting for consent. “Unregistered tug Remainder, clear engagement volume on vector six by eleven. Alien mass exchange meets hostile accumulation criteria.”

Sana answered from the bridge. “Defense Lead, this vessel carries the contact clock and operates under Aphelion hazard authorization. Your hostile classification conflicts with observed trajectory.”

“Clear the volume.”

Ivo clipped the fracture monitor to his belt and climbed the central trunk. “Ask what evidence would change their classification.”

“They closed our reply gate.”

“Efficient.”

The bridge had no captain's chair. Sana held the propulsion rail beside the status board while Bess Tal operated field geometry from the opposite console. Four crew members strapped into acceleration couches along the aft wall. The rest had already moved to burn stations under the standing contact alarm.

Ivo took the rail. Six defense craft entered the plot from sunward, their radiators folded behind armor. Their drive plumes cut narrow white lines through Asteron's distant blue. Weapons solutions converged on the witness-swarm transfer.

“How long to firing position?” he asked.

“Seven minutes at their current acceleration,” Bess said. “Less if the locks already carry remote release.”

“Can Aphelion countermand?”

Sana shook her head. “Light delay puts our last packet behind their targeting decision. Authority traffic reaches them through a military relay we cannot address directly.”

The shared clock rested in a shock cage beside her. Its living magnetic pattern shifted across the field glass, responding to pressure changes no human ear could hear. The pattern had taught them before, after, harm, and passage. It had not taught the defense flotilla patience.

Ivo opened shipwide. “Correction stations, give me a crossing burn between defense line and juvenile shelter. No one loads fuel until the path clears your card conditions.”

Thirty-two names populated the consent panel. Each card had been signed before they crossed the exclusion boundary, with individual conditions attached. Some allowed rescue burns but not deliberate collision. Others required a reserve path home. Two excluded any maneuver whose sole purpose involved protecting equipment. The cards had made their departure possible. They did not grant Ivo ownership of every later risk.

Bess built the burn. “We can cross in front of the first volley if Spine Three sustains eighty-six percent feed.”

“It has not sustained eighty-six since Vela.”

“It only has to lie once.”

Sana studied the crew panel. “Crossing burn fits twenty-nine cards. Three require a reachable retreat trajectory.”

“What does the fuel margin say?”

“It says reachable if the spine holds.”

Ivo hated conditional safety written by a damaged pipe. “Offer those three a transfer to the shielded service pod before ignition.”

The crew replies came quickly. No one transferred. The three amended their conditions to permit the crossing burn if Sana retained abort authority over the final thirty seconds.

“Recorded,” she said.

Ivo authorized fuel loading.

Before the pumps reached full pressure, Sana sent him the three amended cards for countersignature. One belonged to Hull Tech Marr, who had a six-year-old son on Rook and had prohibited any burn that left the tug without two independent maneuver systems. Her amendment accepted a single crossing if the service pod remained powered. Another came from Teren in sensor maintenance, whose debt contract could pass to his sister if his death occurred outside authorized operations. He required the mission code attached before ignition. The third belonged to Bess. She had written only that no captain could call an escape path reachable when the calculation depended on a known fracture.

Ivo signed Marr's pod reservation and Teren's mission code. He rejected Bess's proposed wording because it converted a judgment into a prohibition no damaged ship could satisfy.

“Then state the uncertainty,” Bess said from across the bridge.

He changed the route condition from reachable retreat to modeled retreat, dependent on Spine Three pressure above sixty percent. The consent board accepted the language. Bess read it twice before touching her approval key.

That exchange cost fifty-three seconds. The defense ships kept closing.

Sana assigned the service pod to Marr's hull team and checked its release bolts through a hardwired circuit. Ivo sent two propulsion crew aft to manual valve stations. If remote cutoff failed, they would close propellant behind the crack with wheels designed for gloved hands. No one could reach both stations during acceleration. Two people had to agree through the intercom before either valve moved.

“Port aft ready,” said Kam Oss.

“Port forward ready,” Nils answered.

The ordinary cadence of their reports steadied Ivo more than the rail. Crews survived bad machinery by dividing authority until one person's mistake could not finish the job. Captains liked to call that redundancy. Mechanics called it not trusting anyone alone.

A pulse crossed the shared clock. Its green pattern tightened, released, then tightened twice more. Bess placed the sequence beside Orison pressure readings.

“They are predicting our crossing,” she said.

“Or warning us away.”

“The tolerated error opens around our current burn window. It closes after the flotilla reaches weapon range.”

Ivo examined the tracks. The living pattern did not name Remainder, but it responded to the tug's drive preparations and the defense approach. The source cohort had watched both. Somewhere across distributed alien bodies, disagreement might already be changing who carried which risk.

He transmitted the planned crossing through the clock as three measured positions: tug before, tug between, tug clear. The first-arc pattern flexed around the middle position. Adult pressure shifted in answer, opening a narrow lane among their veils.

“They understood enough to make room,” Sana said.

“Put that in the observation log, not the intent field.”

A call arrived from Picket Three, the nearest human maintenance station. Its supervisor spoke too quickly, with alarms audible behind him. “Defense ordered us to shelter internal. We cannot retract the radiator mast before their firing window.”

Ivo found the station on the plot. Forty-one people occupied a frame built around a Cantilever sensor head. The mast extended across the defense line like a finger too slow to withdraw.

“Can you release it?” he asked.

“Explosive bolts failed inspection last month. Replacement charges remain on the tender they just told to evacuate.”

“Kill coolant flow and rotate the station nine degrees counterfield. The mast may clear the widest solution.”

“That puts our docking collar toward the Orison body.”

“Yes.”

A pause followed. Hostile classification had turned orientation into allegiance. Ivo could hear the supervisor deciding whether survival permitted pointing machinery toward a body the defense intended to shoot.

“We are rotating,” she said.

Two more stations requested geometry. Sana took one while Bess sent the other to navigation. Their local corrections changed Remainder's best crossing line. Ivo shifted the burn nine hundred meters tailward, sacrificing part of the tug's mass shadow to keep Picket Three outside the first projectile continuation.

The amended cards remained valid. Barely.

A defense packet finally entered their receive queue. It contained no response to Sana's challenge. The flotilla supplied a machine-generated threat table that ranked the witness-swarm transfer by accumulated mass, magnetic charge, and proximity to Cantilever infrastructure. Every category described a condition. None measured an action.

Ivo highlighted the line marked coordinated alien concealment. Its supporting evidence showed adult Orison bodies changing pressure around first-arcs.

“They classified sheltering as concealment,” he said.

Sana read the table. “That lets any attempt to protect the juveniles confirm hostility.”

“And any juvenile left exposed becomes an acceptable target.”

The table carried an Authority legal reference, but its authorization seal belonged to defense command. Someone had built a rule that could not observe restraint because the rule renamed restraint before observation arrived.

Ivo attached the threat table to the raw record. “If they fire, this goes with the tracks.”

“If we survive to send it,” Bess said.

“We are maintenance traffic. We complain automatically.”

Her mouth moved toward a smile and stopped when the weapons locks tightened.

Remainder rolled until its reinforced belly faced the defense craft. Heat shutters closed over maintenance ports. The port feed pressure climbed through green and entered amber. Ivo felt each pump engage through the rail, a sequence of shudders inherited from six decades of repairs. Good ships concealed effort. Tugs announced which parts had agreed to cooperate.

At four minutes, the defense flotilla changed formation. Two craft climbed above the magnetic plane. Three spread along it. The sixth remained aft as a relay. Their weapons locks separated, no longer converging on one mass stream.

“They have targets inside the juvenile shelter,” Bess said.

The plot resolved small brackets around first-arc clusters. One bracket enclosed the living pattern's source cohort, the juveniles nearest the shared-clock trajectory.

Ivo keyed the open emergency band. “Defense Lead, your locks include developing Orison bodies. We have observed no attack vector. Hold fire for witnessed prediction.”

No answer came.

The nearest adult Orison body altered magnetic pressure. Its veil folded, pushing first-arcs deeper into Asteron's tail. The defense plot interpreted the pressure rise as weapons charging. Red threat icons multiplied.

“Two minutes,” Sana said.

“Begin.”

The tug's main drive struck.

Acceleration drove Ivo into the rail harness. The old ship rotated around him as loose pain: shoulders, jaw, the half-healed break in his left thumb. Spine Three's pressure trace climbed to the requested feed. The fracture monitor on his belt vibrated once, then again.

Their crossing line carried them between the flotilla and the first-arcs. It also took them through the edge of the witness-swarm transfer. The swarms reacted before contact, parting around Remainder in paired curves. Mineral grains struck the shield like dry rain. Their motion preserved the tug's safety envelope with a precision that no attacking formation required.

“Record every track,” Ivo said.

“Already taking it,” Sana replied.

The first defense craft fired.

Its projectile carried no explosive. At orbital speed, shaped mass supplied enough violence. The slug crossed the field plot too quickly for the bridge display to animate honestly. One instant it left the weapon rail. The next, an Orison adult threw conducting sail into its path.

The impact opened a white wound across the veil.

Charge rushed along biological threads kilometers wide. The adult curled around the strike, bleeding mineral plasma into Asteron's field. Behind it, three human maintenance stations fell out of the projectile's continued line. Their remote tracks stayed green.

Four more weapons fired.

Remainder had not completed its crossing. Ivo ordered lateral correction, placing the tug's mass shadow across the largest first-arc cluster. Bess executed without comment. Spine Three pressure dipped, recovered, then spiked above its fracture limit.

The second volley arrived as broken light.

An adult shielded two human relay platforms. Another spread its body across a Cantilever service station whose crew had spent the week calling the migration hostile. Witness swarms abandoned their transfer and wrapped charged arcs around metal structures to drain impact energy. The maneuver exposed the juveniles they had been sheltering.

A projectile passed beneath Remainder's belly by eighty meters. Its field wake tore first-arcs from the cluster. Some regained their pressure relationships. Others collapsed into disconnected glows.

Bess counted because someone had to. “Seven lost. Eleven. Eighteen.”

Ivo gripped the rail hard enough to aggravate his thumb. The plot kept resolving failures as individual loops ceased correcting. The number rose once more.

“Twenty-three,” Bess said. “Twenty-three first-arcs no longer coherent.”

The count held.

No bridge alarm marked them as people. Their tracks simply changed from amber to gray.

The port feed pressure vanished.

Remainder yawed out of the crossing line. Sana killed the main drive before the imbalance could spin them through the witness swarms. Emergency straps caught the bridge crew while unsecured tools struck bulkheads elsewhere in the ship.

“Spine Three isolated,” engineering reported. “Crack crossed the outer jacket. We have propellant trapped between valves.”

Ivo pulled the fracture monitor from his belt. Its screen showed a branching red line nearly around the pipe circumference. The cracked port fuel spine had survived the promised burn and lost the right to promise another.

Defense weapons began charging again.

“Give me starboard main,” Ivo said. “We can push closer and cover the surviving cluster.”

Sana did not move her hand from the cutoff. “That burn has no balanced feed.”

“We can compensate through attitude jets.”

“For forty seconds. After that we tumble.”

“Forty seconds changes their firing solution.”

“It also removes every escape path.”

Ivo opened the crew consent panel. Several cards flashed invalid because the fuel failure had changed their conditions. He could issue a captain's emergency override. The ship's code reserved one for immediate protection of crew life. If he classified the first-arcs as persons under rescue, the language might stretch far enough to accept his order.

“Prepare override,” he said.

Sana placed her palm over the authorization plate. “No.”

“They are charging a second volley.”

“Your order commits thirty-two people to certain hull loss under conditions they did not accept.”

“I can take manual control and send everyone aft.”

“That changes where they die, not whether they consent.”

The defense locks tightened around the surviving juveniles. Ivo could see a burn that might help. Seeing it felt like authority. The maintenance rail under his hand told a different truth: one ship, one failed feed, people distributed through compartments that could not detach from his decision.

“Captain,” Sana said, quieter now, “you do not get to turn their conditions into your courage.”

Bess kept working the plot. “Adult bodies are moving again.”

Ivo looked up.

Three Orison adults shifted between the flotilla and the human stations. They did not shelter only their own. One spread across the projected line to Cantilever 69. Another took position ahead of two habitat tenders unable to burn clear. The wounded adult remained in front of the maintenance stations, though curling its veil away would have reduced further injury.

The geometry repeated the same distinction the witness swarms had shown around Remainder: avoidance of human bodies despite human fire.

“Hold the burn,” Ivo said.

Sana released the authorization plate but not the cutoff. “Held.”

“Bess, build sensor proof. Raw tracks, firing timestamps, adult pressure changes, every protected human station.”

“Defense relay will classify our feed.”

“Then don't use it.”

They had three paths out of the magnetotail. One narrowbeam returned through the Authority hazard network. A second passed through the military relay and would never survive. The third used maintenance telemetry shared by correction tugs, station engineers, and Cantilever crews across Pelagos. It carried no priority seal and no central editorial gate. It also lacked bandwidth for the full field model.

Ivo unstrapped from the rail. “Strip everything except measured position, clock source, and custody.”

Sana followed his intent. “No interpretation.”

“Put interpretation in a separate packet. Let the tracks arrive first.”

Around them, the crew turned observation into something that could outrun command. Bess compressed trajectories without smoothing them. Engineering attached Spine Three's vibration record, which fixed Remainder's location during the volley. Navigation added independent clocks from Asteron stations. Sana signed each layer, then routed copies to every crew member whose console had recorded the field.

The defense lead reopened the channel. “Remainder, cease unauthorized transmission and clear engagement volume. Alien bodies continue hostile shielding behavior.”

Ivo almost laughed. “You fired through them toward your own stations.”

“Your statement conflicts with defense classification.”

“Our instruments do not.”

A warning appeared over the maintenance telemetry route. Authority traffic control had noticed the packet size and requested a distribution hold pending hazard review.

Sana read it. “Censor gate in ninety seconds.”

“Can we complete before closure?”

“At ordinary priority, no.”

Ivo examined the routing map. Correction tugs reported fuel failures system-wide because a bad batch could kill crews before administrators finished assigning blame. Those alerts preempted commercial traffic and replicated automatically at every maintenance depot.

He attached the fractured-spine record as the carrier packet. “Legitimate critical failure. The field tracks explain how it happened.”

“That will wake every tug engineer in Pelagos,” Bess said.

“Good. They know how to preserve raw data.”

The second volley did not come. Orison adults kept their shields spread. Perhaps the flotilla had seen the contradiction. Perhaps someone waited for a cleaner excuse.

Sana initiated transmission.

Progress bars advanced across thirty-two crew consoles. The defense relay tried to close the route. Vela Maintenance acknowledged first, then Rook. Neme's tide service mirrored the packet. Halcyon fabrication received a partial copy and requested missing frames from Serein. With each acknowledgment, censorship required another jurisdiction to cooperate.

“Authority hold in ten,” Sana said.

The last raw segment left Remainder. Its local copy remained under Ivo's seal, but the custody map now spread beyond the ship.

At zero, the official hazard route went dark.

The maintenance network stayed lit. Across Pelagos, sensor proof of the volley multiplied through stations the Authority did not own quickly enough to silence.

Chapter 14 — Fourteen Maps

Someone projected the dead first-arcs across the Assembly causeway before Olan reached the chamber.

Twenty-three gray loops moved over the basalt under his shoes. Each began as a measured amber trajectory copied from Remainder's sensor proof, then broke at the recorded instant of the volley. Maintenance depots had added the names of the stations the Orison adults shielded. No speech accompanied the display. People crossing toward work stepped around the loops as if the trajectories occupied physical space.

Olan's security officer asked permission to clear the projection.

“On what ground?”

“Unauthorized use of civic infrastructure.”

“The causeway has a public display license.”

“It does not include casualty simulations.”

“Then cite the license violation after the session.” Olan walked through a gap between two failed tracks. “Do not erase evidence before a debate about evidence.”

The officer transmitted the instruction with visible reluctance. At the chamber doors, reporters shouted questions about the defense authorization. Others asked when Olan had learned of the phase wake. One held up a copy of the edited annex summary with every omission rendered as a black bar.

He answered none of them. The session rules protected the walk from press interrogation because delegates needed a route into deliberation. The same rule could turn necessary quiet into a picture of guilt.

Inside, the chamber had replaced its usual system model with fourteen jurisdictional feeds. They occupied the basalt wall behind the delegate floor, equal in area and impossible to reconcile at a glance. Each map displayed the same Pelagos. None displayed the same risk.

Meridia colored power continuity in red and migration pressure in amber. Serein foregrounded reserve envelopes around shelters and ice works. Halcyon Ring rendered school atmosphere lifts as thousands of tiny blue loads, turning a broad blackout band into individual rooms. Vela showed canceled transit futures and fabrication capacity. Neme's feed traced tide displacement against elevators, foundations, and three coastal cities. Rook measured fuel access by contract habitat rather than by region.

The remaining jurisdictions supplied their own omissions. Some ignored Orison bodies because no legal category accepted them. Two treated first-arcs as civilian dependents. One labeled every adult veil a military mass. Another displayed the Pale Interval as a probability cloud that ended at its territorial boundary, though the physical front would not.

Olan stood at the central lectern while clerks verified attendance. Fourteen jurisdictions had sent voting delegates in person or by certified light link. The longest delay came from an outer habitat whose representative appeared eleven minutes behind every question. Assembly procedure paused the voting window to preserve her participation, but discussion continued around a person temporarily trapped in an earlier version of the room.

Laleh Sorn sat at Serein's station with the full annex open on her desk. She had entered it into the public docket before dawn. Olan's office could still challenge chain of custody. Doing so now would confirm every suspicion about concealment and delay the evidence without changing it.

Defense Minister Aven approached the lectern. “The firing board complied with the hostile-accumulation directive.”

“Your directive classified sheltering as concealment.”

“It classified unregistered magnetic mass around strategic infrastructure.”

“The Orison shielded three human stations from our projectiles.”

Aven lowered his voice. “After moving mass into weapon range.”

Olan looked toward the gray loops still visible through the open chamber doors. “Did any measured vector intersect a station before your firing order?”

“No.”

“Then do not ask me to defend the word hostile as a substitute for a trajectory.”

Aven's expression hardened. “If you concede alien civilian status, the defense inquiry will reach your lock. You used the same mass-risk premise.”

“I used emergency infrastructure authority.”

“You trapped them.”

The minister left before Olan could answer. The distinction remained legally sound and morally narrow. Olan had locked Cantilevers 61 through 72 because an uncontrolled entry threatened the resonance lattice. The lock had compressed first-arcs behind adult bodies. After the volley, no doctrine could pretend those effects belonged to separate decisions.

The session bell sounded.

Laleh rose first. “We request immediate release of the phase-wake annex, suspension of centralized phase control, and local vetoes over any Cantilever action that changes a jurisdiction's power, tides, atmosphere capacity, or evacuation path.”

Her proposal appeared beside the fourteen maps. Olan read the operative verbs. Release. Suspend. Veto. Each removed an instrument his office relied upon during cascading failures.

The Meridian delegate challenged her. “Can Serein guarantee its veto will not destabilize another region?”

“No,” Laleh said. “Neither can Director Brace guarantee his order will not export our losses. The difference concerns who can refuse.”

Neme's tide engineer entered a correction from her station. “Refusal at Cantilever 64 changes our moon's load. Serein cannot hold a veto without naming Neme as affected.”

“Agreed,” Laleh said. “Consequence defines participation.”

That answer unsettled both supporters and opponents. A territorial veto came with clean boundaries. A moving consequence could collect claimants as an orbit changed. Olan saw adjudication multiplying faster than light-delay procedure could hold it.

He activated the central allocation display. Power dependence lines crossed the fourteen maps. “If every affected region must authorize every correction, no urgent correction can occur. The Tern Cascade killed people while independent operators waited for one another to accept liability.”

A remote delegate from Rook answered eight minutes after his statement began. “Tern also killed people because the central forecast marked contract habitats as recoverable margins.”

Olan knew the report. He had helped write its reforms after Pell died. Delayed local action had allowed a resonance fault to propagate through stations that assumed someone else held command. Yet the final casualty map did show contract habitats in a lighter category. Pell's rescue tender had crossed from a protected lane into that margin and never returned.

“The Cascade combined unowned decisions with unequal protection,” Olan said. “I will not repeat either.”

“Your lock already did,” Laleh replied.

The chamber wall changed as Serein overlaid the first-arc compression field. Halcyon added its true reserve floor. Vela drew stranded transit workers around canceled phase gates. Neme placed its tide loads above all three. The maps did not merge. Their contradictions remained visible.

Olan asked the clerks for the pending lock forecast. The central model projected stable power if all twelve outer Cantilevers retained phase. It also projected increasing pressure on the migration. The Orison response sat outside the confidence band because no Authority standard recognized a living body as a correction partner.

Ansa Meren appeared by secure link from Aphelion. Her background held no polished briefing wall, only Cartography Bay Four and an equipment rack with one panel removed.

“State what can be measured,” Olan said.

“Continued lock increases shear along the first-arc shelter within every modeled field state,” she said. “Full abrupt decoupling exceeds safe power loss in at least six jurisdictions. Neme blocks the low-shear corridor. Moving it safely requires staged authority and local load limits.”

The Meridian delegate asked, “Do you have a complete plan?”

“No.”

A murmur moved through the chamber.

Ansa continued before anyone could use the answer as closure. “I have a design method that retains branches the Authority optimizer discards. It cannot produce one certified solution until jurisdictions supply actual reserve floors and refusal conditions.”

Olan felt every eye turn toward him. “Can such a method meet the Pale Interval deadline?”

“Only if model control starts distributing now.”

“Will distributed edits remain auditable?”

“Not under current provenance rules.”

Laleh said, “Then change the rules.”

The chamber broke into procedural challenges. Three delegates requested the model. Two refused to expose local reserve data without liability protection. Neme demanded tide authority before sharing foundation tolerances. Meridia objected that infrastructure owned by the Cantilever Authority could not submit to territorial refusal. The remote representative still heard an earlier question and began answering it over the argument.

Olan watched fragmentation happen at conversational speed. None of the demands lacked reason. Together they could consume the remaining days.

He called the chamber to order and requested one map at a time.

Serein went first. Its reserve envelope showed outer shelters that the Authority model aggregated into licensed population centers. Some tunnels from Ansa's childhood eclipse drill still lacked full designation. A phase correction safe for the moon's average load stranded those spaces beyond heated transit.

Halcyon's presentation followed. Edrin Meren had signed the school figures along with water engineers and district controllers. Twelve days of safe local power under full decoupling appeared not as a promise but as a conditional band. The central database still claimed nineteen.

Neme displayed a routine correction from the previous year. Authority control had lowered shipping risk across Pelagos while raising foundation loads in one tide city for six weeks. No structure failed, so the increase disappeared into acceptable system variance. Local residents had paid through closed elevators, temporary relocations, and repair shifts never charged to the correction.

Vela showed Min Calder's canceled scholarship transport among thousands of disrupted journeys. The individual loss looked small beside planetary power. Multiplied across apprenticeships, medical rotations, and family contracts, the lock altered who could build a future outside their present jurisdiction.

By the seventh map, Olan stopped looking for a common visual grammar. The incompatibility supplied evidence. Every region measured survival through systems it maintained and people it could see. Central control translated those measures into one scale, then treated the translation loss as uncertainty.

The eighth map arrived from the delayed outer delegate. She had heard his Tern argument and prepared a reply before receiving later discussion. Her jurisdiction operated three old ballast stations with no local thrust capacity. If phase loosened without a replacement tug, the stations drifted. If the lock continued, correction heat exceeded one bearing's inspection limit. Her people occupied danger on both branches.

“What do you request?” Olan asked.

“A choice between named risks,” she said from eleven minutes ago. “Not a safe category assigned by someone who does not have to repair either outcome.”

Olan ordered the clerks to test Laleh's veto language against three live corrections rather than continue arguing nouns. The first involved Cantilever 62, where lock heat had reached an inspection threshold. Reducing phase there protected the station but cut transfer power to Rook's ballast habitats. Under the proposed rule, Rook could refuse. Its refusal pushed heat toward Cantilever 64 and gave Neme a claim. Neme's refusal then returned the load to 62.

“There,” the Meridian delegate said. “A circular prohibition.”

Laleh approached the central display. “Only if your objective remains preserving the original phase at someone else's cost. Add a temporary fuel tow to Rook.”

“No tow can arrive inside the correction window,” Olan said.

“Then identify who accepts the bearing risk at 62.”

The station supervisor appeared by audio, breathing hard from a machinery space. “My crew can hold through one inspection cycle if the Authority guarantees evacuation priority before the next rise.”

Rook's delegate asked, “Priority through which transfer lane?”

Olan traced the available path. Granting evacuation priority displaced medical cargo bound for Vela. Vela's delegate refused until a second shipment could be moved ahead of commercial ballast. The exchange reached four jurisdictions before producing a sequence all affected parties would accept.

It took nineteen minutes. During that interval, the bearing temperature at Cantilever 62 climbed another measurable fraction, making delay itself one of the negotiated loads rather than empty procedural time. Central control could have issued the same physical commands in forty seconds. Yet the Authority model had not included the supervisor's willingness to hold one cycle, because personnel courage could not enter a certified capacity. Nor had it exposed which Vela cargo a Meridian priority displaced. The negotiated route carried less elegance and more information.

The second test concerned a routine orbit trim for an uninhabited ballast rock. Meridia considered it urgent because delay would raise shipping insurance. Serein's shelter routes crossed the future correction cone. Its delegate vetoed the trim until the cone moved. No life-support threshold had been crossed, and the Meridian bench accused her of using safety authority for economic leverage.

Serein replied with maintenance rosters from the shelters. A future closure would force night crews onto surface transit during an eclipse interval. The central map counted the route as available because licensed vehicles could complete it. It did not count who had access to those vehicles.

Olan narrowed the veto language. “Declared floors require a measured infrastructure or evacuation consequence. They cannot rest on price alone.”

“Price decides whether our people reach the vehicle,” Laleh said.

“Then submit access as part of evacuation capacity.”

That revision entered the amendment. It turned cost into a measurable constraint without granting every contract dispute power over orbital control. Olan recognized the compromise as good procedure. He also recognized that no central draft would have discovered it without Serein's obstruction.

The third test failed because the delayed outer delegate could not respond inside the modeled burn window. Meridian counsel proposed constructive consent after a missed deadline. Outer representatives rejected that language at once. A jurisdiction could lose its authority through distance precisely when distance made its participation most necessary.

Ansa spoke from Aphelion. “The burn can split into a reversible preparation and an irreversible transfer. Begin preparation while the answer travels. Do not cross the transfer point without receipt.”

Defense objected that an adversary could exploit the delay. Laleh asked which measured action justified calling the absent delegate an adversary. No one answered.

Olan ordered the two-stage requirement added. The amendment grew another page. Each safeguard consumed time, computation, and reserve capacity. It also removed a hidden assumption from the current system: that speed belonged to whoever already held the key.

The remaining maps changed no central fact. They changed who stood inside it.

During recess, Olan entered the director's chamber beneath the Assembly floor. Pell's name remained on an old emergency contact in the wall console. Olan had never removed it. The system no longer attempted delivery, but it still displayed the address whenever he opened a crisis file.

He pulled up the full phase-wake annex.

His office counsel joined by audio. “You can release the technical measurements while withholding deliberative records and institutional liability sections pending review.”

“That repeats the first summary.”

“With a broader evidence set.”

“It repeats the governing choice.”

Counsel paused. “Full release exposes the Authority to claims from fourteen jurisdictions. It may also support an alien-harm claim if the Assembly recognizes standing.”

“Can any withheld section change the wake's physical history?”

“No.”

“Then liability no longer justifies information asymmetry.”

“You will lose control of the response narrative.”

Olan looked at Pell's dead address. Control of the Tern response had arrived too late for him. Afterward, Olan had built procedures so no official could evade responsibility by claiming incomplete authority. He had not intended those procedures to erase the people who carried a central decision's margins. Intent offered no maintenance access to a damaged system.

He signed full release.

The annex crossed into the public docket in eight secured volumes. Source measurements, dissenting ecology reports, suppressed mission requests, and every liability review went with it. Olan added his office's renewal seals. He did not add an apology. An apology attached to continued control would function as another claim on interpretation.

When the chamber reconvened, the maps displayed the release receipt.

Laleh read it, then looked directly at him. “Will you lift the phase lock?”

“Not before a controlled transfer can prevent abrupt decoupling.”

“You have released proof of harm while preserving the instrument causing it.”

“I have preserved power and orbital stability for the duration of this vote.”

The difference satisfied no one. Olan had not expected it to.

Clerks distributed the renewal measure. It continued the lock for seventy-two hours while ordering public development of Ansa's staged plan. Laleh introduced an amendment granting bounded local vetoes over any stage that crossed declared reserve, tide, atmosphere, or evacuation floors. Meridia objected to the word veto. Neme objected to local because its tides crossed jurisdictions. Serein proposed affected authority. Procedure consumed forty minutes and produced language no one trusted fully.

Olan accepted the amendment. Without it, Laleh's coalition would reject renewal outright. With it, central command would lose the ability to certify a single executable map. He could feel the office changing shape around a phrase.

Voting opened by certified link.

The first three jurisdictions supported renewal. Serein opposed. Halcyon opposed after attaching its reserve conditions. Neme supported only after entering a formal protest that the lock itself increased tide risk. Vela rejected continued central control. Rook's vote arrived late and turned the count even.

Seven supported the amended renewal. Seven opposed.

The clerk read the result in the required form. “The Assembly divides seven-seven. No majority order issues.”

Voices rose across the floor. Laleh demanded immediate expiration. Defense counsel argued that failure to renew did not constitute an affirmative release command. Outer delegates called that interpretation rule by inaction.

Olan requested the emergency-continuity clause. The clerk displayed it with a warning that no prior case involved an external civic intelligence trapped by infrastructure.

“Existing emergency configuration continues pending adjudication when an evenly divided Assembly cannot issue a successor order,” Olan read. “The lock remains at present phase. No expansion of scope is authorized.”

Laleh stood motionless at her station. “You call deadlock permission.”

“I call it the procedure that prevents twelve Cantilevers from receiving contradictory commands.”

“Everyone outside your map will hear refusal.”

A priority alarm interrupted the chamber.

Aphelion tracking replaced the voting display. Along Asteron's magnetotail, one Orison adult had separated from the formation. Witness swarms arranged pressure marks associated with Tull-of-Narrow-Mercy in the shared-clock record. Ansa's team appended a cautious interpretation: the voting interval had closed without human release; the Narrow Mercy pattern now treated delay as a denied passage.

The adult body began moving conducting mass toward Neme.

Olan watched the trajectory lengthen across all fourteen maps. This time, every jurisdiction drew the same approaching line.

Chapter 15 — The Shove at Neme

Tull moved before the human vote's last heat left the shared clock.

Pressure gathered along Neme's sunward field, carried by mineral swarms that had once sheltered first-arcs. Kehr felt the change through five adult bodies at unequal intervals. Ember-Side received it as a hardening against the sail. Little Weight tasted iron lifted from the moon's exosphere. Bent Morning saw Tull's witness lines close around a future orbit. Silt Crown lagged behind the consensus, protecting juveniles deeper in Asteron's magnetotail. Harrow of Rain remembered another corridor taken before refusal could become pursuit.

The human signal still held seven permissions and seven denials. Its grammar remained incomplete, but the fixed interval had ended without release. To Tull-of-Narrow-Mercy, division had supplied an answer.

Tull's pressure declared passage through action. Mass flowed into a correction arc around Neme.

Kehr spread warning across the migration. Do not complete. The settled bodies remain divided. A divided refusal can still change.

Narrow Mercy answered by shortening the interval. Change without force wastes first-arcs. The Pale Interval comes. Vaul has already taken the cost of patience.

The name of Vaul pulled at the partition inside Kehr's twelfth return. A blank correction opened where memory should have carried consequence. Harrow touched the edge of it, then withdrew to the present danger.

Kehr turned Ember-Side and Bent Morning across Tull's pressure path.

No words accompanied the movement. Ember-Side folded its conductive veil against the incoming mass stream. Bent Morning rolled a mineral core into the projected correction plane. Their combined pressure did not strike Tull's hosts. It changed the cost of continuing. Any shove strong enough to pass them would shear witness bodies and expose first-arcs clustered behind Silt Crown.

Tull did not stop.

Narrow Mercy separated its own moving mass into three currents. The first pressed high above Neme's magnetic equator. The second dipped beneath the moon's orbital plane. A third dispersed through witness swarms, too fine for one counterpressure to catch. Kehr had learned such geometry during migrations where stars supplied no stable shelter. It allowed a civic mind to create one large correction from many actions that each appeared survivable.

Little Weight urged pursuit of the fine current. Ember-Side held the upper pressure. Bent Morning demanded more mass. Silt Crown would not uncover the juveniles. Harrow carried memory of narrow routes becoming graves when adults fought inside them.

We cannot hold every current, Kehr admitted.

Then choose the first-arcs, Silt Crown answered.

The human moon moved.

At first the change occupied only an instrument's tolerance. Neme advanced along its orbit by less than the length of a tide-city foundation. Its ocean answered immediately. Water already raised by Asteron pulled against a new timing. Elevator counterweights began crossing their guide channels early. Human correction beacons around the moon flashed in sudden disagreement.

The shared clock opened a rapid series of positions. Human observers predicted Neme before the shove, Neme after, and inhabited structures outside tolerance. Their signal carried no negotiated term for accusation. It did not need one.

Kehr pushed Ember-Side harder against Tull.

Conductive tissue heated across the adult veil. Symbiotic swarms retreated from the pressure edge, carrying burned fragments toward cooler field lines. Bent Morning received the same load at a different phase and turned it sideways. For three intervals, Tull's high current slowed.

The fine current reached Neme's far side.

Its distributed mass coupled with the moon's magnetic environment and added a second pulse. Ocean bulges separated from their expected longitudes. Along one tide city, foundation alarms appeared as ordered lights in the human telemetry. Elevator cars halted between moon and orbital hubs. Their cables continued moving after the brakes engaged, long structures unable to accept a changed orbit as quickly as software did.

Tull marked the opening corridor behind Neme. It remained too narrow for the widest adults but could pass damaged first-arcs if the shove continued.

Narrow Mercy sent a sheltering instruction toward the juveniles under its care. Some first-arcs followed. Others stayed within Kehr's pressure relationships. Personhood among the young had not yet settled into the civic forms adults used. Their choices came as adhesion, resistance, and which remembered path they repeated when fear compressed the field.

Kehr felt several lean toward Tull.

That altered more than position. Narrow Mercy had broken migration consensus, yet first-arcs now treated its correction as a possible route to survival. If Kehr stopped the shove by force, the act could look like loyalty to settled humans over developing kin.

Harrow of Rain moved closer to Ember-Side, sharing heat across the oldest body's wide magnetic arches. Its remembered corrections entered Kehr as pressure histories rather than counsel: a gas giant crossed without disturbing a feeding ecology; a moon abandoned when local minds used it to bait predators; an earlier contact where Orison silence preserved the migration and left another living system to meet harm alone.

The current could still be broken. Kehr would need to release Silt Crown from shelter, expose its first-arcs, and strike the fine mass stream before it completed another loop. Tull would lose the shove. The juveniles might lose the protection they needed during the fight.

Human drives lit around Neme.

Defense craft rose from equatorial stations and orbital docks. Their firing lines appeared on Kehr's bodies as hard, narrow predictions. Unlike the volley in Asteron's tail, these weapons defended an inhabited moon already moving outside its certified path. The distinction mattered. It did not make their aim accurate.

Kehr sent the shared-clock pattern for harm and passage. Tull overlaid a different signal: motion, opening, survival. Human targeting systems received both as magnetic accumulation.

The first projectile entered Bent Morning's outer veil.

Kehr rolled the body away, but the shot had not targeted Bent Morning. It crossed toward Tull's fine current and tore through witness swarms carrying correction mass. Their loops failed too quickly to return measurements. Several had hosted pieces of Narrow Mercy's present attention. Others belonged to no adult polity and had followed the densest path because it promised shelter.

Orison died in the crossing.

Their released charge coupled with Neme's field. Instead of reducing Tull's pressure, the impact added an irregular pulse to the moon. Neme's orbital oscillation widened.

Human alarms multiplied. A tide-city seawall reported a load reversal. Two elevator cables entered conflicting resonances. One defense platform lost alignment and fired its thrusters across a civilian transfer lane to avoid collision.

Tull used the new oscillation.

Narrow Mercy pushed at the high point, turning damage into added motion. The corridor widened another fraction. Its declaration reached Kehr through every stressed body: settled fire makes the next necessity. Do not spend kin to protect those who shoot them.

Little Weight agreed.

The agreement cut through Kehr as a pressure withdrawal. One of five bodies stopped supporting the countercurrent and angled toward Tull's line. Not defection, not yet. Little Weight refused to carry load for humans while their weapons killed Orison.

We are not carrying it for humans, Ember-Side answered. Neme's motion will kill first-arcs when the rebound crosses the shelter.

Tull had not modeled the rebound through the full magnetotail. Kehr had, using the human moon data and Harrow's older memory of coupled tides. If Neme oscillated beyond one narrow range, Asteron's field would return pressure through the corridor after the first opening. First-arcs entering early could be caught between the moon's wake and the locked Cantilevers.

Kehr transmitted the predicted rebound through the shared clock. Before. Shove. Opening. Passage. Closure. Harm.

Narrow Mercy rejected the final relation. Its swarms altered the predicted closure, claiming enough mass could hold the path.

Kehr tested the claim across five bodies. Enough mass existed only if adults stripped shielding from the first-arcs and accepted direct exposure to the coming Pale Interval. Tull's correction preserved the nearest young by spending those farther back.

That choice followed Narrow Mercy doctrine. It did not arise from error.

A second human volley armed.

Bent Morning wanted to pursue the defense craft and end their firing. Ember-Side still opposed Tull. Little Weight held apart. Silt Crown protected the nursery train. Harrow carried unbearable memory near the blank twelfth return.

Kehr's consensus broke.

We cannot answer both harms, the bodies told one another.

The shared clock showed Neme's oscillation. One line climbed toward elevator failure and tide-city foundation limits. Another showed Tull's mass retreating beyond pursuit if Kehr turned away. The correction window narrowed with every orbit fraction.

Kehr chose the moon.

Choice among distributed bodies required more than intention. Kehr had to move load away from every path that could become pursuit and place it where Neme could answer. Ember-Side offered its unburned outer sail. Bent Morning released mineral ballast held since the corridor before Vaul. Harrow opened phase memory from the ninth return. Silt Crown refused to leave the nursery train, and Little Weight withheld agreement.

For one interval, Kehr contained only three-body consensus. The civic name did not vanish, but it lost reach. Decisions sent through Ember-Side arrived at Little Weight as proposals rather than self-command.

Tull felt the fracture and pressed into it.

Narrow Mercy sent a witnessed correction from the first nursery loss: young bodies trapped behind adults who waited for a safer route until no route remained. The memory carried true measures. Tull did not falsify the dead. Its argument placed those dead beside Neme's inhabitants and demanded that living first-arcs take priority now.

Little Weight turned farther away.

Harrow answered with no general principle. It shared the rebound path through Little Weight's own conductive tissues, forcing the body to feel where exposed juveniles would meet returning pressure if Neme continued oscillating. The predicted closure crossed the first-arc cluster Tull had begun moving toward the gap.

This moon carries both harms, Harrow signaled.

Narrow Mercy replied that every corridor carried both. Survival belonged to whoever stopped waiting for innocence.

The missing twelfth return opened again inside Kehr. A pressure relation had been severed from its consequence. Kehr sensed the shape of a prior decision without its bodies: departure chosen, damaged young omitted, silence maintained. Vaul pressed against the blank from both sides.

Kehr could not use a memory it had partitioned. It could use the present trajectories.

The nearest first-arcs received competing predictions. Tull showed passage through the widening gap. Kehr showed closure after passage. Some juveniles could test neither at adult scale, but they compared smaller intervals through their own developing loops. One cluster copied Tull's opening. Another copied Kehr's rebound. A third changed both patterns, adding the motion of human elevator cables as if inhabited machinery also belonged to the path.

That third response reached Little Weight.

The body had carried those first-arcs since a dim star before Pelagos. Their developing choices had often arrived as imperfect repetitions of adult pressure. Now they produced a correction neither polity owned. Little Weight rotated back toward Kehr by a narrow degree, enough to share observation but not enough to accept load.

Kehr sent a promise bounded to the measurable event. Hold Neme from the rebound. Preserve the first-arc shelter. Do not pursue Tull during the hold.

Silt Crown accepted because the promise named juveniles. Ember-Side accepted through offered heat. Bent Morning accepted after narrowing the pursuit clause to the duration of direct correction. Harrow had already begun transferring the ninth-return phase. Little Weight remained separate.

The human beacons added consequence marks while Orison bodies negotiated. One line traced a tide city's seawall, whose gates now closed against water arriving at the wrong local hour. Another represented elevator cars suspended with people inside them. A third described orbital debris drifting toward the nursery shelter if platform thrusters continued emergency burns.

Kehr could not map each mark to personhood. The shared clock had taught that predicted harm changed action. Human controllers had also placed their own tugs between weapons and Orison bodies. Enough reciprocal measure existed for a temporary correction, not trust beyond it.

Tull broadcast Kehr's promise to the migration with a changed emphasis. Kehr abandons passage to maintain settled walls. The statement preserved the action and removed the rebound. Long Recall bodies accepted the omission because it supported retreat from human contact. Narrow Mercy swarms repeated it as proof that Kehr's judgment had become externally captured.

Near Turning witnesses requested the full prediction. Kehr released it without demanding support. They carried the rebound model across the migration, but several withheld their bodies from the coming load. Understanding did not require consent.

Political authority drained from Kehr before any position changed. Shared correction reserves closed. Memory witnesses chose other hosts. Juvenile escorts that once responded to Kehr's timing began waiting for narrower local signals. The migration still recognized Kehr-of-Twelve-Returns, but no longer treated the name as a sufficient reason to move.

The cost entered the five bodies as missing help. Ember-Side would carry more heat. Bent Morning would spend mineral mass that could not be recovered before the Pale Interval. Harrow would hold phase without support from adjacent adults. Silt Crown would protect first-arcs through the rebound with fewer witness swarms.

Only after those losses became measurable did Little Weight offer a limited current. It would damp Neme, protect the juvenile line, then withdraw. It would not oppose Tull again without a new consensus.

Kehr accepted the condition. A plural mind survived by remembering which agreement had limits.

Ember-Side released pressure against Tull and descended toward Neme's far field. Bent Morning followed after one resistant interval. Little Weight joined only when Harrow transferred a remembered trajectory for damping a moon without cracking its outer magnetic arches. Silt Crown remained with the first-arcs, shaping shelter against the predicted rebound.

Tull's hosts accelerated away from the counterpressure. Narrow Mercy had won the immediate political contest. Its passage faction streamed behind it, carrying surviving witness swarms toward the corridor edge.

Kehr did not pursue.

Human instruments noticed the changed geometry. One defense battery held fire. Another retained its lock on Bent Morning. The shared clock emitted a human prediction that placed Kehr's two descending bodies opposite Neme's next oscillation peak.

Agreement did not exist. A usable coincidence did.

Kehr matched the predicted interval.

Ember-Side opened its veil across the moon's magnetic wake. Bent Morning placed its mineral core along the lateral correction plane. Harrow carried phase between them, letting two bodies act as parts of one damping loop. The first pressure application slowed Neme's forward error but drove tides higher on the moon's inner face.

Human controllers changed their thruster pattern. Seven orbital tugs burned away from the original defense formation and toward tide protection. Their mass produced little direct correction. Their timing marked the local limits Kehr could not measure from magnetic pressure alone.

A Neme beacon sent three repeating thresholds. Elevator cable resonance. Foundation reversal. Civilian transfer clearance. Kehr accepted them as veto boundaries inside the damping action.

The next Orison pressure pulse stayed below the first threshold. Neme continued oscillating.

Bent Morning asked for more load. The beacon denied it by repeating the cable relation with shorter urgency. Kehr shifted the pulse sideways instead, trading faster orbital correction for lower structural stress. That choice allowed Tull to move farther beyond reach.

Narrow Mercy broadcast the loss of pursuit through the migration. Kehr protects fixed walls while first-arcs wait. Kehr accepts settled veto during Orison death. Kehr no longer speaks from migration consensus.

Witness swarms changed position around the five bodies. Those aligned with Narrow Mercy widened their intervals from Kehr. Long Recall bodies withdrew as well, judging open conflict proof that contact had already corrupted the route. Near Turning swarms stayed close but carried no authority to restore consensus.

Political standing had material distance. Kehr felt it as cooling pressure where other bodies ceased sharing load.

Another defense platform fired.

The projectile targeted witness mass falling toward Neme. A human tug crossed its line at the last possible interval, not close enough to be struck but close enough to force the platform's safety system to break lock. The act repeated Remainder's earlier motion in smaller form. Some human bodies had learned the warning. Others still prepared weapons.

Kehr sent the tug a witnessed position through the clock. Before danger. Human crossing. Fire absent. Continued correction.

The tug answered by placing its next burn inside Kehr's tolerated error. No word for trust passed between them. Position carried enough.

Neme reached the far edge of its oscillation.

Ember-Side and Bent Morning pressed together. Heat tore strips from Ember-Side's biological sail. Bent Morning's mineral core rang through symbiotic structures, scattering memories into witness swarms for later return. Harrow held the phase relation. Little Weight accepted the lower current and added its pressure at last.

The moon slowed.

Its tides did not settle. Water kept moving after orbital error began to shrink. Elevators remained locked between surfaces and hubs. Foundations along the inner coast carried loads outside ordinary certification. Yet the widening stopped.

Human controllers marked a temporary hold.

Kehr reduced pressure before the damping action became another shove. The moon occupied a damaged orbit between its prior certified line and Tull's attempted corridor. Holding it there required continued small corrections from human tugs and two Kehr bodies. Any abrupt release would restore the oscillation.

Tull sent no assistance. Narrow Mercy had moved beyond immediate reach and now organized mass for passage around the changed moon. Its supporters repeated the broken vote interval as evidence that settled humans had chosen obstruction.

Kehr's bodies remained divided.

Little Weight wanted to leave Neme before defense weapons found a clean line. Bent Morning wanted pursuit while Tull still carried heat from the shove. Silt Crown reported first-arcs clustering along two incompatible shelter paths. Ember-Side could not sustain another full damping load without repair. Harrow compared the moon's residual motion with an older correction from the ninth return.

The shared clock gathered human predictions.

Neme local control supplied tide thresholds. Orbital crews added elevator and foundation tolerances. A distant cartographic source contributed a longer sequence, careful to preserve all local boundaries instead of averaging them. The combined message projected the residual oscillation forward.

Kehr tested it against bodily pressure. Every branch that avoided immediate structural failure still accumulated correction debt. Human tugs lacked enough balanced fuel to finish now. Kehr's heated sails needed recovery before another safe load. Tull's first shove had changed the orbit too far for passive damping.

One later interval remained wide enough.

The human clock named it with a system marker Kehr had learned through witnessed repetitions: SD57.

Kehr placed a pressure acknowledgment around that future point. Human beacons answered from Neme. The moon remained temporarily held between their corrections, its oceans still moving under the altered pull.

At SD57, they would have to move it again.

Chapter 16 — Consent Cards

The docking claw struck Remainder twice before it found metal strong enough to hold.

Ivo felt both impacts through his boots. Neme Anchor Station had lost alignment during the shove, and its docking collar now described a slow oval around the tug's approach axis. Sana matched the motion with attitude jets fed from the intact starboard spine. The third contact brought a deep clang through the hull. Capture bolts drove into Remainder's reinforced belly.

“Hard dock,” Sana said. “If anyone asks, we meant to arrive sideways.”

“No one who saw that approach will ask.” Ivo checked the port feed isolation. Spine Three remained empty between the emergency valves, its fracture wrapped in a containment sleeve that could restrain vapor but not carry thrust. “Load at the collar?”

“Within station limits. Our limit objects to the word within.”

A structural gauge flickered amber near the aft attachment. Ivo sent a hull team to inspect it before extending the passage tube.

Below them, Neme turned under broken timing. The moon's oceans had not settled into their former tide cycle. White surge lines pressed against the three tide cities at hours when their seawalls expected withdrawal. Orbital elevators hung under reduced loads, some stopped with cars parked between surface and counterweight. Correction tugs held the temporary orbit in small alternating burns. Every plume spent fuel needed for SD57.

The station opened its inner hatch. A Neme dockworker in a pressure hood stood behind it, one hand on a manual equalization wheel.

“Your automatic handshake keeps rejecting our collar,” she said over the local channel.

“Our handshake has standards.”

“Your ship hit us twice.”

“Standards can mourn privately.”

She turned the wheel. Pressure entered the passage tube through valves loud enough to carry across both hulls.

Ivo met the Neme anchor crew at the far hatch. Their work suits carried tide-city patches rather than Authority blue. Salt corrosion marked tools brought up from surface foundations. The dockworker introduced herself as Chief Lio Dey and handed him a physical torque key.

“Station command wants your signature before we unlock the anchor lockers.”

“What am I signing?”

“That you accept local calibration.”

He read the plate attached to the key. Neme load limits appeared beside Authority certification values. They disagreed in four places.

“Local figures run lower,” he said.

“Authority figures average three tide cycles. We have not had three comparable cycles since the shove.”

Ivo signed for receipt, not acceptance. “Show me the anchors.”

Dey led him along a spoke that flexed with each station correction. The lockers held twelve physical anchor packages, each built around a dense ballast core, magnetic clamp, and burn collar. During the second correction, tugs would place them along Neme's orbital field. Human drives could then pull against known masses while Orison pressure supplied the broad force no tug could produce.

A clean diagram made the work look like distributing markers.

The first package had a cracked clamp housing. The fourth carried a coolant pump designed for a different propellant. Two burn collars had been certified from remote inspection because no tug had visited Neme during the phase lock. Ivo opened every panel.

Dey watched him reject the second remote certificate. “We told Meridia the stores needed physical inspection.”

“They told you the models included degradation.”

“They told us the confidence band covered it.”

“Confidence never turned a bolt.”

Her crew began unloading test gear from Remainder. Neme riggers worked in pairs, one from orbit and one from a tide city, because anchor timing linked foundation load to field pressure. Authority procedure assigned the local crews an advisory role. In the locker, advisory hands already knew which pump seals had hardened and which clamp had been repaired after a salt-water fabrication delay.

Ivo connected his torque key to the first anchor. “Who commands deployment?”

“Your tug places packages one through six. Two Neme tugs place the rest. Authority Correction Control issues burn marks.”

“Who can halt for tide load?”

“Local station can request.”

“Request whom?”

“Meridia.”

Light delay to Meridia remained short enough for conversation, long enough for a cable to cross resonance while an official decided whose number counted.

Dey pulled an inspection cover free. “We requested a hard local stop.”

“What did they give you?”

“A priority channel.”

Ivo had heard enough priority channels go silent under competing alarms. “Put your stop circuit beside ours.”

“Authority seal rejects local command keys.”

“Then we have found the first repair.”

They spent four hours testing anchors. Package Three could not hold rated magnetic load until Dey's surface rigger replaced a sensor bracket with one fabricated for seawall gates. Package Seven passed every electronic diagnostic and failed when Ivo asked a deckhand to strike the clamp with a mallet. A hidden fracture opened along the casting. The crew tagged it for replacement.

During the eighth test, Sana sent him a private packet with no message. It contained the Authority operation plan.

Ivo opened it against the anchor casing.

The burn sequence ran from SD57 minus six hours through the final damping window. Remainder would place six packages, lock its own field collar to the correction plane, and provide balancing thrust while Kehr's bodies pressed Neme. The plan reserved starboard fuel through the first two stages. After that, it assumed full depletion.

A failure tree marked likely consequences. If the Orison pressure arrived late, Remainder would hold anchor position until hull load exceeded certification. If one Neme tug lost thrust, Ivo's ship would absorb its vector. If the port containment sleeve ruptured, the sequence continued without a retreat burn.

Crew evacuation appeared only after final release.

The probability column for usable hull at that point had been shaded red.

Dey leaned over the casing but did not look at his display. “Bad news?”

“Expensive assumptions.”

“Whose?”

“Ours, apparently.”

He closed the plan and asked Sana to meet him aboard.

Remainder's mess occupied a widened junction near the crew berths. Thirty-two people could not sit there at once, so the consent review used three connected compartments with the doors latched open. Crew members strapped themselves to benches, handrails, and folded acceleration couches. The ship remained under light station gravity, enough to make loose cups slide whenever Neme tugs corrected.

Ivo placed the operation plan on every wall display.

“The Authority expects this ship to remain in the correction plane after we lose balanced thrust,” he said. “Their primary branch ends with likely hull loss. Evacuation starts afterward.”

No one spoke for several seconds. Engineers respected complete failure diagrams even when they disliked who occupied the boxes.

Sana stood near the hatch with the original consent cards in a physical stack. She had printed them before leaving Vela because no network failure could edit paper in a drawer.

Ivo continued. “The ship can perform the anchor role with a reduced crew. I will keep propulsion, field control, and two hull stations aboard. Everyone else transfers to Neme Anchor before the first burn.”

Marr raised her hand from the aft compartment. “How many does reduced mean?”

“Six.”

“Can six release all physical locks if the field collar jams?”

“No. We would abandon the locked sections.”

Teren asked, “Who handles casualty isolation?”

“The station.”

“Through a docking collar under correction load?”

“If the collar holds.”

Bess folded her arms. “You have described a six-person death branch and called it reduction.”

“It removes twenty-six people from exposure.”

“It removes the people needed to keep the ship alive.”

Ivo pointed at the plan. “The Authority already assumes the hull goes.”

“The Authority does not maintain this hull,” she said.

A few crew members murmured agreement. Others studied the red branches.

Ivo had expected resistance from those asked to stay. He had not expected anger from those he ordered away. “No one gains professional virtue by occupying a failing ship.”

Kam Oss answered from propulsion. “No. But you cannot run two manual valve stations by yourself.”

“The port spine stays isolated.”

“Until anchor load twists the sleeve. Then someone closes aft while someone checks forward. You know this.”

“I know I am not spending thirty-two lives to make one ship harder to break.”

Sana lifted the paper stack. “You are not entitled to spend six either.”

He looked at her. “I volunteered.”

“You volunteered everyone assigned to the jobs you cannot perform alone.”

“I can ask for five volunteers.”

“Under what conditions?”

“The conditions in the cards.”

Sana placed the stack on the table. “Those cards cover crossing the exclusion boundary with a functioning two-spine retreat path. They cover witnessed contact burns and rescue intervention under crew review. We now have one full spine, a damaged hull collar, an altered moon, and an Authority plan that schedules evacuation after probable failure. Conditions changed. Consent expires with them.”

The paper edges shifted as the ship made another small station correction.

Ivo knew she had procedural right. He also knew procedures could become a way to move burden from a captain onto people with less information. “A vote does not equal free choice when every person here knows someone on Neme.”

“Then do not ask one question,” Sana said. “Ask what each person requires to make staying acceptable, and record who has power to meet it.”

Marr spoke first. “My son lives on Rook. If I die outside authorized terms, his other parent loses the dependent maintenance stipend. I will stay if this operation carries rescue status from every jurisdiction using our anchors, not a discretionary defense code.”

Teren shook his head. “Status does not reserve a ship to pick us up. My sister already carries my training debt. I want fuel physically set aside where Correction Control cannot burn it to improve the moon solution.”

A navigation tech named Esi raised two fingers. “My parents live in South Tide. Their building takes the inner surge. I am staying whether this room approves me or not, but I will not follow a Meridian burn mark that crosses local seawall limits.”

“Professional duty still matters,” said Kam. “We know this tug. Neme crews do not. Leaving creates its own risk for people below.”

Dey had joined by local channel from the anchor locker. Her face appeared on the end display. “Neme crews know the moon. Your plan lists us as deployment support. That means we provide hands while Meridia and your bridge retain command.”

Bess looked at Ivo. “There is the second condition.”

He understood before she said it. “Shared command.”

“Not consultation,” Dey said. “Our tide controller holds stop authority on foundation and elevator loads. My station holds stop authority on anchor hardware. Your bridge controls tug burns. No one combines those into one override.”

Authority procedure would reject the arrangement. The plan depended on synchronized action, and synchronized action preferred a single mark. Ivo had also watched a defense directive rename sheltering as hostility. Unity inside the wrong model only made error arrive everywhere at once.

“What about Orison pressure?” he asked.

“The shared clock carries their acceptance,” Dey said. “If the pressure falls outside the witnessed tolerance, every human burn holds.”

Sana began writing conditions on a blank display. “Local Neme crews share command. Rescue status certified across participating jurisdictions. Rescue fuel placed outside correction allocation. Renewed consent after final anchor inspection. Any full-path failure invalidates the burn authorization.”

“Full-path failure needs definition,” Ivo said.

“Loss of every escape route for crew.”

“That can happen midway through a burn when stopping creates greater hull load.”

“Then someone must have authority to stop before the final route closes.”

The room turned toward him.

Ivo saw the shape of their demand. It did not ask him to protect everyone by choosing correctly. It asked him to surrender the right to keep redefining escape as reachable whenever the mission needed one more burn.

He delayed answering and hated himself for recognizing the impulse.

“Review each card,” he said. “No collective show of hands.”

Sana distributed fresh forms.

The meeting lasted three hours. Some crew initially refused and moved to Anchor Station while the terms remained unsettled. Two changed their positions after Dey showed the tide-load stops. One required a guaranteed seat on the first rescue pod because she supported an elderly partner on Vela; the crew accepted the condition and placed her at a station near the pod collar. Another refused any branch requiring manual work outside the hull during Orison pressure. Ivo reassigned the task rather than bargaining the limit downward.

Marr requested direct confirmation from Rook that her son's stipend would continue under cross-jurisdiction rescue status. The reply arrived before the review ended. Teren demanded the debt clause in writing and received it from Neme's civil coordinator, who sounded offended that such a clause required negotiation during a system rescue. Teren signed only after the coordinator's seal validated.

All thirty-two crew members chose to stay for the correction under the renewed terms. Four would work from Neme Anchor during preparation and return aboard before the first burn. The arrangement left every tug station staffed for SD57 while placing ship knowledge inside local command during setup. No one accepted an obligation beyond the conditions on the signed card.

Next came the fuel.

Remainder carried enough in the starboard spine for anchor placement, balancing thrust, and one narrow retreat if every modeled burn stayed inside tolerance. Correction Control had assigned all of it to the moon sequence. Teren and Dey proposed moving a fixed reserve into two detachable bladders mounted near the service pod.

An Authority controller joined the channel. “Any segregated reserve reduces correction capacity below certified margin.”

“Your certified margin ends with our hull loss,” Ivo said.

“The operation protects 8.4 million people on Neme and stabilizes dependent orbits.”

“Then it can afford to identify who retrieves the thirty-two people performing it.”

“Fuel aboard an active correction vessel remains under mission allocation.”

Sana tapped the proposed condition. “No crew consent without rescue fuel.”

The controller tried three times to reclassify the reserve as contingency correction mass. Each version gave Meridia authority to spend it. Dey brought Neme's civil coordinator onto the call. Rook and Serein had already certified rescue status. Halcyon offered two fabrication tenders as remote retrieval capacity, though neither could arrive in the immediate window.

Under public witness, the controller yielded.

They transferred the fuel physically. Ivo supervised while station riggers locked the bladders to the service pod frame. Red seals marked them unavailable to main drive control. Opening either line required one key from Remainder and one from Neme Anchor. A quantity that had existed as a disputable column became metal no single command could reach.

Dey delivered the local stop circuit after shift change. Her crew had built it from tide-gate hardware because Authority keys still rejected Neme command access. Three guarded switches connected to separate systems: foundation load, elevator resonance, and anchor integrity. Any switch could hold the next burn mark. None could reverse a burn already underway.

Ivo installed the bridge receiver beside Sana's cutoff.

“What if local control panics?” he asked.

“What if you do?” she replied.

“I have training.”

“So do they.”

He tightened the receiver bracket until the torque key clicked. The practical answer sat in the wiring. Shared command created new failure modes. It also made local knowledge capable of stopping a remote plan before damage became a report.

They tested the circuit against a recorded correction mark. Dey's anchor switch held the simulated burn at the first gate. The bridge board showed a local stop, but main drive control continued counting toward ignition because Authority software treated the signal as advisory.

Sana canceled the test manually. “Your shared command currently sends a polite objection.”

Ivo opened the drive interlock. The program accepted hard stops only from propulsion safety, captain's control, or Meridia Correction Control. Adding three local sources required breaking a certification seal and changed the tug's liability class.

Dey watched from the end display. “I have a cutter.”

“So do we.” Ivo removed the seal with his torque key. “Yours probably has salt in it.”

“It still cuts.”

He routed the local receiver through propulsion safety rather than captain's control. Each Neme switch now interrupted ignition before fuel valves opened. Once a burn began, the switches shifted to a request channel because an abrupt cutoff could tear the anchor plane apart. Sana added an automatic hold before every irreversible stage, forcing the system to seek fresh clearance from all three local circuits.

The second test stopped cleanly. On the third, Ivo triggered a false elevator alarm while Dey authorized foundation and anchor loads. The sequence held. No Meridian command could clear it. Only the elevator controller could reset the stop after checking local measurements.

“Now fail our bridge,” Sana said.

Ivo disconnected the receiver. Loss of local signal produced another hold rather than constructive consent. That rule would cost time if a cable broke. It also prevented silence from impersonating permission.

They ran a final simulation with the service pod launching during a staged burn. The reserved bladders fed its small drive without opening any path into the main correction manifold. One bladder showed a pressure sag from a bad coupling. Dey's rigger replaced it with hardware from an anchor coolant line, then repeated the launch test until both reserves held.

By the time Ivo closed the interlock panel, the registry listed Neme's three stop sources as propulsion safety authorities aboard Remainder. Shared command had become part of the tug's machinery, not a meeting note someone could reinterpret under pressure.

Sana placed the renewed cards in the drawer beneath the propulsion rail. “One issue remains.”

“I know.”

“Say it as an authority transfer, not a promise to listen.”

Ivo opened the command registry. Captain's control covered propulsion and emergency rescue. First-officer safety authority could halt operations after a verified crew hazard, but the captain could override for immediate mission necessity. That final clause had followed him across every tug he served.

He removed his override from one defined condition.

The registry asked for scope. Ivo entered: Any burn that, by current measured fuel, hull, and docking state, threatens all crew escape paths.

It asked for the receiving officer.

“Sana Vi,” he said.

Her key lit beside his. “Accepted.”

He looked around the old bridge, at the starboard fuel trace, the dead port line, and the local stop receiver fixed beside Authority controls. None offered a clean route through SD57.

Ivo signed the transfer. From that moment, Sana held operational veto over any burn that could close every way home.

Chapter 17 — The Clock We Carry

The clock came through Neme's freight lock in Ren Adek's arms, pulsing hard enough to shake frost from its case.
