Ansa caught one handle while he found his footing on the dock grid. The case had left Aphelion as a rectangular instrument pack with two shield plates and a legal seal. It had returned from contact wrapped in copper mesh, tug insulation, three layers of witness-swarm filament, and a maintenance strap marked with somebody's dried blood. Blue light traveled through the alien fibers in irregular strokes.

“Don't level it,” Ren said.

She stopped adjusting her grip. “You told me the carrier had to remain level.”

“That was before it grew an opinion about down.”

A freight alarm sounded behind them. Neme Dock Three had lost its normal gravity reference during Tull's shove, and every incoming load required two certified vectors: where the deck expected mass to settle, and where the moon's oscillation would pull it six minutes later. Painted arrows on the floor had already been crossed out twice. Beyond the pressure wall, repair crews moved anchor cable through a corridor built for passengers.

Ren nodded toward a yellow cradle bolted beside the lock. “Set the Aphelion end there. Let the living end choose its tension.”

Together they lowered the case. The alien filaments tightened before the cradle touched metal. Their pull shifted from Ansa's left hand to her right with no visible movement, as if the clock had compared two possible falls and selected the less damaging one.

A local engineer named Mara Venn watched from behind the hazard line. Her tide-city patch carried a black diagonal for structural loss. “That thing did not clear biological inspection.”

“It cleared contact quarantine,” Ren said.

“Contact quarantine sits forty-one light-minutes from my elevator.”

Ansa opened the transfer record. “No matter crosses the inner case boundary. The filaments carry magnetic state only.”

Mara looked at the blue strokes running under Ren's palm. “State has broken more equipment here than matter.”

The objection had substance. Neme's damaged elevators still used the Cantilever lattice for lateral stabilization. A foreign magnetic pattern placed near their control bus could turn semantic curiosity into another foundation failure. Ansa authorized a passive screen and watched the local team wheel one around the cradle. The clock's pulse shortened as the mesh closed.

Ren flinched.

“You felt that,” Ansa said.

“Through the handle.” He spread his fingers. Red correction burns crossed both palms where the portable clock's manual phase contacts had heated during the voyage. “It expected the screen. Not this exact screen. The range mattered.”

On her slate, Ansa pulled the pulse history beside Neme's predicted oscillation. The living pattern had arrived without words. It offered intervals measured against a frame humans and Orison had established through prime pulses, witnessed trajectories, and the first-arc's touch. Each stroke implied a future condition. Each next stroke arrived near that condition, though never at its mathematically perfect center.

Ren enlarged six cycles. “We kept treating the deviation as transmission damage.”

“You ruled that out on the tug.”

“I ruled out random damage. We assumed the remainder came from different bodies measuring the same event.”

The clock gave three quick contractions. Its case tugged against the cradle, then relaxed before the dock's gravity estimate updated.

Ansa overlaid the local prediction envelope. The contractions fell inside it, not along the central line. “It didn't predict the pull.”

“It accepted our prediction of the pull.”

Mara stepped closer to the screen. “Accepted how?”

Ren touched two burned fingers to the safe side of the mesh. “By staying coupled when our estimate missed.”

Ansa stripped out the prime markers and compared every exchange since the shared clock had entered the magnetotail. Human instruments had sent future events as narrow points. The Orison replies had widened or narrowed around them. At first the variation looked like poor precision from an ecology spread across kilometers. Yet the accepted band changed after each successful correction. When Ivo's crew returned mass rather than taking a sample, the clock tolerated a larger human timing error. After the defense flotilla fired, the band contracted nearly to nothing. Saving the first-arc at the partial loosening widened it again.

A vocabulary assembled itself through consequence. Not nouns. Not even verbs. The pattern kept a record of how wrong another body could be before cooperation ceased.

“Tolerated prediction error,” Ansa said.

Ren's tired grin appeared and faded. “Trust with units.”

“Trust has no certified unit.”

“It does now.”

She wanted to object that one negotiated measurement could not contain a relationship. Then the dock shifted under her boots. The clock predicted a slightly different shift, accepted the discrepancy, and remained coupled to human metal. Precision alone would have broken contact. Continuance occupied the margin.

Mara crouched beside the passive screen. “Can it coordinate a correction?”

Ansa looked past her through the freight gallery. Neme's horizon filled every exterior display, a dark curve striped by tide-city lights. Tull's first shove had pulled the moon off its managed track. Kehr and human crews had arrested the worst oscillation, but the temporary hold expired at SD57. Seven Cantilevers would have to push while Orison bodies shaped magnetic pressure from the far side. An error small enough to satisfy Aphelion could still flood a tide district.

“It can tell us which disagreement breaks cooperation,” she said. “That does not tell us which risk to accept.”

Mara stood. “Good. We already have people for that.”

The correction room had once scheduled freight elevators. Its old wall map showed fixed shafts descending through Neme's crust in clean white lines. Three lines now carried red closures. One had moved thirty-eight meters sideways relative to its upper terminus. Technicians had covered the fixed map with transparent local overlays until every shaft appeared to have several incompatible histories.

Ansa connected her full Neme model to the central table. Her customary output package would have shown one recommended burn sequence, confidence intervals, and a list of consequences arranged by probability. Instead she opened the assumption layers beneath it: structural age estimates, tide response, power reserves, evacuation delays, Orison pressure tolerances, witness-swarm lag, crew burn limits, and the Authority's liability weights. Forty-seven thousand dependencies unfolded across the table.

Mara gave a low whistle. “You brought us the machinery.”

“I brought you everything that can make the answer wrong.”

Fourteen jurisdictional channels waited along the wall. Meridia's seal occupied the top left by protocol. Serein had refused that arrangement and appeared in a window on the lower edge. Halcyon transmitted from a classroom fabrication hall because its formal modeling chamber had lost reserve power. Rook sent three engineers and a labor delegate. Vela's channel showed atmosphere tanks behind Min Calder's former training supervisor. Delays ranged from seconds to nearly half an hour.

Olan Brace joined without a backdrop. His image showed only his face and the blue Authority bar at his collar. “Senior Cartographer Meren, confirm distribution level.”

“All model layers. Live edits enabled.”

A pause crossed the channels unevenly as his words reached each place. “The emergency review authorized local thresholds, not alteration of the core solution.”

“The core solution assumes every local threshold can be converted into a common loss function.”

“That assumption permits a single executable sequence.”

“It also hides who absorbs each error.” Ansa released the edit keys before he could answer. Fourteen authentication marks appeared. “Neme's correction cannot use a map that gives affected jurisdictions access only after optimization.”

Laleh Sorn entered from Serein with a tide engineer's field board tucked under one arm. “We asked for veto authority.”

“You asked for control over consequences inside your jurisdiction,” Ansa said. “Start by correcting what my model claims about them.”

The first edit came from Neme. Mara replaced the certified foundation response curve for Tide City Kesh with data gathered after the shove. The city could tolerate less lateral acceleration than Aphelion had assumed, but more vertical change if crews evacuated two lower wards. Her revision erased Ansa's safest sequence.

Rook changed a ballast delivery time. Vela cut the available rescue propellant because two tankers had been reassigned after defense mobilization. Halcyon increased the minimum power floor for school atmosphere lifts. Meridia objected to a delay that would close three inner phase transfers. Serein refused a Cantilever ramp that placed correction debt into its reserve envelope.

Within eleven minutes, no single recommended line remained.

Olan watched the solution field split into branches. “This demonstrates my concern.”

“It demonstrates ours,” Laleh said. “Her elegant answer required our numbers to stay wrong.”

Ansa kept her hands off the controls. Every instinct trained at Aphelion told her to reconcile the branches, remove inconsistent assumptions, and restore a line crews could follow. Yet reconciliation would place her judgment above the people reporting broken elevators and missing fuel. She could identify physical contradictions. She could not decide that a school lift mattered less than a transfer window merely because both entered her model as power.

Ren moved behind the table, trailing the portable clock on its mobile cradle. The living fibers responded to the branch field. Blue pulses spread whenever two jurisdictions' predictions overlapped. They contracted around incompatible claims.

“It's treating edits as proposed trajectories,” he said. “Not statements about trajectories.”

Ansa saw the distinction. A model assumption did not need universal agreement if the correction could carry several bounded predictions and preserve cooperation through their differences. She assigned each local edit its own error tolerance. Where claims overlapped, the shared clock marked room for coordination. Where they did not, the model displayed the bodies exposed by choosing either branch.

Edrin appeared on Halcyon's channel, still wearing a school emergency vest. “Your atmosphere figure counts reserve duration after evacuation begins.”

“Yes.”

“We cannot begin until the transfer children arrive from the outer berths. The variance has them scattered across four hubs.”

“Give me the latest arrival distribution.”

“I already entered it.”

His edit opened below the power layer. Ansa had treated delayed students as a transit problem and local population as a stable quantity. Edrin's data connected the two. Holding Halcyon's reserve floor required power before the correction, not only during it. The change removed another favored branch.

For once, he did not ask whether she understood what had been absent. He had put the missing bodies directly onto her map.

More edits arrived than the table could display. Some carried raw logs. Others offered political limits with no physical measurement attached. A Rook delegate declared that no correction could strand its miners beyond rescue range. Meridia demanded preservation of hospital transfer capacity. Neme required local command over elevator shutdowns. Vela tied fuel release to crew recovery guarantees. Several claims contradicted prior certifications; two contradicted each other inside the same jurisdiction.

Olan leaned toward his camera. “Who certifies this model after alteration?”

“The people making each alteration,” Laleh said.

“That answers authorship, not audit.”

“It answers responsibility.”

“Responsibility without reconstructable procedure becomes accusation after failure.” His voice stayed level. “I will not order crews into a sequence whose origin cannot be recovered.”

Ansa disliked that he had found the correct objection. Distributed authority did not excuse an untraceable decision. If two edits changed the same load, a later reviewer had to know which one controlled, who saw the conflict, and what evidence survived. Otherwise sharing the model could dissolve responsibility as effectively as centralizing it.

Ren parked the clock beside the table. “Give every change a trajectory.”

Ansa turned. “In the model?”

“In custody.” He opened the sealed record format they had used for the first wrong orbit. “An edit arrives with its source state, local clock, evidence, affected layer, and the prior version it changes. No overwrites. We preserve the branch even if no one chooses it.”

Olan's gaze shifted toward him. “Forty-seven thousand dependencies will generate millions of custody links.”

“Then we should stop pretending a clean answer came from nowhere.” Ren pulled a witness-swarm filament across the reader. The blue pulse divided around three stored versions and reunited when their predictions converged. “The shared clock already keeps disagreement as history. We can do the same.”

He named the method provenance because ownership sounded too much like control. Each modification would carry a verifiable descent from observation to assumption to consequence. Local teams could alter their layers. Cross-jurisdiction changes required both affected seals. Physical contradictions remained visible rather than being averaged away. The current executable branch would never erase the roads not taken.

Ansa built the first custody graph by hand. Mara's foundation curve descended from sensors installed after the shove. Edrin's power floor descended from actual student locations. Vela's fuel restriction traced to tanker assignments and signed crew conditions. Meridia's transfer demand traced to scheduled patients, not an abstract service quota. When two branches competed for the same Cantilever interval, the graph displayed the people and machinery at either end.

Ren automated only the links. He refused to let the system infer consent from overlap.

They tested the method on an elevator shutdown that crossed three authority layers. Neme proposed stopping Shaft Nine before the first correction rise. Serein's Cantilever team needed that same interval to drain structural heat, while Meridia had assigned it to a hospital transfer. In Ansa's former workflow, priority weights would have selected the patients and hidden the rejected loads in a sensitivity report.

This time the graph kept all three claims alive. Mara moved the elevator stop eleven minutes earlier, exposing a lower tide ward to one additional surge. Its local council accepted that risk only after the evacuation lead signed the revised route. Serein divided its heat drain across two shorter intervals. Meridia's hospital coordinator shifted four patients to a slower berth and refused to move a fifth whose support frame could not tolerate the delay.

No optimizer discovered the arrangement. People changed separate plans after seeing who occupied the margins. Ansa checked the resulting physics and found one conflict: the shorter heat drains left Cantilever 64 above its certified shell temperature. A Rook materials engineer supplied a field inspection showing an intact reserve layer. Olan required the image stream, calibration history, and inspector's credential before allowing that claim into the executable branch.

The procedure consumed forty-two minutes to recover eleven. It also left a record clear enough that Mara could point to the exact ward accepting extra water, Meridia could identify the patient who constrained timing, and Serein could withdraw its heat concession if the shell reading changed. Ansa had never seen an efficient model contain so much refusal. The accumulated refusals made this one executable.

By Neme's next local dawn, fourteen jurisdictions had altered the model. None possessed the whole answer, yet each could reconstruct the active sequence from raw measurement through every disputed choice. Olan remained on the channel long after his staff began sending him private warnings.

“This can be audited,” he said at last.

Laleh studied him. “Will you permit it?”

“I said what it can do.”

Ansa saved his distinction in the custody log. The model now held an executable second correction, conditional on local approvals still pending. It also held seven rejected branches, three unresolved power conflicts, and an Orison pressure schedule expressed as tolerated prediction error. She sent the active trajectory through the portable clock.

The living pattern answered by widening around six human corrections. At the seventh, it collapsed.

Ren's hands stopped above the table.

Ansa reran the exchange. The failure occurred forty-three minutes into the burn, when Cantilever 67 shifted phase while Kehr's outer bodies carried Neme's lateral load. Human timing remained inside tolerance. Orison pressure remained inside the witnessed band. One element sat between them: Relay Kappa, the mobile clock repeater assigned to the correction field.

“Shielding heat,” Mara said after checking the hardware layer. “The relay crosses the exhaust wake twice.”

“It survives either crossing,” Ansa said.

“Not both.”

The second exposure would blind the clock for ninety seconds. Without its reference, human Cantilevers would continue on the certified sequence while Orison bodies corrected against a changing Neme. Their separate accuracies would amplify the moon's oscillation.

Olan's staff proposed a hardened drone. Vela reported no launch window. Rook offered a spare shield plate that could not arrive before SD59. Neme suggested cutting one crossing, which moved fatal foundation load into Kesh. Every branch closed somewhere else.

Ren rotated the relay path. “A person can replace the damaged plate between exposures.”

Ansa felt the room become geometrically simple. “A suited technician would take the first dose.”

“Within field limits.”

“The second crossing exceeds them.”

“Only if the technician stays with the relay.”

“The clock loses alignment during an external transfer.” She heard her own voice taking refuge in procedure. “You would need to carry it through the field.”

Ren flexed his burned hands. He had already understood.

“No,” Ansa said.

Mara looked between them, then closed the public audio on Neme's channel. Olan did not interrupt. Across the table, the custody graph continued drawing links between assumptions and bodies.

Ren touched the clock's human-made case. Blue light gathered under his palm, holding close to the scars. “We asked it how much error it could tolerate from us.”

“That does not make you correction mass.”

“I built the relay interface. I know which failures can be repaired in a suit.”

“Then train another technician.”

“Six days?” He shook his head. “You know the deadline.”

Ansa opened the personnel exposure model and hated the speed with which it supplied numbers. A rotating pair could survive if the clock released coupling during transfer. Its living pattern refused every simulated break. Remote repair failed under magnetic noise. An unmanned relay preserved hardware and lost the shared frame. Ren's proposal alone kept the coordination path open through both crossings.

He entered the change himself. Source state. Exposure estimate. Affected layer. Alternatives rejected. Personal consent did not certify system necessity, so Mara countersigned the technical claim. Ansa could still deny assignment authority.

Ren sent her the final custody link.

On the central table, his name appeared inside the correction trajectory, not beside it. The model no longer hid the body required to make its clean line hold.

“Authorize me to carry Relay Kappa,” he said.

The portable shared clock pulsed once against his damaged hands, already measuring the error they had agreed to carry.

Chapter 18 — The Price of Correction

The second correction began with six bolts refusing to turn.

Ivo hung under Remainder's port fuel spine, boots locked to the service rail, while Neme occupied half the viewport below him. A torque driver whined against the first bolt and stopped at its safety limit. The collar had warped during the defense burn. Five neighboring collars showed the same pale crescent where metal had stretched past memory.

“Report,” Sana Vi said over the work channel.

“Six fasteners have developed political objections.”

“Can they be persuaded?”

“Not before ignition.” Ivo switched the driver to manual and tested the collar with both hands. “We leave the inspection plate open. If the crack grows, I need to see where.”

“That plate sits over a live feed manifold.”

“I know what we built badly.”

“You inherited it.”

“At sixty years, inheritance becomes participation.”

Sana marked the plate open on the maintenance board. She held operational veto over any burn that threatened every escape path, a condition Ivo had accepted because his crew had refused his habit of deciding for them. The renewed consent cards sat in a sealed panel on the bridge. Their terms also reserved rescue fuel and placed Neme's local crews inside command. No one could spend either condition as morale.

Chief rigger Bex Olun passed him a fiber scope. Ivo threaded it behind the collar. The port fuel spine's inner jacket showed a crack nine centimeters long, branching toward the cold line. They had known about the branch. During the last six days it had acquired a second tip.

“Expected growth under first burn?” Bex asked.

“Four centimeters if the jacket cools evenly. Eight if it doesn't.”

“And if the branch reaches the manifold?”

“We isolate the spine, lose twelve percent thrust, and miss Neme.”

Bex waited.

Ivo gave the rest. “Or we hold feed, keep thrust, and give the fracture somewhere else to go.”

“Useful ship.”

“It has always been good at narrowing choices.”

They left the plate unsealed and pulled themselves toward the bridge. There was no central chair. Thirty-two crew worked from rail consoles arranged around propulsion, navigation, rigging, and rescue control. Two Neme anchor coordinators occupied temporary boards beside Sana. Their feeds ran to tide-city foundations, elevator brakes, and seven Cantilevers that would push the moon over the next five hours.

Ivo clipped in beside propulsion status. Relay Kappa moved ahead of them as a hard white point. Ren Adek rode its service frame with the portable shared clock fixed against his chest. Two Orison bodies curved beyond the field, visible only as distortions in Asteron's blue magnetic tail. Witness swarms held a loose path between them.

“Relay check,” Ivo said.

Ren answered through static. “Clock coupled. First shield reads nominal. Suit dose low. My left glove has stopped pretending to fit.”

“You had six days to find a glove.”

“I used them making your ship intelligible.”

“That work remains incomplete.”

Blue pulses crossed the shared clock display. Human timing occupied one side of the bridge pane. Orison pressure predictions occupied the other. The living pattern did not merge them. It showed the error each side had agreed to carry without breaking contact.

Neme Control entered the final hold. A woman in Tide City Kesh counted occupied shelter wards, elevator positions, and foundation crews still outside hardened rooms. Her list contained no rhetoric. Each confirmed number removed one reason to delay. Two atmosphere lifts on the western shelf had not reached reserve speed. Cantilever 64 ran hotter than certification but inside the inspected layer Rook had vouched for. Four hospital patients remained in transfer. A fifth had been moved early because the provenance graph had kept her support frame visible.

Sana opened the consent channel. “Conditions unchanged from renewal. Local command remains active. Rescue reserve remains sealed. Anyone withdrawing, speak now or signal private.”

The bridge waited through one full circuit of delayed replies. No card changed state.

“Correction authority shared,” said Neme Control.

“Burn authority accepted,” Sana replied.

Ivo put his hand on the propulsion rail. “Feed port and starboard at six percent. Let the old metal remember slowly.”

The drives lit without drama. A vibration entered the rail and settled into his bones. On the main pane, Remainder's thrust line joined seven Cantilever vectors and two broad Orison pressure arcs. No single force could move Neme safely. Together they began changing where the moon would arrive days later.

For the first twenty minutes, the work followed cards. Crew watched temperatures rise, moved coolant between tired channels, and corrected nozzle alignment by fractions too small for a person to feel. Neme's horizon shifted less than a finger's width against the stars. Far below, tides had already begun responding.

Kesh reported water climbing an evacuation wall seven minutes early.

“Reduce Cantilever 66 by point four,” its local coordinator said.

Meridia Control objected from the shared channel. “That violates the central ramp.”

“It preserves Kesh's accepted limit,” Sana said.

The Neme coordinator entered the change. Provenance links flashed across the board. Serein took part of the displaced load. An Orison arc widened to accommodate the new timing. Ren's clock pulsed acceptance.

Ivo eased port feed upward. The open inspection plate trembled on his suit camera. A measurement line crossed the old crack. Ten centimeters. Eleven.

At forty-three minutes, Relay Kappa entered its first exhaust wake.

Magnetic noise turned Ren's voice into clipped consonants. The relay shield heated through its safe band, exactly as the shared model predicted. He left the service frame, crossed two meters along a handline, and released the damaged outer plate. It spun away with a bright edge. His replacement plate locked on three corners.

“Fourth latch won't seat,” he said.

Ivo watched the suit feed. “Debris in the receiver.”

“I can see that.”

“You asked for repair advice by broadcasting failure.”

“I was sharing the experience.” Ren braced one boot against the frame and drove the latch with his burned hand. “Seat confirmed.”

The clock widened its acceptance band. Kehr's pressure arc arrived two seconds later than the human center and well inside the tolerated range.

Hours changed the bridge. Meal bulbs replaced tools at consoles without anyone leaving a station. Sanitation breaks required signed handoffs. Sweat dried cold inside Ivo's shirt while the port feed temperature climbed. The correction's purpose remained abstract on the main trajectory, a white future line bending away from catastrophe. Its price arrived through individual alarms.

A Neme anchor crew lost its ranging mast when a tide surge struck the lower platform. Their coordinator asked Remainder to supply radar through the tug's maintenance array. Navigation could give them range, but pointing the array would blind the rescue cone for thirteen minutes. Sana sent the conflict back to local command.

Kesh's controller chose a shorter scan window and divided it into three passes. During each pass, the rescue board lost one slice of sky. Ivo had spent years treating incomplete sensor coverage as a defect awaiting repair. Now the gaps had names, durations, and people authorized to accept them. He watched the first dark interval cross the board while an anchor team used their borrowed range to tighten a cable below.

“Load rising on Anchor Four,” Neme Control said.

The cable carried part of Remainder's thrust into a foundation ridge. Its outer strands had begun separating under a harmonic the Aphelion model placed outside the certified band. A local rigger proposed releasing one clamp and letting the cable turn with the load.

Ivo saw the consequence before propulsion plotted it. “That sends torsion into their lower gantry.”

“We have cleared the gantry,” the rigger answered.

“Cleared people or equipment?”

“People. We lose the two service crawlers.”

Sana looked toward him. The old command arrangement would have let a tug captain preserve mission hardware in another jurisdiction by refusing the release. Their shared terms left the exposed crew in charge of their own foundation. Ivo entered his thrust limits and waited.

“Release approved locally,” Neme Control said.

Anchor Four rotated. Two service crawlers broke against the gantry and disappeared into the dark below. Cable tension dropped into tolerance. Nothing on the main trajectory recorded the machines except a minor improvement in confidence. Ivo added both losses to the maintenance ledger, not because anyone could recover them during the crisis, but because surviving plans had a habit of forgetting what they consumed.

An elevator on Neme's northern shelf jammed between transfer nodes. Its brakes drew emergency power from Cantilever 63. The extra draw cooled one tide-city pump below service pressure. Kesh rerouted water and lost two shelter seals. Local crews held both by hand. A station around Serein shed residential power to keep its phase surface from tearing. On Vela, rescue tankers missed their departure plane and had to spend part of their own return margin.

Each failure moved the next one. No explosion granted a clean boundary between cause and consequence.

“Spine crack at fourteen,” Bex said.

Ivo brought the scope up. The main branch had reached the cold-line brace. A white mist appeared at its tip and vanished into the capture hood.

“Leak?” Sana asked.

“Permeation. Not flow yet.”

“Escape paths?”

“Starboard can still back us out.”

She checked the reserve ledger rather than trusting him. “Agreed. Continue.”

During the third hour, Orison pressure faltered on the outer arc. The body humans labeled Little Weight moved too close to Neme's induced field, then corrected away. Its withdrawal shifted load toward Remainder. Ivo added two percent thrust. The port jacket gave a sound like a wrench dropped in a distant room.

Bex stared at the scope. “Branch split.”

“How many?”

“Three visible.”

The feed manifold pressure began to wander. Ivo sent riggers into the armored service passage with clamp collars and a foam injector. He did not tell them to hurry. Speed made hands stupid around stressed fuel.

Ren's relay approached the second wake crossing.

According to the model, its new shield would fail near the center. Ren had to detach it, replace an inner sink, and carry the shared clock beyond the hot region while the relay frame lost alignment. Once he entered, no ship could reach him without crossing the same exhaust.

“Relay Kappa, verify exit package,” Sana said.

Ren held a small return canister to his suit camera. “Clock release charges armed. Witness filaments secured. If I miss the handoff, the frame ejects toward Remainder after crossing.”

“If you remain attached, it ejects you too.”

“No. Combined mass misses the capture cone.”

Ivo cut into the channel. “Then widen the cone.”

“Your port spine cannot support lateral capture thrust.”

“My spine did not join this conversation.”

“It contributed data.” Ren's face appeared for one clear second through his visor. He looked younger without the Aphelion workroom around him. “Keep Neme inside the accepted line, Captain.”

“Keep yourself on the return canister.”

The relay entered magnetic glare before Ren could answer.

Static flooded the clock channel. Blue pulses broke into fragments across Ivo's display. Cantilevers continued their certified ramp. Kehr's pressure correction altered in response to Neme's live movement, but humans could no longer see how much deviation the Orison would accept.

Relay shielding dropped to sixty percent, then thirty-one.

Ren moved by suit telemetry alone. One hand released the outer cover. The cover did not clear. It caught on the clock's filament mesh and began dragging the instrument away from alignment.

“Cut the mesh,” Ivo said.

Ren's suit transmitted no voice. His tool current spiked. The cover separated, taking one layer of shielding with it.

At that moment, the port fuel spine cracked fully.

The jacket opened from inspection collar to manifold brace. Fuel flashed into the capture hood faster than its vents could route. The passage kicked sideways. Bex and two riggers struck the armored wall. Their lines held.

Ivo isolated the cold line and felt the thrust imbalance rotate Remainder toward Neme.

“Starboard compensate,” he ordered.

Sana's hand covered the command rail. “That spends the shared escape path.”

“If we don't compensate, we cross Kappa's line.”

“Port feed status?”

“Open jacket. Manifold intact for now.”

“Can you hold pressure?”

Ivo watched fuel frost spread across the camera. He could close the spine and preserve the tug. Neme would lose its physical anchor during the worst segment. The resulting oscillation would exceed every tide-city limit. Holding feed risked a manifold rupture inside the hull.

“Seven minutes,” Bex said from the passage. Blood floated in beads along her cheek. “We can collar the brace in seven.”

Sana made the choice before Ivo could take it from her. “Maintain port feed for seven minutes. Rescue section moves to armored stations. All nonessential compartments vented. I hold veto if manifold pressure crosses red.”

The bridge obeyed. Ivo kept the damaged spine alive by adjusting pressure faster than its cracked jacket could change shape. His hands moved between valves, coolant, and thrust trim. Each correction bought seconds and damaged another component.

Relay Kappa lost all external shielding.

Ren had reached the inner sink. His suit dose climbed past the planned limit. The shared clock still transmitted because he held it against the relay's surviving core with both arms. Its living filaments had wrapped around his chest and one leg.

A human trajectory flashed toward Kehr: continue pressure, five seconds late accepted.

No answer appeared.

“Orison arc collapsing,” Navigation said.

Little Weight had withdrawn too far. A second vast body rolled into its load, perhaps Harrow of Rain, though the human tags could not establish civic identity during the field noise. Its pressure front gathered slowly. Neme's motion approached the edge of the human line.

Ren sent another prediction. Continue pressure, eight seconds late accepted.

His suit medical channel reported tissue heating. He could still release the clock and push toward the ejection canister. If he did, the shared frame would drift before Kehr's correction arrived.

Ivo opened a private line he knew the field could not carry. “Ren Adek, you have preserved it. Release now.”

Only static answered.

The relay's camera showed Ren locking one elbow through the service frame. His other hand flattened over the portable clock. He had stopped trying to repair the shield.

At six minutes, Bex closed the first clamp around the port brace. The manifold pressure crossed red and came back by one division. Sana did not call the veto.

Neme Control reported simultaneous elevator brake failures in two tide cities. Station power fell under reserve on three dependent habitats. Emergency channels multiplied along the edge of Ivo's board, each asking for thrust changes the correction could no longer make without exporting a worse load elsewhere.

The Orison answer arrived eleven seconds late.

The shared clock accepted it.

A pressure front curved around Neme, broad enough to move oceans before instruments registered the force as local acceleration. Kehr's bodies took the lateral load. Cantilever 67 passed through its phase shift. Remainder held the physical anchor on a leaking spine while the moon crossed the narrow center of the revised path.

Ren's medical channel stopped.

No one on the bridge said his name. They still had two hours of burn.

The aftermath entered by attrition. A tide barrier failed after its evacuation completed, but the water reached an atmosphere plant below. One station restored life support after nine minutes and another after twenty-six. Elevator cars became shelters where their brakes held. Crews opened pressure doors, moved oxygen bottles, and cut rotating habitats into smaller survivable sections. Ivo saw only status marks changing color.

Bex's team completed the second clamp. The port spine could no longer vary thrust without tearing loose, so Ivo fixed it at four percent and built every remaining maneuver around that limit. Sana reassigned seven injured crew from active consoles while keeping all thirty-two accounted for. The tug's port radiator folded halfway and stuck. Heat filled compartments already stripped of atmosphere.

When the final Cantilever ramp ended, Neme remained inside the corrected envelope.

Its elevators did not all remain standing. Its tide cities did not all keep power. Dependent stations across three moons continued reporting structural failures long after the burn line flattened.

Remainder drifted out on starboard trim, hull scarred, port fuel spine held together by two clamps and an armored passage kept empty in case the manifold let go. The correction had not destroyed the ship. It had taken away most of the actions a ship could perform.

Ivo stayed at the rail until Neme Control certified the temporary orbit. Then he released his hands and discovered a metal splinter buried across his right palm. He did not remember receiving it.

The first confirmed toll reached them six hours later. It changed upward in irregular steps as isolated stations restored contact. Sana kept the number off the general channel until every jurisdiction certified its own losses. No camera feeds accompanied the count. No district names appeared until families received notice.

At 1,140, the total held.

Ivo read the figure once. One thousand one hundred forty civilians had died in structural and life-support failures across dependent stations. Ren made 1,141. The arithmetic connected them without making their deaths equivalent.

A retrieval drone found Relay Kappa beyond the exhaust wake. It carried no living suit signal. The ejection charge had fired exactly as programmed, sending the portable clock toward Remainder's capture cone without the extra mass of Ren's body.

Bex brought the canister to the bridge. Her cheek had been sealed under a transparent patch. She set it on the propulsion rail because no clear table remained.

The human case had blackened. Most witness filaments survived beneath its mesh. When Ivo touched the handle, the shared clock gave a sequence he recognized from the burn: prediction, accepted error, correction. Then it repeated the structure with one interval left empty where Ren's timing had occupied it.

The Orison pattern tightened around that absence. Pressure markers folded inward and held, not smoothing the gap or replacing it. After three cycles, the pattern opened again toward Neme's continuing path.

Ivo lifted the clock from the rail. Its scorched handle warmed his injured palm while Remainder limped beside the moon they had not managed to save without loss.

Chapter 19 — What Happened at Vaul

Kehr drew the dead human's interval through five living bodies and found no place to set it down.

Harrow of Rain received the first pressure rise. Ember-Side took the second across its torn outer sail. Little Weight, still recovering from Neme, failed to answer on the expected beat. Bent Morning supplied the missing correction. Silt Crown held the wide return around the first-arcs. The sequence passed through Kehr as a trajectory with one participant absent and every remaining participant required to continue.

Near Neme, the humans had fastened their scorched shared clock to a service frame outside the damaged tug. Its filaments carried Ren Adek's final pattern. Kehr knew the human by correction rather than label: the small body who had kept an agreed future open while heat destroyed his ability to reach it. Human radio now supplied a sound for him. Ren. The clock preserved more than the sound.

Witness swarms gathered around the frame without touching it. The tug had weapons scars, leaking heat, and one thrust line that held too steady because damaged machinery could no longer vary it. Thirty-two human signals remained inside. Kehr distinguished their separate metabolic flickers poorly, but the clock carried their common reference with painful clarity.

Narrow Mercy approached from behind Silt Crown. Tull's pressure compressed the juvenile sheltering arc by a fraction. Not harm. A reminder that every delay occupied space needed by the migration.

Send passage, Tull proposed through mass. Send deadline. The settled bodies understand consequence.

Kehr widened the first-arcs away from that compression. They understand incomplete consequence.

The exchange carried no grammatical objects. Silt Crown shifted inward. Tull's outer mineral skein shifted across its path. Their possible collision held a conditional meaning: continued delay threatened both civic minds and every juvenile traveling behind them.

Harrow placed a slow correction against Kehr's oldest remembered route.

Vaul pressed near.

For twelve returns, Kehr had carried an absence shaped like a choice. The memory line entered Harrow, crossed two bodies, then appeared to end inside Silt Crown without a recorded departure. Each time Kehr sought the missing span, consensus produced an old explanation: predatory contact, nursery failure, necessary silence. The explanation had edges but no interior. It permitted shame to exist without attaching shame to an act.

Kehr had called that mercy once.

Now the dead human's clock offered a different rule. An accepted error did not disappear when correction continued. It remained in the shared frame so future bodies could know what trust had survived.

Kehr turned all five bodies toward the clock.

The movement cost corridor distance. Ember-Side spent stored charge. Little Weight exposed an injured sail to Asteron's shear. First-arcs adjusted behind them in confused waves, some following Kehr's old route and others following Tull. The division ran through living bodies, not through an abstract vote.

Harrow of Rain opened the first witnessed trajectory.

A ring of swarms arranged themselves around the human clock as Vaul's nursery had once arranged around its star. They marked the low magnetic arches where first-arcs developed correction loops. Silt Crown supplied mineral dust matching the nursery's old mass. Bent Morning shaped a broad, gentle pressure field and let the swarms learn it. Human instruments watched from the tug.

Kehr sent the clock a prediction: the innermost swarm would cross a chosen plane after seven intervals.

It crossed after seven.

Ember-Side introduced a narrow disturbance through the field. The swarm arrived late but living. Harrow repeated the disturbance at greater coherence. Three swarms lost their paths. On the next pass, none could complete the predicted crossing.

The human clock contracted around the failed futures.

That showed sterilization poorly. Humans might read attack, experiment, or present threat. Kehr required them to witness origin.

Silt Crown released fourteen small pulses, the count the humans used for their inhabited regions. Witness swarms copied the timing signature of Pelagos phase gates from the shared reference. One pulse produced a local wake. The next joined it before coherence faded. With each repetition, the disturbance grew broader and traveled farther from the clock.

Kehr could not show years in a human attention span. The clock allowed ratio. One interval became one gate season. A hundred became decades. Harrow compressed the repetition while preserving cumulative force. The resulting front entered the model nursery from beyond its boundary.

Every swarm lost its developed path.

The demonstration changed the first-arcs behind Kehr. Those near Bent Morning copied the nursery geometry, expecting a lesson in passage. Their young loops sought the vanished arches and found only phase noise. One juvenile recoiled into another's sail. A third repeated the seven-interval crossing after every model swarm had already failed, unable to release a prediction once learned.

Kehr had intended a representation. The first-arcs experienced damaged habitat.

Harrow reduced the field, but Tull increased sheltering pressure around the juveniles. Narrow Mercy bodies moved between them and the human clock. Their action carried both protection and accusation. Kehr had spent living attention to make old harm legible to strangers. No model remained harmless to bodies asked to recognize themselves inside it.

Bent Morning guided the repeating juvenile out of the dead arch. It offered a new crossing at nine intervals and changed its own position to make the future true. The first-arc followed. Two others joined, each carrying a different tolerated error. Kehr recorded their recovery beside the Vaul pattern instead of overwriting the failure.

From the tug, human jets answered with a crude copy of the cumulative front. Three pulses accumulated. A fourth arrived too far apart and coherence decayed. After a pause, they tried again with shorter spacing. Their second pattern sterilized only the outer model swarms.

Kehr read the error as a question. Did proximity determine damage?

Silt Crown moved the simulated nursery farther from the clock while preserving pulse interval. Harrow reduced each phase wake but increased their count. The dead region formed at the same cumulative coherence, not at the same distance or single-pulse strength. Human instruments immediately redirected toward their own Cantilevers. The strangers had not merely witnessed accusation. They had extracted a causal relationship they could test against Pelagos.

That result disturbed Long Recall. Three distant bodies folded their witness surfaces and erased themselves from easy observation. Their civic signals pressed through Kehr's outer swarms: knowledge of wake mechanics could help humans loosen the system, or help them shape the disturbance as a weapon. Vaul had begun with unintended exhaust. Another nursery might die under deliberate calculation.

Near Turning answered by extending witness swarms toward the human receiver. Concealment had already allowed the front to grow unwitnessed across decades. More silence offered no neutral path.

One Near Turning cohort carried scars from the second sterilized nursery. It opened those damaged surfaces to the human sensors, linking Vaul's demonstration to another loss on a different route. Long Recall tried to occlude the display with a dust veil. The cohort changed altitude rather than forcing contact, preserving both evidence and distance. Even disclosure required a correction that did not turn disagreement into collision.

Tull held both positions long enough to examine them. Narrow Mercy distrusted humans and distrusted a doctrine that had failed to preserve two nurseries. Its bodies wanted the corridor, not contact. Yet corridor passage now depended on humans understanding which phase behavior created lethal coherence. Refusing causal proof could protect future Orison from human intent while killing the first-arcs already present.

Political division entered the model as three motions. Long Recall withdrew outward. Near Turning approached the clock. Narrow Mercy stayed between the receiver and juveniles while permitting the test to continue. Human sensors tracked each movement. Kehr did not translate them into votes. A polity could shelter, refuse, bargain, and threaten within one correction. Any summary would discard the bodies doing different work.

Little Weight sent a weak pulse toward the tug. Its injury from Neme distorted the interval. Human instruments accepted the pulse anyway, widening around its error. The response reached Kehr like an offered support surface. A damaged participant could remain inside shared timing without pretending damage had vanished.

Kehr returned that allowance to the juvenile who had repeated seven. The new nine-interval route remained available. Its loop steadied against Bent Morning's sail.

The model swarms gathered at Vaul's failing arches and emitted uneven distress trajectories. Some asked for adults to hold the magnetic folds open. Others tried to follow the departing migration and lacked the mass to cross its shear. Eleven pulses survived after the final healthy cluster left. The human clock marked each pulse separately. No statistical compression hid the late signals inside a total.

Human instruments changed state. Tight beams left the tug toward Neme, Aphelion, and the settlements beyond. Kehr could not know the words attached to them. The humans had recognized their own phase signature. Recognition altered where they sent evidence. That made it semantic enough.

Tull drove a pressure wedge through the outer demonstration. Several swarms scattered before completing the dead nursery pattern.

You give them injury and hide need, Tull signaled. They will calculate debt. Debt does not open corridors.

Kehr answered by restoring the displaced swarms. Injury without our choice remains false.

Our choice preserved the first-arcs who live.

Not all who lived then.

That disagreement traveled into Kehr. Little Weight held Tull's premise. Ember-Side rejected its conclusion. Bent Morning carried fear that humans would use confession as permission to deny passage. Silt Crown wanted the corridor even if proof required a simpler lie. Consensus did not hold.

Kehr became we.

We remembered Vaul's star dimmed by coherent wake. We remembered mature bodies arriving after the nursery arches had collapsed. We remembered first-arcs still signaling from broken magnetic folds. Yet these recollections lacked position. No body in the current loop admitted having occupied the deciding path.

Harrow moved close enough to Silt Crown for their sails to exchange charged dust.

The contact hurt. Harrow's old surface carried six centuries of correction scars. Silt Crown's mineral veil scraped across them, opening luminous faults. Memory did not pass as image. It changed which future movements each body could predict.

A route appeared inside Kehr.

Vaul's young star had occupied one focus. The wounded nursery occupied another. Three adult bodies held enough charge to return for damaged first-arcs. A fourth carried healthy juveniles away from the approaching phase front. Human instruments had not yet detected the Orison migration. Returning bodies would have crossed a bright observational plane and made concealment unlikely.

Twelve civic minds had formed the decision loop. Kehr had supplied the decisive correction.

The remembered pressure entered Bent Morning first. Kehr had shifted that body across the rescue route, forcing the three willing adults to follow the departing migration or collide. The action converted disagreement into geometry. Afterward, elders ratified silence doctrine and named the abandoned first-arcs already lost.

They had not been lost when Kehr moved.

The nursery carried weak signals for eleven more intervals. Harrow had received them. Silt Crown had held the route away. Kehr's other bodies had taken shelter around the healthy cluster and damped their own signatures. The migration preserved secrecy while damaged juveniles failed behind them.

Our correction abandoned them.

The restored memory did not arrive everywhere at once. Little Weight received the rescue route but not the last signals. Ember-Side received the signals without the elder deliberation. Bent Morning held Kehr's physical obstruction. Silt Crown admitted the sheltering maneuver. Harrow carried all portions and refused to let the loop close until each body corrected against the same past.

For centuries, Kehr had mistaken this division for ordinary distributed recollection. Orison memory always depended on bodies meeting again. A route carried by one sail could fade if migration placed it beyond exchange. Vaul had used that material fact as procedure. The elders partitioned the twelfth-return memory deliberately. No later consensus could experience the whole decision unless every assigned body surrendered its portion at once.

Harrow now did so.

Pressure tore through Kehr's five hosts. Ember-Side curled its sail. Little Weight dropped out of the Neme holding arc. Bent Morning released charge in a bright involuntary flare. Silt Crown tried to close its exchange surface, but Harrow drove remembered dust into the seam. First-arcs behind them scattered from the abrupt field.

Kehr held no body outside the act.

The complete memory carried more than abandonment. It preserved arguments.

Long Recall had believed human detection would bring predators or weapons. They cited the earlier settled species that had harvested Orison nursery mass after a signal exchange. Narrow Mercy had counted healthy first-arcs against damaged ones and called return an extinction risk. Near Turning had proposed a small rescue body, visible but deniable. Kehr had feared all three futures and chosen the route that ended debate fastest.

No polity had possessed innocence at Vaul. Some had resisted. Others had obeyed pressure they could physically have opposed. Harrow had carried the abandoned signals without breaking formation. Tull's predecessor had supplied the survival count. Kehr had made the correction that rendered every later defense procedural.

Tull struck Silt Crown with a hard lateral pulse.

End this exposure.

Kehr absorbed the blow across Bent Morning and held position.

The humans killed twenty-three first-arcs, Tull continued. Their wake sterilized Vaul. Their lock compresses us now. You turn our corridor claim into a balance of guilt.

Kehr answered in remembered trajectories. A corridor claim built on hidden bodies carries an unmodeled load.

Settled law will use confession as refusal.

Perhaps.

Then you sacrifice the living to accuracy.

That accusation reached Little Weight and remained there. Kehr could not discard it. Truth offered no guaranteed passage. Humans might convert Orison complicity into equivalence, treating abandoned first-arcs as cancellation for sterile nurseries. Olan Brace might use the history to preserve the lock. Defense ministers might call the reenactment proof of alien brutality. First-arcs could die because Kehr chose intelligibility.

Harrow curved around the model nursery and began the second witnessed trajectory.

This time the demonstration showed the Orison choice. Three swarm clusters moved toward the damaged arches. Silt Crown crossed them, reproducing Kehr's old obstruction. Healthy juveniles departed along a hidden route. Eleven weak pulses remained at Vaul. No rescue returned.

The human clock narrowed until the final pulse. Then its tolerated band opened around the absent response.

Kehr felt Ren's interval inside the comparison. A human had died holding shared reference through a destructive field. Orison elders had preserved living bodies by withdrawing shared reference from those they abandoned. The events did not erase or equal each other. The clock could hold both because neither occupied the other's place.

A signal came from the damaged tug. Human maneuver jets fired one brief pulse. Their timing repeated the rescue route toward Vaul, then stopped at Silt Crown's obstruction. A second pulse traced the departing migration.

They had understood choice.

Kehr sent the full trajectory again with one change. Harrow released original witness grains from its oldest sail. The grains had traveled from Vaul for six hundred human years. Their magnetic scars carried the nursery's developing loops, the phase front's arrival, and the eleven unanswered pulses. They moved toward the shared clock in a sealed mineral envelope.

Tull shifted to intercept.

Little Weight moved first. Its injured sail had favored Narrow Mercy during the corridor dispute, yet it now placed itself across Tull's path. Ember-Side joined from below. Their combined pressure did not defeat Tull. It made interception costly enough to require an open political act.

Tull stopped short.

You divide migration authority before hostile witnesses, Tull signaled.

Kehr held the envelope on course. Migration authority already divided. Concealment prevented humans from witnessing it.

Long Recall bodies gathered farther out. Some damped their fields, refusing participation. Near Turning sent swarms to escort the Vaul grains. Narrow Mercy hosts tightened their shelter around first-arcs as if confession itself could exert lethal shear. No unified Orison response existed. Humans saw that too.

The envelope entered the tug's capture field. Mechanical arms closed around it with painful slowness. Kehr waited through light lag while human instruments tested mass, field, and contamination. The shared clock maintained Vaul's final unanswered interval.

Harrow began returning the memory portions to ordinary circulation. Not all bodies accepted them. Two witness cohorts refused the Vaul route and detached from Kehr's recording stream. Several first-arcs copied the final pulse without understanding its history. Tull withdrew behind Silt Crown but did not attack.

Kehr became singular again only by accepting that consensus now included refusal.

The next message required a future, not another past. Kehr arranged five bodies along the low-shear corridor. Human Cantilevers appeared as twelve fixed pressure sources around them. First-arcs occupied every available path. Kehr predicted a passage in which human stations loosened their hold while Orison bodies carried assigned loads away from inhabited moons.

The clock replied with uncertainty wider than any prior exchange.

Kehr did not narrow the request. A narrow prediction would imply certainty no body possessed. Instead Kehr added the Vaul witness grains, Tull's attempted interception, Little Weight's defense, and the human dead interval from Neme as references. Each fact remained attached to its actor.

Human radio sent approximations through the shared clock. Vaul acquired a spoken sound. Abandoned acquired another. A third human label attached to culpability, though Kehr could not determine whether it meant guilt, cause, or liability. Labels alone could not bear the request.

Kehr shaped one final trajectory. It began with the human phase wake sterilizing the nursery. It continued through the Orison obstruction of rescue. It ended with both migrations entering a future corridor neither could create alone.

Tull sent no agreement. Harrow held the past open. Across the five bodies, Kehr allowed every disagreement to remain measurable.

The request went to Neme, Aphelion, and all human jurisdictions carrying the clock's frame: proof of human harm, proof of Orison abandonment, and passage without innocence.

The Vaul grains settled into the human receiver while first-arcs moved behind Kehr in routes no elder could partition again.

Chapter 20 — A Jurisdiction That Moves

The first veto arrived before Ansa had finished drawing the thing it could stop.

Serein's marker crossed the provisional correction path in red. The line did not reject the Open Chord as a whole. It attached itself to Cantilever 64's second decoupling ramp, where Ansa's model sent structural heat into Serein's reserve envelope for nineteen minutes.

Laleh Sorn leaned across the Neme planning table and read the objection aloud for the public record. “No imported correction debt above the inspected shell limit. Local release required after live temperature confirms margin.”

Meridia's representative appeared on the wall channel. “That condition can delay every downstream phase.”

“It can delay the phase that uses our infrastructure,” Laleh said.

“Which means it controls all of them.”

Ansa split the downstream paths into separate colors. Six timings depended directly on Serein's release. Three could move around it if Vela supplied additional mass. Two had no safe alternative yet. “The objection binds nine phases under the current design. It does not bind the other twenty-three.”

A dry voice came from Remainder's damaged bridge. “You have twenty-three phases because your map does not count the repairs between them.”

Ivo Calder sat beside the tug's propulsion status with one hand bandaged. Behind him, the port fuel spine showed no usable variation range. The ship could hold a fixed low thrust or isolate the spine. Every other port maneuver had become a theoretical luxury.

Ansa opened his maintenance layer. “Enter the missing work.”

“I did. Your display filtered tasks below correction-critical priority.”

“That filter should have been removed.”

“It should never have decided whether people turning bolts belonged to the correction.”

He sent fourteen maintenance cards into the active branch. Nozzle inspection. Coolant transfer. Hull compartment clearing. Rigger exposure. Manual clamp checks after every thrust change. Each card acquired a place between Ansa's clean phases. The twenty-three independent timings fell to eleven.

Laleh watched the map contract. “Better.”

“More accurate,” Ansa said.

“Accuracy that redistributes authority qualifies as better today.”

The planning chamber had once managed freight elevators. Its fixed wall map still showed shafts that no longer met their lower stations after Neme's shove. Transparent repair overlays covered those lines. Ansa had used the room for the second correction because nobody working there could forget that geometry changed while law tried to remain attached to its original walls.

All fourteen jurisdictions occupied public channels. Some sent engineers. Others sent delegates, reserve coordinators, labor representatives, or legal officers. Neme supplied tide-city controllers and families from two failed elevator districts. The Authority observed through Olan Brace's staff but did not control the edit keys.

The shared clock rested under passive shielding beside Ansa. Its case carried blackening from Relay Kappa. Ren's last interval remained inside the living pattern, a gap held without repair. Vaul witness grains occupied a sealed reader near it. Beyond Neme, five Orison bodies maintained the contact geometry. Their signals arrived as proposed trajectories, refusals, and changes in load, never as a convenient translated voice.

Ansa placed Serein's veto beside the Orison passage request. “We need a rule that can survive both.”

“Both what?” asked Meridia.

“Bodies refusing a correction made elsewhere.”

The public channel filled with objections delayed by light lag. Human jurisdictions held charters, boundaries, and populations that could sign terms. Orison civic minds moved across several bodies. Their polities overlapped. A witness swarm could carry Near Turning memory while sheltering inside a Narrow Mercy field. Kehr could commit five host bodies only while their internal correction loop held. No fixed territorial analogy survived first use.

Laleh pushed a blank authority layer onto the table. “Start with consequence.”

Ansa selected the second Cantilever ramp. Its physical effects spread across Serein's station shell, Neme's tides, two transfer habitats, Remainder's coolant reserve, and the pressure surfaces of Little Weight and Bent Morning. Current law assigned command to the Authority because it operated the Cantilevers. Current migration practice assigned Orison correction to whichever civic mind could physically shape the load. Neither structure gave every exposed body a decision.

Ivo tapped the propulsion rail. “Do not give my ship a vote. Give my crew one.”

“Your renewed consent conditions already bind the tug,” Ansa said.

“They bind this burn. You are designing eleven days.”

“Then the crew can renew by phase.”

“No. That turns thirty-two people into a switch everyone pressures before each deadline.” He brought up Sana's consent protocol. “Set limits now. If a phase removes every escape path, operational veto activates. If it stays inside agreed exposure and preserves rescue fuel, command executes without another performance of consent.”

A Neme controller adapted the rule for tide districts. Local authorities could stop a phase when measured water, foundation load, or atmosphere loss crossed published limits. They could not veto because of an unrelated political demand. Meridia proposed the same condition for hospital transfer capacity. Halcyon added school atmosphere reserve. Rook required a rescue floor for exterior mining crews. Vela attached tanker return mass.

The objections did not align. Some concerned immediate physical harm. Others concerned services failing hours later. Serein argued that accumulated correction debt counted even when no single ramp crossed a limit. Neme insisted evacuation delay belonged in the trigger, not only measured force. Meridia wanted a systemwide override for aggregate survival.

Olan's legal adviser entered at last. “Fourteen local stop authorities can produce paralysis at the exact moment synchronized execution becomes necessary.”

“So can one central command using false numbers,” Laleh said.

“The correction cannot pause safely at every point.”

Ansa marked each phase by reversibility. Seven allowed a safe hold. Four permitted only reduced continuation. Three created a branching choice with no neutral stop. At those points, any veto needed to specify an executable alternative inside pre-negotiated limits.

“Not an unlimited veto,” she said. “A bounded veto tied to bodies made vulnerable by that phase.”

Neme's controller rotated the authority layer. “Who decides which bodies qualify?”

“The provenance graph identifies modeled exposure,” Ansa said.

Ivo gave a quiet, humorless laugh. “Your graph identified my repairs after I sent them twice.”

The criticism landed correctly. A model could not grant recognition only to what it already knew how to count. Ansa added a claim procedure. Any group could enter evidence of exposure. The relevant phase could not proceed until the claim received a physical test or a public rejection with reasons. False claims would cost time. Unheard true claims had already cost lives.

Laleh wrote the provisional rule in ordinary language: authority followed the bodies made vulnerable by a correction, for as long as the vulnerability traveled with them.

The sentence detached jurisdiction from place. A tide district moving through a hazard retained authority after crossing an administrative boundary. A tug crew carried its limits into another region's orbit. A first-arc cluster acquired standing along its changing path. The Cantilevers could no longer convert distance from Meridia into distance from decision.

“Moving jurisdiction,” Neme's controller said.

Ansa tested the phrase against the map. Territory still mattered. Stations required local law, supply, and accountability. Yet corrections created temporary communities of consequence across fixed borders. Their authority began when the modeled or claimed exposure began. It ended only when the load passed or another body accepted it through an explicit transfer.

Meridia objected that no existing charter recognized such a unit. Laleh replied that no existing charter recognized a civic mind distributed across moon-scale sails either. The exchange did not settle the law. It clarified what new law would have to contain.

The shared clock pulsed beside them.

Kehr's bodies had altered formation. Little Weight moved into the proposed Neme lateral load. Bent Morning moved out. Harrow of Rain traced a slower arc that would protect first-arcs while raising risk to its oldest sail. Silt Crown held at the edge without accepting a role. Ember-Side repeated Harrow's prediction with a narrower error band.

Ansa assigned the trajectories to specified loads. “Kehr is not offering one collective guarantee.”

“Can we distinguish which polity commits?” Laleh asked.

“Not with legal confidence. We can distinguish bodies, timing, and correction behavior.”

A Near Turning witness cohort formed three agreement pulses around Harrow's arc. Long Recall bodies farther out withdrew from the reference. Narrow Mercy shelter pressure tightened around Silt Crown, neither taking the proposed load nor attacking the plan.

“Then record responsibility at the level we can witness,” Laleh said. “Body and trajectory first. Political attribution only where their own behavior sustains it.”

Ansa created Orison load covenants in the model. Each named the body carrying pressure, the first-arcs protected, the human infrastructure affected, the acceptable timing range, and the observed polity signals. No covenant treated Kehr as monarch. None interpreted Tull's silence as consent. If an Orison body altered course outside its agreed band, affected human regions gained a local response path. If humans changed Cantilever timing, Orison bodies could withdraw loads without that withdrawal being classified automatically as attack.

They tested reciprocity against a live correction too small to alter Neme's orbit. Cantilever 62 produced a brief phase rise while Harrow of Rain shaped pressure around an empty ballast canister. Neme had authority over foundation vibration. Serein monitored imported heat. Near Turning swarms carried the Orison witness record. The exercise used no inhabited load, but every control path matched the proposed framework.

At the second pulse, Serein's shell reading exceeded its local trigger by one percent. Its engineer entered a veto. Cantilever 62 stopped increasing and held at the pre-negotiated floor. Harrow continued pressure for four intervals, then withdrew inside the covenant band. The ballast canister drifted thirty meters from its predicted line.

Meridia's representative pointed to the miss. “Distributed stop authority degraded the correction.”

“It exposed a false shell assumption,” Laleh said.

The Serein engineer sent raw thermal images. A coolant shadow crossed one sensor and made the shell appear hotter than adjacent plates. Under central procedure, Authority control might have overridden the local stop as instrument error. The veto prevented that choice until evidence arrived. Inspection confirmed the shadow, but it also found a clogged coolant path beneath the plate. Both readings had carried part of the truth.

Ansa revised the shell response. Serein released the hold. The next pulse began four minutes late.

Harrow did not resume its original arc. Instead it shifted outward and asked the shared clock to accept a wider timing error. That change reduced pressure on the ballast while lowering strain on an old sail. Near Turning marked agreement. A Long Recall body withheld its witness surface. Narrow Mercy moved no mass.

Human operators accepted Harrow's revised load and increased Cantilever duration rather than amplitude. The canister came back toward the prediction line without erasing its thirty-meter deviation. Provenance preserved Serein's stop, the sensor error, the real coolant blockage, and Harrow's refusal of the first arc.

Ivo watched through Remainder's ranging feed. “Now put a maintenance crew in the hypothetical.”

Ansa added two suited workers beside Cantilever 62's coolant path. Under the original restart, they had six minutes to clear the plate. Harrow's revised timing gave them ten, but the longer duration increased their cumulative exposure. Their crew lead accepted only if a service pod remained attached throughout the pulse. That pod's mass required another correction to the ballast path.

The exercise accumulated conditions until Meridia called it unusable. Then the Neme controller compiled them into four executable checks: shell below trigger, service pod attached, Harrow inside its load band, and Cantilever duration below exposure limit. Any failed check stopped the pulse at a known hold point.

Complexity had not vanished. It had moved from one optimizer's hidden weights into conditions participants could inspect. The test consumed more time than central command would have allowed. It also finished without anyone surrendering authority over an unmodeled body.

Ivo studied the reciprocal clauses. “You still use responsibility as if every failure has an operator.”

“Give me the failure that doesn't.”

“Little Weight gets hurt holding a load. It withdraws three seconds early. A Neme elevator fails. Did the body violate, did our prediction miss, or did your clock tolerate too much error?”

Ansa opened all three branches. “We preserve uncertainty until evidence separates them.”

“And who bears the immediate consequence?”

“Neme does.”

“Then Neme needs a response before the inquiry finishes.”

The local controller entered a pre-authorized adaptation: elevator braking would draw from an atmosphere reserve if Orison pressure withdrew early. That protected foundations but shortened shelter support by eight minutes. Neme accepted the trade only if Meridia released portable life-support stacks before execution.

Meridia's representative refused. “Those stacks protect the model centers needed to coordinate decoupling.”

A new voice entered from Halcyon before Ansa could answer. “Your models do not breathe.”

Edrin appeared in the school fabrication hall. Children had been cleared from the visible floor, but their atmosphere lift status occupied one side of his feed. Three units ran on reserve bearings. A fourth had been stripped to keep the others operational.

“The allocation preserves computational continuity,” Ansa said. She heard the defense and disliked its familiar shape.

“It preserves a forecast about whether our lifts will fail while denying us equipment that keeps them running.”

“Halcyon's minimum reserve remains inside the plan.”

“On aggregate. You count school power after transfer loads stabilize.” Edrin entered his local schedule. “Students move before the first decoupling. Four thousand eight hundred need lift support across rotating sectors. If power drops during movement, your later reserve does not retrieve them from stalled cars.”

Ansa overlaid his data. The Open Chord branch protected Aphelion computation, Meridian coordination, and three jurisdictional model mirrors through every decoupling phase. School atmosphere lifts entered guaranteed service only after each local transfer window. She had preserved the machinery that described changing vulnerability before preserving the machinery carrying vulnerable bodies.

Edrin let the numbers remain between them. He did not mention their childhood shelter map.

Olan's adviser said, “Cutting model-center protection increases forecast error systemwide.”

“Yes,” Ansa said.

Silence followed across channels that received her answer at different times.

She reduced Aphelion's dedicated power floor. One model mirror on Meridia lost continuous service. Serein's archive took a delayed update rather than a live stream. The released power and portable stacks went to Halcyon's school lifts, Neme's emergency shelters, and two Rook rescue hubs. Model latency increased. Three correction windows widened their uncertainty.

The shared clock responded by narrowing its accepted band around one Orison load.

“We lose trust by becoming less precise?” Meridia asked.

“We lose tolerated error because our timing gets worse,” Ansa said. “That makes the correction harder. It does not make the allocation wrong.”

Edrin checked the revised lift schedule. “Unit Four still has no bearing reserve.”

“Can local fabrication make one?”

“In thirty hours if we receive raw ceramic mass.”

Vela offered a shipment. Rook had the launch window but wanted fuel credit. Laleh tied the exchange into Serein's unused reserve allotment. Four jurisdictions altered resources for a school lift none of them governed. The authority layer followed the affected students through each transfer sector.

Ansa watched the branch settle into a less efficient, more survivable shape.

The public session had continued for nine hours when Laleh asked the question everyone had been postponing. “Senior Cartographer, does this plan save Pelagos?”

Ansa looked at the complete model. Twelve Cantilevers had to decouple. Neme still needed the remaining distance inside its eleven-day shift. Local vetoes could change timing. Orison bodies could refuse loads or fail while carrying them. The Pale Interval would arrive on SD94 regardless of law. Every probability depended on cooperation no authority could compel without destroying its premise.

The old professional answer would have named a confidence range, then placed uncertainty in a footnote. The public channels showed tide workers, school coordinators, Assembly offices, tug crew, and dark Orison trajectories beyond Neme. None needed reassurance purchased by omission.

“No,” she said. “I cannot guarantee success.”

Meridia's representative leaned toward his camera. “Then you ask fourteen regions to surrender power and transit on an unverified alien promise.”

“I ask us to develop a sequence where every promise has a witnessed load and every exposed jurisdiction has a defined refusal. Locked continuation also lacks a guarantee. Its failure arrives with the Pale Interval, trapped first-arcs, and growing correction debt.”

“Give the public your survival percentage.”

“We do not have one that survives current Orison uncertainty. Publishing a single figure would imply one stable actor and one command path. Neither exists.”

Ivo shifted his bandaged hand on the rail. “Say what we do know.”

Ansa marked the measured facts. The cumulative wake came from human phase traffic. Full locking compressed the migration and increased local structural heat. Abrupt decoupling would collapse settlements. Staged loosening allowed local reserves to take load but created severe shortages. Orison pressure had corrected Neme when the shared clock held. Kehr's polity remained divided. Tull had not accepted the corridor design. Vaul proved both human harm and Orison abandonment.

“What we know does not absolve either side,” she said. “It gives us enough causality to build reciprocal conditions.”

Laleh called for an indication of support, not a lawful authorization. The distinction entered the record. Jurisdictions were being asked whether to continue developing the Open Chord under the moving-jurisdiction framework. No Cantilever control would transfer. No final loads would bind. Dissent remained available in later negotiation.

Responses arrived according to light lag.

Neme supported development after its tide limits entered the veto layer. Serein joined once correction debt remained locally releasable. Halcyon supported after the school lift revision. Rook accepted the rescue floors. Vela attached tanker return mass and joined. Four smaller regions entered support with separate reserve conditions.

Meridia opposed. Two inner habitats followed, citing unlawful fragmentation of Authority command and unbounded exposure to Orison withdrawal.

The remaining channels held open. One outer settlement wanted proof that Long Recall would not obstruct. A manufacturing habitat needed a revised ration model before choosing. They entered undecided rather than letting delay masquerade as assent.

Laleh waited until every timestamp cleared its local lag. Then she fixed the count into the public record.

Nine jurisdictions supported developing the Open Chord. Three opposed. Two remained undecided.

Beside Ansa, Ren's scorched clock widened around the imperfect coalition and kept the future path open.

Chapter 21 — The Renewal

The authority clock reached zero while three delegates were still arguing over the meaning of delay.

Olan Brace watched the final digit vanish above the Assembly floor. Behind each of the fourteen desks, live allocation columns climbed or narrowed as local controllers spent reserves they had expected the phase lattice to replace.
