Halcyon's bar pulsed amber around its atmosphere lifts. Neme showed a tide-pump deficit. Vela had begun taking fabrication lines off the shared schedule to preserve local air production.

No chime marked the end of his emergency order. The Cantilevers did not release themselves. Seventy-two hours earlier, Olan had instructed Control to require an affirmative command before any locked station could resume local phase. That precaution now gave his expired authority physical momentum.

“Director Brace.” The presiding clerk spoke without looking at him. “Do you confirm that the original declaration has elapsed?”

“I confirm the time.”

Laleh Sorn stood at the Serein desk. Fatigue had roughened her voice, but not her diction. “The question concerns authority, not clocks.”

Across the causeway, a power shortage interrupted the public gallery's cooling fans. Olan heard their bearings slow in sequence. People lifted paper agendas to move the warm air. The chamber remained visible to every jurisdiction with enough reserve to receive the feed.

The clerk tried again. “Do you confirm expiration of the emergency phase lock?”

A lawful answer had only one shape. Yes, followed by release instructions to Cantilevers 61 through 72. The outer stations would then resume local control under rules written for ordinary load balancing. Nine jurisdictions supported developing Ansa Meren's Open Chord, yet no one had adopted its control charter. Three opposed any coordinated loosening. Two had not settled their own delegation mandates. If the stations decoupled now, each would respond to local heat and debt. Their separate safety routines could pull Neme's corrected orbit in twelve incompatible directions.

Olan touched the authorization pane set into his desk. “I confirm that the original period has elapsed. I am filing a renewal under Tern precedent, effective without interruption.”

The chamber's allocation columns held steady. For one breath, nothing else did.

Laleh put both palms on her desk. “Tern precedent applies when delegated responders cannot communicate.”

“Pelagos lacks an executable decision.”

“We are communicating with you now.”

“Communication and decision differ in law.”

“So do inability and refusal.”

The clerk's pane lit with objections before she could recognize speakers. Outer delegates demanded suspension. Meridia's civil reserve office requested immediate confirmation that transfer priority would continue. Neme asked whether a renewed lock preserved Authority liability for tide damage. Someone in the gallery called Olan a thief. Another voice demanded that the lock remain until the Orison migration withdrew.

Olan entered his seal. The renewal moved from his pane into the Authority causeway network, then outward through Cantilever Control. Eleven station acknowledgments arrived. Cantilever 67 required a second path because its civil relay had lost power. Cantilever 71 reported structural heat at eighty-one percent of its licensed ceiling. The lock remained.

He had prevented one uncontrolled transition. He had also used a rule for silence against a chamber full of voices.

The presiding clerk read the filing title aloud. Her discipline made each word sound more serious, not less. “Emergency continuity renewal under Authority Charter Section Twelve, incorporating the findings of the Tern Cascade Commission.”

At the mention of Tern, Olan's right hand tightened around the edge of the desk. He released it before any camera could make grief into commentary.

Pell had died thirty-one years ago in a transfer concourse with power available on both sides of a jurisdictional boundary. Local controllers waited for confirmation that never came. Each later inquiry described the failure through verbs that lacked subjects: messages were delayed, authority became unclear, reserve transfer did not occur. Olan had spent his career putting names beside such verbs. Now his own name stood beside a command that extended beyond its lawful purpose.

Laleh raised a procedural token. “I file a public challenge to the renewal, on behalf of Serein and any jurisdiction that joins before adjudication. I ask the clerk to append our live objection to every station copy.”

The clerk looked to Olan. Under the old practice, an emergency order traveled cleanly while challenges waited in a separate docket. Olan could preserve that distinction for another hour.

“Append it,” he said.

Laleh's expression did not soften. “You do not earn consent by documenting its absence.”

“No.”

“Release the lock.”

“I will not.”

The answer quieted the nearest desks. Olan preferred their anger to the false comfort of procedural ambiguity. He transmitted the full renewal text, including the challenge, and watched twelve acknowledgments become fourteen.

Before the clerk could call the next speaker, the Neme delegate requested a direct load ruling. His city's western retaining plates had taken permanent distortion during the second correction, and the renewed lock required them to absorb another scheduled tide peak without Cantilever relief. He placed three engineering seals in the chamber record. The first permitted the load for eleven hours. The second permitted it for seventeen if evacuation lifts received priority power. The third refused it entirely because no one had inspected the foundations under the drowned south district.

“Which seal governs?” he asked.

Under Authority practice, Olan's engineers would choose the most conservative verified case, then issue a uniform restriction. That method protected Control from optimistic local estimates. It also meant the people standing above the foundations could only supply evidence, never decide what uncertainty they would bear.

“The third seal holds for the south district,” Olan said. “Local tide control may narrow the restriction after inspection. The other districts remain under their certified limits.”

The delegate looked almost offended by the concession. “You claim power to renew against us, then offer a local boundary inside your order.”

“Your foundations do not become mine because I signed the lock.”

“The load does.”

“The liability does as well.”

Laleh turned from her desk. “Put that sentence in the order.”

Olan added it to the interpretive schedule. The clerk marked the amendment as contested because an emergency filing ordinarily could not change after activation. Each useful accommodation made the renewal less defensible as emergency continuity and more obvious as legislation by one office.

Three further requests followed. Rook demanded a floor for hospital transfer power. Vela required local custody of its atmosphere buffer. Halcyon asked for manual authority over school seals during any phase fluctuation. Olan granted the first two within fixed limits. Halcyon's request implicated eleven shared relays, so he sent it to immediate technical review rather than pretend a simple signature could make it safe.

By the time the clerk suspended questions, the renewal contained four local exceptions and nine unresolved challenges. It no longer resembled the single clean command Olan had built his office to issue. It resembled the actual system.

Beyond the chamber, the new order began altering lives. A Rook hospital lost a scheduled gate shipment of superconducting pump bearings. Serein extended industrial darkness by six hours. The migration display showed first-arcs compressing behind the locked corridor, their small magnetic forms occupying less room than Ansa's safety model allowed. Each update entered the legal record as consequence. None revoked his hand from the seal.

The Assembly adjourned for emergency caucus before noon. Olan crossed the public causeway without security. The basalt under its clear floor held heat from Meridia's day, while the Authority tower ahead kept its interior cold enough for control hardware. Citizens filled both rails. Some held Neme casualty counts. Others carried signs naming settlements that would fail if outer power loosened. A woman in a Cantilever maintenance coat asked him whether her crew at 71 had permission to evacuate if structural heat reached the red line.

“Yes,” Olan said.

“Control told them release required your office.”

“Evacuation does not.”

“They asked for that in writing nine hours ago.”

He opened his public pane and issued the clarification under her witness identity. It cost forty seconds. It should have cost none.

Farther along, two students from Meridia held a hand-painted model of the phase lattice. Their seventy-two silver threads converged on one black hub. The model misunderstood the network. No single hub carried all control. Yet Olan recognized the political truth they had built: one person currently prevented twelve threads from moving.

Inside the Authority tower, his deputies had assembled in the response room. The wall displayed six projected failures, each generated under different assumptions. Without the lock, the worst branch put Cantilever 64 into a local protective phase that would tug against Neme. With the lock, Cantilever 71 crossed heat limits in seventeen hours unless Meridia reduced transfer draw. A partial release might open room for the Orison first-arcs while preserving human power, but no current key architecture could assign its correction debt according to the bounded vetoes promised in the Open Chord.

Deputy Director Aven Tor had removed his jacket. “The chamber feed makes it sound as if we possess a stable locked option.”

“We possess a slower failure.”

“Then state that publicly.”

“I did in the renewal findings.”

“You filed forty-three pages.”

Olan looked at the public summary drafted by communications staff. Its first line read: CONTINUITY OF ESSENTIAL SERVICES SECURED. He deleted the line.

“Replace it with measured consequences,” he said. “Heat margins by station. Reserve losses by jurisdiction. First-arc compression projections from Meren's model, with her confidence bounds intact.”

Aven studied him. “That will strengthen Sorn's challenge.”

“The challenge needs facts whether it strengthens or fails.”

“Do you intend to survive adjudication?”

The question referred to office, not body, but Olan's mind supplied Pell's name before he could stop it. “I intend the stations to survive it.”

Aven did not accept the substitution. “Your renewal theory depends on treating political deadlock as communications failure. No serious court will let that stand.”

“I know.”

“Then why give them your complete reasoning?”

“Because concealment cannot improve the theory.”

“Neither can candor.”

Olan sent the summary back for publication. “Candor improves the record used after we lose.”

They spent two hours reducing load on Cantilever 71. Meridia gave up three priority transfer banks. Vela released a stored phase buffer after receiving a written floor for atmosphere production. Neme accepted a tide-pump interruption only after local engineers gained direct access to the correction telemetry. Every bargain weakened central control and extended the lock it opposed. Pelagos had begun governing through contradictions its statutes did not name.

During the fourth hour, a witness-swarm projection reached the response room through Ansa's shared clock network. Small bodies moved around the blocked corridor in timed groups. Their pressure changes predicted one first-arc's failure within nine hours. The Orison had supplied no demand, only a measurable outcome if human geometry held.

“Can we loosen 69 for the predicted passage?” Aven asked.

“Not under the current lock architecture without releasing its dependent pair.”

“Then permit local manual phase.”

“That creates an unmodeled Neme load.”

Aven struck the table once with two fingers. “We cannot keep naming what each choice creates and call that decision.”

Olan enlarged the prediction. A first-arc appeared as three bent lines crossing a tolerance band. No human display could show whether it feared, understood, or merely failed. The clock translated its vulnerability into a trajectory because trajectory supplied the only shared evidence available.

“Ask Neme local control for a load boundary,” Olan said. “Ask Meren for the shortest safe window. If both concur, I will authorize a temporary phase relief.”

“That gives two local actors a veto over your emergency order.”

“It gives affected bodies authority over a bounded consequence.”

Aven's eyebrows rose. “You sound like Sorn.”

“I sound like the model.”

Ansa Meren joined from Aphelion before Neme's answer arrived. Transmission delay split the exchange into recorded blocks. Her first block displayed three release windows, none wider than seven minutes. Her second removed the safest-looking window because a witness swarm had crossed the modeled volume since calculation began.

“Do not treat the remaining windows as permissions,” she said from the wall. “They describe loads my model can bound. Neme still owns the foundation risk. Kehr's bodies still decide whether the first-arc can tolerate our timing.”

Olan recorded his response. “We lack a semantic channel for Orison consent.”

“We have witnessed predictions. Offer both windows through the shared clock. If they shape toward one, we have evidence of preference.”

“Evidence, not authorization.”

“That distinction has protected your office better than it protected them.”

He almost ended the exchange. Instead he asked what changed if the Orison chose the later window.

Ansa's next block arrived with no rebuke in it. The later release shifted correction debt toward Cantilever 70 and cut Neme's western tide margin by four percent. It also gave two first-arcs room to unfold before crossing. She included the raw model, local amendments, and the confidence range contributed by every jurisdiction. Olan could audit its history but could not reduce it to one author's guarantee.

“Your provenance record identifies every change,” he said when his reply channel opened. “It does not identify one accountable decision maker.”

“No. It identifies everyone whose decision changes the outcome.”

The statement lodged beside Pell's old arguments without resolving them. Distributed authorship could expose responsibility or dissolve it. Tern had taught Olan how quickly local good faith became collective death. Yet the current lock concentrated responsibility so completely that every unavailable choice led back to his desk.

He approved transmission of both windows. The shared clock rendered them as pressure tolerances, not demands. Orison bodies altered their spacing toward the later path. Neme's boundary had still not arrived.

Yet the choice came too late. Neme could not certify the load before light lag consumed the safe interval. The predicted first-arc folded inward nine minutes ahead of Ansa's midpoint estimate. Two adult Orison bodies caught its pressure and carried it away from the hard edge, compressing the other juveniles around them. The first-arc survived. The migration paid for Olan's delay with capacity no Authority ledger could price.

At shift change he left the response room and descended beneath the tower. The memorial archive occupied a former relay vault, where obsolete control links had once entered Meridia through armored channels. Families could store testimony, work records, and private artifacts from infrastructure failures. Access required no office. Olan entered as Pell's registered husband, a category the archive had not updated because death made legal states durable.

Cold shelving extended beyond the light. He requested TERN CASCADE: CALDER PELL, CIVIL SYSTEMS MEDIATOR. The archive delivered a narrow case from a wall of thousands.

Olan had not opened it in six years. Public inquiries quoted Pell's final relay transcript, which showed him requesting cross-boundary reserve transfer until atmosphere alarms drowned his voice. The case held more ordinary things: accreditation records, a repair claim for a scorched kitchen wall, three years of transit receipts, a recording of Pell trying to teach Olan a tide-city dance in their first apartment.

He chose the last item and stopped it after four seconds.

Pell stood barefoot inside the frame, one hand raised to correct Olan's balance. “You keep trying to lead a step that changes leaders.”

The younger Olan moved out of view. His answer came from the room's speaker shadow. “Then the step lacks accountable design.”

Pell laughed. The sound hurt less than the ease behind it.

Olan opened the private materials from the commission years. Pell had annotated draft emergency rules in a patient blue hand. Olan remembered the arguments about response clocks, delegated power, and who carried liability when a local controller acted before central confirmation. He did not remember this sentence until it appeared on the archive glass:

If every safe action needs one distant signature, delay has not been solved. It has acquired a face.

Olan read the line twice. The Tern statute adopted after Pell's death allowed any reachable responder to act when communication failed. Olan had spent three decades teaching the Authority to coordinate those responders, standardize evidence, and preserve a named chain of responsibility. Over time, coordination hardened into permission. Permission condensed into his seal.

Grief had not deceived him. Tern had killed people through fragmented delay. Central standards had prevented other deaths, and Pell would have recognized that. The corruption lay elsewhere. Olan had treated one catastrophe as a complete model of danger. Every later uncertainty entered through its terms.

His pane flashed with Laleh's filing. Seven jurisdictions had joined the public challenge. Halcyon had not, though Edrin Meren submitted an affidavit demanding local atmosphere authority during any decoupling. Neme requested accelerated adjudication. Meridia defended the renewal while objecting to reserve cuts imposed for Cantilever 71.

Olan copied Pell's sentence into no official file. Its authority came from private candor, not foresight. He closed the annotation and returned the case to storage.

Aven waited outside the archive. “The removal petition has enough sponsors to reach the chamber tomorrow.”

“Tomorrow may be generous.”

“Security wants to know whether an interim director should receive provisional Cantilever keys.”

Olan began walking toward the lift. “Yes. Prepare three packages. One for you, one for the Assembly clerk, and one distributed among the outer station controllers.”

Aven followed. “A distributed package cannot activate without a new protocol.”

“Use Meren's provenance method. Every key fragment carries its conditions and every alteration remains visible.”

“You are building the council Sorn asked for while refusing its plan.”

“I am building continuity for my removal.”

The lift doors opened. Inside, a live status strip showed all twelve outer locks in white. Cantilever 71 had fallen to seventy-six percent heat after the Meridia cuts. First-arc compression continued beyond the survey boundary. No immediate indicator demanded a new command, which meant his existing command kept acting.

Aven held the door. “If the packages work, do you release tonight?”

Olan entered the lift and selected Control.

“No,” he said. “Publish the succession conditions. Keep the lock.”

The doors closed on the archive level. Twelve white marks traveled upward beside him, held in place by an order he had prepared others to survive without obeying forever.

Chapter 22 — Neme Beneath the Tides

The anchor line came out of the water with a school roof attached to it.

Ivo Calder killed the winch before the twisted metal reached the repair barge. The line snapped taut between the deck drum and the drowned district below, streaming black water across the yellow armor of his boots. Three local riggers leaned over the rail. One crossed two fingers against bad engineering and looked toward Ivo as if a tug captain ought to have authority over submerged buildings.

“Roof panel,” Ivo said. “Not the whole roof.”

The rigger kept his fingers crossed. “That helps.”

“It helps the winch.”

He locked the drum and lowered a crawler down the line. Its camera found the panel wrapped around the anchor fork thirty meters below. A faded lesson schedule remained visible under silt. The second correction had moved Neme only a fraction of the distance required for the corridor, yet the tide cities had taken the change through every foundation and gate. Water had entered districts built above the old surge marks. Elevators hung two centimeters out of true. People now slept beside sealed bags because the moon's next safe waterline depended on burns not yet authorized.

Ivo guided the crawler's cutting jaw around the panel. His left glove shook when the tool met resistance. He shifted control to his right hand before anyone could offer help.

The barge master, Teren Vos, watched the tool feed. “That building held eleven hundred students.”

“How many were inside?”

“None. Correction alarms cleared it six hours before the surge.”

A clean answer, followed by the fact Ivo had learned to wait for.

Teren rubbed salt from his beard. “They moved into fabrication halls. No desks, half atmosphere load, and three sanitation blocks for all of them. Their families call that saved because the alternative has a casualty count.”

The crawler cut through the last fold. Ivo paid out line until the roof panel sank away from the anchor fork. A local diver drone caught it and towed it toward the salvage grid.

“Bring tension to twelve percent,” he said.

The riggers worked the manual capstans. Neme's three tide cities had lost enough automated equipment that every available machine drew a queue. People supplied the missing control loops with shoulders, calluses, and shouted numbers. Along the northern seawall, crews restrung tide gauges from building to building. Farther inland, portable atmosphere trunks ran through open windows because foundation shifts had broken sealed distribution ducts.

Above them, Remainder crossed the daylight sky once every ninety-three minutes. Its scarred radiators remained folded to protect the damaged port side. The ship could still move mass. It could no longer pretend movement came cheaply.

Ivo brought the anchor line to working load. The deck plates trembled under his boots.

“Six point two meganewtons,” a rigger called.

“Hold it there.”

Teren checked the new tide prediction on his wrist pane. “Your Array people give us a surge arrival at fourteen ten. Local basin model says fourteen twenty-four.”

“Which one owns the water?”

“Neither, if they miss.”

A shimmer crossed the bay beyond the barge. At first Ivo mistook it for sun on spray. Then hundreds of Orison witness swarms rose through the magnetic shelter maintained over Neme. Each small body held less mass than the crawler, but their formation extended across kilometers. They changed spacing in measured pulses.

The tide crews stopped pulling.

One of the younger riggers backed away from the rail. Defense fire had killed twenty-three first-arcs forty days earlier. Neme's second correction had killed 1,140 civilians across dependent stations and Ren Adek beside the shared clock relay. No one standing on the barge possessed a reason to treat unfamiliar motion as harmless.

Ivo opened the portable clock interface mounted beside the winch. Ren's last hardware occupied a shock case wrapped in fresh coolant lines. The living magnetic pattern inside it responded to the witness formation with three tolerance bands.

“Prediction,” Ivo said. “They're offering a prediction.”

Teren did not lower his shoulders. “Of what?”

The witness swarms descended in sequence. Seven touched the bay's magnetic surface and climbed. Eleven followed. A third group crossed the seawall sensor plane, then curled back toward the old high-water marks.

Ivo fed their intervals to the local tide board. The board translated motion into a pressure range. Surge arrival: fourteen sixteen, plus or minus forty seconds. Height: three point eight meters above the revised datum. The result fell between the Array and basin forecasts but agreed with neither.

“Useful trick,” Teren said.

“Test it before you call it useful.”

They waited with the line under partial load. The tide cities continued working around their pause. Barges carried drinking water over streets that had become canals. A funeral launch moved along the far wall with no music, only a list of station names displayed on its canopy. At fourteen fifteen and thirty-eight seconds, the bay drew back hard enough to expose the foundation skirts. The surge struck forty-four seconds later.

Water climbed to within five centimeters of the Orison prediction.

Teren looked from his board to the swarms. “Can they do the southern basin?”

Ivo sent the request as two witnessed waterlines and an uncertainty interval. The swarms divided. Half traveled south. The rest stayed above the barge, correcting their own formation against gusts no human forecast had included.

By the end of the shift, local crews had accepted four Orison tide predictions. They did not call the swarms allies. They assigned them a channel number, compared their errors, and moved two pump crews before the water arrived. Trust among working people began as a tool allowed near the machinery.

An old basin inspector named Seya refused the fifth forecast. She brought Ivo to a flood stair where brass marks recorded ninety years of high water. The swarms predicted a low reflection there. Seya's hand gauge showed pressure building inside a drainage culvert, enough to reverse the local result.

“Your visitors see the bay,” she said. “They don't see a blocked pipe under my feet.”

Ivo transmitted the contradiction through the shared clock: predicted waterline, observed culvert pressure, tolerance exceeded. The swarms broke formation. A dozen descended until the stair lights flickered around their magnetic fields. They traced the culvert's buried course from the seawall to an obstruction beneath a collapsed market berth.

Seya watched their path, then sent a crawler into the pipe. It found two cargo shutters jammed across the channel. Her crews cleared one before the reflection arrived. Water climbed thirty centimeters below the revised mark.

“They corrected,” Teren said.

“They accepted a measurement,” Seya answered. “Keep the distinction.”

She logged the failed forecast under the same channel as the successful ones. The witness swarms remained above the stair until her record transmitted, holding their positions against the city's shifting magnetic noise. Ivo understood the exchange without needing a label for apology. They had offered knowledge, received a local fact, and changed what they did.

The Vela atmosphere team landed after dusk on a flat cargo carrier with patched heat tiles. Ivo had gone to the dock to sign for sealant membranes. He saw the team's blue pressure cases first, then six technicians in gray work skins passing them hand to hand. The seventh technician turned sideways to clear the hatch.

Min Calder had cut her hair close enough for a suit seal. A tool harness crossed her chest. Her old scholarship patch remained sewn on one shoulder, though the phase-gate academy it named had no open route from Vela.

Ivo stopped with the manifest pane in his hand.

Min handed a case to the dock loader. “If you drop that, the western school gets to breathe canal vapor.”

The loader adjusted his grip. “You the inspector?”

“Apprentice.”

“You sound expensive for an apprentice.”

“Dropping it costs more.”

Only then did she look at Ivo. “Hello, Dad.”

He checked the carrier behind her as if a proper parent might still emerge. “Who cleared you for Neme?”

“My team lead.”

“Who cleared your team lead?”

“Vela civil atmosphere. Neme requested seal crews.”

“You are seventeen.”

“Still true after landing.”

Her team lead, a square-faced woman named Par Tal, approached with a custody slate. “Calder, if this becomes family leave, say so before I assign her to the south district.”

“It does not,” Min said.

Ivo had no command over the dock, the atmosphere team, or his daughter. The knowledge arrived as three separate irritations.

“The south district foundations have not passed inspection,” he said.

Par tilted the slate toward him. “We work above the third seal line. Local structures signed the route.”

“Conditions changed during the last surge.”

“They change every surge.”

Min took the membrane manifest from his hand and verified the case count. “You can brief us on a real hazard. You don't get to convert being worried into jurisdiction.”

“Do you practice these lines on the transport?”

“No. Vela gave us actual work.”

Par signed receipt and sent the other technicians toward a waiting crawler. Min remained long enough to claim two personal filters from the carrier locker.

Ivo lowered his voice. “You could have told me.”

“I did. The message reached Remainder yesterday.”

“The ship lost two civil channels during the correction.”

“I know. Sana acknowledged it.”

That left no equipment to blame. “She did not tell me.”

“I asked her not to until our landing clearance became final.”

Ivo looked up at Remainder's moving light. Sana had granted Min control over information concerning Min's own arrival. The act resembled disloyalty only from the center of his habit.

“You should be on Vela,” he said.

“Why?”

“Because Neme remains inside a correction field. Because another full burn could alter every seal on this moon. Because the Narrow Mercy already shoved it once.”

“Vela remains attached to the same system.”

“It isn't under the tide.”

“No. It has atmosphere plants losing replacement membranes because the gates are locked. We sent what we could spare. I came because I know the stock, and because my apprenticeship covers emergency seals.”

“Your apprenticeship began six months ago.”

“Seven.”

“That improves nothing.”

Min clipped the filters to her harness. “You crossed a military boundary with thirty-two people and a cracked fuel spine. Did your years make that safe?”

“They let me measure what would fail.”

“Then measure us. Don't erase us.”

The dock loader passed between them carrying the last membrane case. Ivo noticed the case's pressure indicator had dropped two green segments. He caught the loader's sleeve and pointed. They lowered it onto a cradle. Min knelt, opened the service strip, and found a transit valve seated crooked against its gasket.

“Carrier vibration,” she said.

“Or loading shock.”

“You would like it to be loading shock.”

“It gives me someone to complain to.”

Her mouth moved toward a smile, then decided against it. Together they changed the gasket. Min's hands knew the Vela case pattern better than his. She kept her fingertips clear of the spring collar without needing a warning.

When pressure climbed, Par called from the crawler. Min stood.

“South district lodging has one local room left,” Ivo said. “Take a berth on Remainder.”

“No.”

“The ship has certified seals.”

“It also has you, which would turn every meal into a safety review.”

“I can restrain myself through one meal.”

“Not convincingly.” She lifted the repaired case with the loader. “We sleep where the local crews sleep. If their atmosphere becomes unsafe, ours should not stay invisible aboard your ship.”

“That sentence belongs on a committee plaque.”

“It belongs on a work order.”

She joined the crawler. Ivo watched it move toward the drowned district until a cargo stack blocked his view.

Sana called him from orbit. “You can yell now or after ascent.”

“After.”

“Efficient.”

“Put Min's message in my personal queue, retroactive priority.”

“It was there. You haven't opened that queue since Ren died.”

He looked at the unread marker on his pane. Forty-one messages waited behind it. Crew families, Authority queries, a condolence from a Neme relay worker, three notices from Min. Ivo had kept the ship moving by treating private traffic as optional load.

“I'll clear it tonight,” he said.

“Start with the living.”

He returned to the barge instead. The repaired anchor had held through two surges, but the southern line needed a new fork. Witness swarms had predicted a cross-basin reflection the local model missed. Teren accepted the forecast and changed the repair sequence, then required the swarms to repeat it against a second waterline before committing crews.

At the south district, Min's team worked from a school fabrication hall. Children had marked sleeping areas between idle printers with colored cable. Every atmosphere trunk carried a paper tag listing the rooms it served, the people requiring enriched mix, and the time remaining on its filter. No central board contained the whole arrangement. A teacher walked the floor gathering changes and photographing each tag for the district record.

Ivo found Min inside a classroom whose outer seal had warped. She held a tension brace while Par injected membrane foam around the frame. Six children waited in the corridor with breathing hoods hanging loose on their chests.

“Brace reads point seven left,” Min said.

Ivo checked the frame. “Floor shifted under the hinge.”

“We know.”

“You need a jack under the inner corner.”

Par jerked her chin toward the equipment rack. “Both jacks went to the clinic.”

Ivo studied the ceiling rail. “Use the cargo strap as a diagonal pull.”

Min passed him the strap. They rigged it from the rail to the frame, tightening by hand until the brace centered. Foam expanded into the corrected gap. The children watched without speaking.

A boy near the door asked Ivo whether the next moon move would flood the hall.

“Not if the anchors hold,” he said.

Min glanced at him. “And if the burn stays inside the local tide limit.”

“Yes,” Ivo added. “Both have to happen.”

The boy considered the answer. “Who decides the limit?”

“People here measure what the foundations can take,” Min said. “Other crews measure the corridor. They have to agree before the burn.”

Ivo could have described emergency exceptions, Authority keys, and the gap between agreement and control. None would improve the child's air. He tightened the strap one more notch.

After the seal passed pressure, Min followed him into the hall. “You left out the burn condition.”

“I added it.”

“Because I made you.”

“Yes.”

The admission slowed her anger. She leaned against an idle printer, exhaustion plain around her eyes.

“I don't need you to approve every place I can be hurt,” she said. “I need you to tell the truth about the machinery when I get there.”

“I know what uncontrolled corrections do to ships.”

“You also know what atmosphere failure does to Vela. You never ask me for that model before you tell me to stay home.”

Ivo looked across the hall. Families had made shelves from printer feed trays. A local tide map covered the wall where student work once hung. Beside it, someone had drawn the witness swarm intervals in blue chalk.

“What do you need from Remainder?” he asked.

“Portable compressor couplings. Four if you have them. A pressure tech to show us whether your emergency tanks can feed this fitting.”

“That will reduce ship reserve.”

“How much?”

He opened the inventory rather than saying too much. “Four couplings leave us six. Tank feed for a two-hour test costs point zero three percent of reserve gas.”

“Can you spare it?”

“Yes.”

“Then let us sign custody.”

They did. Min did not thank him for allowing work she already had authority to perform. She signed her name, accepted the load, and called one of her teammates to carry it.

Ivo returned to orbit at second dark. Remainder's air smelled of hot insulation and the citrus solvent used on damaged coolant lines. Sana met him at the rail console with propulsion chief Meret and a complete mass audit.

“Port spine remains isolated,” Meret said. “No additional leak, no useful flow. Two starboard modules can feed the main cups. Center spine passes pressure but its mount has propagated cracks across frame fourteen.”

Ivo enlarged the scan. The cracks formed pale branches around bolts his crew had replaced before Min learned to walk.

“Remaining correction mass?” he asked.

“Eighteen point six percent of rated load,” Meret said. “Three point one stays reserved for attitude, undock, and crew separation. A full Open Chord profile consumes fifteen point five if Ansa's timing holds.”

“No margin.”

“Measurement margin, yes. Operational margin, no.”

Sana placed the crew consent record beside the audit. “And the hull?”

Meret changed views. Neme's last burn had bent the tug along its port truss. Another maximum cup load would drive frame fourteen beyond elastic tolerance. The computer offered a survival range because computers had no category for a ship held together long enough to finish dying.

“Could we split the burn?” Ivo asked.

“Not without replenishment or a second tug. No replenishment route exists. Every other heavy tug has a jurisdictional load assignment.”

“Strip correction mass from the landing reserves.”

“That moves the arithmetic, not the frame.”

Below them, Neme's tide cities turned through darkness. Repair barges marked the seawalls in amber. Tiny blue traces from witness swarms crossed their lights, carrying another tide prediction toward local crews.

Ivo opened the maintenance card for frame fourteen. Sixty years of inspections filled the record. The ship had never promised permanence. Only service within stated limits.

He entered the new limit in terms no office could round away: Remainder retained fuel for one final full correction and crew separation. Maximum-profile execution would make the hull unrecoverable. No second maximum burn available.

Sana added her seal. Meret added hers. The card propagated to every crew station and to the local Neme command that shared their operational veto.

On the external camera, Remainder's cracked truss crossed the moon's bright limb. Ivo watched the damage rotate into sunlight, no longer hidden by shadow and no longer described as repairable.

Chapter 23 — Halcyon in Darkness

Halcyon lost its water lifts four seconds before the model said it would lose anything.

Ansa Meren saw the pressure traces flatten across six sectors, each line dropping with the clean vertical edge of a severed wire. In Cartography Bay Four, the planned partial decoupling still unfolded inside its green tolerance corridor. Cantilever 66 reduced phase coherence by two percent. Cantilever 67 carried the released load. Asteron's corridor widened by the predicted margin.

On Halcyon Ring, sixty-one million people entered a power failure the plan had not predicted.

“Stop the sequence,” Ren's successor called from the clock station.

“Sequence stopped,” Ansa said. “Do not re-lock.”

She froze the next decoupling command without reversing the two already completed. The distinction cost time to explain, and every second now had an atmosphere value. Halcyon's rotation kept the habitat's apparent gravity stable. Its water, air, lifts, seals, and emergency pumps depended on distributed buses that should have separated cleanly from phase power. Instead demand vanished from Ansa's display in expanding patches.

Her brother's priority channel opened with no image.

“School control to Aphelion,” Edrin said. Voices moved behind him, counting children and door states. “We have general dark in education sectors three through nine. Local cells failed to start. Atmosphere seals cycling on stored pressure.”

“Halcyon control reports a bus cascade.”

“Halcyon control reports from a room with power.”

Ansa pulled the raw telemetry beneath the civil summary. Seven fabrication cells had received the start command. None had synchronized. The model had assumed each cell kept an independent timing crystal. Maintenance records showed three recent replacements but no shared fault.

“How long on classroom seals?” she asked.

“Twenty-six minutes at required margin. Less in old rooms. We initiated school drills before the lights finished dropping.”

Across the channel, Edrin called a color and another voice repeated it farther away. Distributed school drills did not gather children in one central shelter. Every classroom sealed locally, then moved according to its own air, water, and access state. The method had grown from outer settlements where corridors could lose pressure faster than a central office learned which route remained open.

Ansa had modeled those drills as demand reduction.

She opened his live school map. It looked nothing like an Authority evacuation plan. Hand-entered arrows crossed official sector boundaries. Workshop teachers offered compressed-air carts. Cafeterias listed water bladders. Fabrication colleges marked rooms with independent scrubbers. Unlicensed maintenance passages appeared in gray, added by local custodians who knew which doors opened when powered bolts failed.

The old Serein eclipse map returned to her with physical precision: four blue shelters, no farm tunnels, Edrin asking who had been omitted. She pushed the memory aside before it could become an explanation. Halcyon required choices in the present.

“Cause trace,” she said to Bay Four.

Three cartographers separated power demand from Cantilever phase. A fourth compared maintenance work against the decoupling pulse. Ansa sent every layer to Halcyon's local engineering board, including confidence ranges and the two unverified assumptions that had passed review.

Edrin came back on channel. “Sector five has a classroom seal failing shut.”

“Failing shut preserves air.”

“It also keeps thirty-one students inside a room with a damaged scrubber.”

A camera feed appeared. The classroom held emergency red light. Children sat along an inner wall with hoods ready. A teacher worked a manual release whose gear had jammed halfway through travel. Outside, two fabrication apprentices removed a corridor panel.

“Do they have a bypass?” Ansa asked.

“They have a custodian.”

An older man crawled through the panel gap carrying a mechanical driver. He disappeared beneath the floor. The seal readout fell from eighteen minutes to sixteen while he worked.

Ansa found the door model. Its manual release assumed access from the classroom side. The maintenance cavity provided no approved control point.

“Lower hinge has a service shaft,” she said. “Thirty-two centimeters behind the atmosphere conduit.”

Edrin repeated the location. Metal struck metal over the feed. The door moved five centimeters. Students passed their hoods through first, then crawled under one at a time. The smallest child stopped beside the opening to hold a light for the next.

No master evacuation command produced that movement. People with partial maps corrected one another in real time.

The first casualty estimate reached Ansa's board as a range from zero to forty-eight. Its upper bound assumed every sealed classroom remained occupied until central release. She rejected the assumption and requested head counts from the school network. Data arrived in incompatible forms: voice lists, teacher slates, corridor camera tallies, and photographs of names written on walls. Bay Four's civil parser discarded two of them as uncertified.

“Accept witnessed local counts,” Ansa said.

“They may duplicate students who moved between rooms,” a cartographer warned.

“Preserve movement times. Let the duplicates remain visible until adjacent schools reconcile them.”

The result looked untidy. It also reduced the unknown population from eight thousand to six hundred and twelve in four minutes. Edrin's coordinators called each unresolved name across neighboring sectors. A child listed twice became evidence of a successful transfer, not a database error. Three names with no destination triggered physical searches.

One search reached a water-lift lobby in sector eight. The lift had stopped below floor level with its doors open, leaving a gap too narrow for an adult. Two students had crawled through before a brake warning sounded. A third remained on the car roof beneath the landing. The local drill map routed a machine-shop instructor to the brake cabinet while classmates spoke to the child through the gap.

Ansa opened the lift's certified rescue procedure. It required phase power, a service key held at central transit, and an inspection camera unavailable during general dark. She closed it. The instructor used a manual chain hoist from the machine shop. A custodian locked the counterweight with a steel teaching bar. They lifted the car eleven centimeters and pulled the child through.

Edrin marked all three names safe. The casualty range fell again. Ansa attached the local method to Halcyon's live record without upgrading it into a universal procedure. A steel bar that saved one child could kill the next if the counterweight geometry differed. Provenance had to preserve limits as carefully as success.

Halcyon engineering chief Vassa Nyr entered the channel. Her voice clipped from packet loss. “Aphelion, re-lock 66. We can restore phase support in under three minutes.”

Ansa displayed the consequence before answering. Re-locking would push correction debt back into the outer pair and narrow the low-shear corridor. The current decoupling had exposed a load path the model needed to measure. If they reversed now, the fault would remain hidden until the Open Chord, when twelve stations changed state under the Pale Interval.

“Three minutes restores your bus,” Ansa said. “It also ends the validation before the fabricator cells reveal why they failed.”

“Children do not breathe validation.”

“I know.”

“Then send the re-lock.”

The command sat armed on Ansa's board. Olan's renewed phase lock technically denied her authority to execute it, yet emergency restoration protocol allowed a local request through. Vassa's seal would satisfy the rule. One motion could bring Halcyon power while closing the only full-scale test available before SD94.

A second display showed the Orison migration. First-arcs had begun using the space opened by the partial decoupling. Re-locking 66 would compress that space. Human survival and Orison passage occupied the same control surface again, neither reducible to a clean moral priority.

Ansa did not touch the command.

“Chief Nyr, what do your local cells need to black-start?”

“Stable timing. They rejected the Cantilever clock when phase dropped.”

“They have internal crystals.”

“Three replacements came from one Vela lot. The rest disciplined themselves to those three during maintenance.”

There lay the omitted dependency. Seven independent cells had quietly learned one clock.

“Can they accept a mechanical rotation reference?” Ansa asked.

Vassa answered after consulting someone off channel. “Fabrication cells can. Hospital sets cannot.”

“Use the Ring's spin beacons.”

“Conversion takes twenty-two minutes if the old controller works.”

Edrin heard the number. “Our school seal margin averages twenty-six.”

“Averages,” Ansa said.

“Yes. I know what that word hides.”

Bay Four populated the margins by room. Eleven school spaces fell below twenty-two minutes. Four could move occupants through safe corridors. Three had independent compressed air not entered in central inventory. Two could connect to workshop tanks. The last two served atmosphere-supported students whose hoods offered only twelve minutes at their prescribed mix.

Vassa said, “Re-lock remains the safe system action.”

“For which boundary?” Edrin asked.

“Halcyon.”

“Then let Halcyon choose after seeing both routes.”

Ansa pushed the complete consequence map to him and Vassa. She included corridor compression, first-arc exposure, every school seal, local cell uncertainty, and the fact that holding long enough to black-start would validate one essential part of the Open Chord. She removed her optimized recommendation before transmission.

Vassa noticed. “What do you advise?”

“I advise no hidden assumptions. The choice belongs to the people carrying these loads.”

“That sounds careful from Aphelion.”

“It frightens me from Aphelion.”

Edrin's channel filled with local voices. Sector school coordinators reported room states. Atmosphere technicians named portable reserves. A clinic objected to any hold beyond eight minutes. Fabrication workers offered power from thermal stores if control buses could isolate them. Halcyon's civil delegate joined and tried to impose order, then stopped when Edrin asked whether she knew which maintenance corridors remained open.

The decision did not become clean because more people spoke. It acquired edges.

A coordinator from sector seven refused the hold unless atmosphere-supported students moved first. A water engineer warned that restoring power through re-lock could restart lifts against closed valves and rupture three mains. An Orison contact technician reported that the witness swarms had shaped away from Halcyon's radiators, reducing local magnetic noise. Vassa assigned a named engineer to every uncertainty instead of asking Ansa for one final answer.

At minute five, Edrin issued a Halcyon school condition: hold only if every room below twenty-two minutes had a physical route or independent air in hand, not promised. The coordinators began confirming them one by one.

Ansa watched status markers change. Sector three moved eighty-six students into a gym with chemical scrubbers. Sector six opened a maintenance bridge between two schools. The two atmosphere-support rooms received workshop tanks on manual carts. One coupling did not fit.

“Adapter?” Edrin asked.

“No certified part,” a technician said.

A fabrication teacher held two fittings up to her camera. “We can print a bridge in nine minutes if cell four takes mechanical timing.”

“That cell has not started.”

“It can run one printer from flywheel current.”

“Do it,” Edrin said.

Ansa saw the decision propagate through work rather than command. Halcyon did not vote as one body. Local groups established conditions, disclosed their margins, and accepted different tasks. The civil delegate recorded the resulting authority while people were already moving.

At minute seven, Vassa transmitted Halcyon's seal. “Hold the decoupling for twenty-two minutes. Re-lock command remains armed as local abort. Any school margin below four minutes triggers it automatically.”

Ansa linked the abort to the shared provenance record. “Accepted.”

Only then did she remove her hand from above the manual re-lock.

Darkness covered most of Halcyon Ring. External cameras showed a long curved band with isolated windows burning on battery. Its rotation carried those lights into night and back toward the sun without changing their scarcity. Water stopped climbing to upper districts. Families filled containers from pressure taps before residual head vanished. Clinics shifted noncritical patients toward wards with independent air.

The school network kept reporting.

At minute nine, the printed adapter failed pressure test. The teacher altered wall thickness and began again. At minute eleven, a water lift brake released unexpectedly in sector four. Local crews caught the car between levels and opened both doors. At minute twelve, one atmosphere seal dropped below its forecast margin because sixty-three people had entered a room rated for forty. Edrin ordered half of them through an unlicensed storage passage into a college clean room.

The storage passage crossed a fire partition whose magnetic latch had frozen when phase power vanished. Halcyon control advised waiting for a portable release pack. Its nearest pack sat three sectors away behind a stalled water lift. The clean room held enough air for everyone if they could open the partition.

A college materials teacher proposed cutting the latch. Her saw would throw conductive dust into a corridor carrying exposed atmosphere trunks. The local coordinator refused. A student then remembered that fabrication classes used the same latch assembly in maintenance training. The class rig had a hand-crank release plate designed to show the mechanism in section.

Edrin sent two apprentices to strip the plate from the rig. Their helmet camera showed their hands working by emergency light, one turning fasteners while the other read pressure numbers aloud. The plate fit the real latch with a three-millimeter gap. They packed the gap using a polymer ruler cut from a classroom kit and turned the crank.

The fire partition opened. Twenty-seven people moved into the clean room. The coordinator closed it manually behind them and reported six minutes of atmosphere gained across both spaces.

“Add the training rigs to emergency inventory,” Edrin said.

“After the event,” Vassa answered. “Right now every addition changes the map faster than we can verify it.”

“The map can be late. The tools cannot.”

Ansa heard no triumph in her brother's reply. Improvisation consumed people. Every unlisted tool required someone to remember it in darkness, reach it, prove it fit, and accept responsibility when official instructions offered no protection. A distributed system did not remove expertise. It placed expertise closer to consequences and made its absence local enough to see.

Ansa tested every new load against the armed abort. Each adjustment changed what remained tolerable. She had spent years believing good models converted motion into a stable picture. The Halcyon map refused stability because the people inside it kept saving one another in ways no certified route included.

At minute fourteen, the first fabrication cell accepted the Ring's spin beacon. Its local frequency wandered beyond normal phase tolerance but held long enough to power a control rack. Vassa isolated the output before it could synchronize badly with neighboring buses.

“Cell four alive,” she said.

The school channel answered with brief applause, then returned to counts.

Cell four powered two printers, three air pumps, and the controller needed to start cell two. Minimal power moved through temporary cables strung across fabrication floors. The network remained too weak for water lifts. It could support atmosphere seals if operators shed every nonessential process.

At minute sixteen, the replacement adapter passed. Workshop air reached the two supported classrooms with five minutes left in their portable tanks.

Edrin's voice came directly to Ansa. “You still have the re-lock armed?”

“Yes.”

“Good.”

“You want it available.”

“I wanted the choice shared. I did not want the machinery to become symbolic.”

Ansa looked at his school map. “I used to think your objection concerned who drew the boundary.”

“It concerned whether people outside it could alter the route.”

The distinction landed without reconciliation. They had no time to make it personal.

At minute eighteen, cell two started. Its output brought corridor lights and one water pump online. Hospital emergency sets still lacked timing. Vassa routed local cell power around them rather than risk a bad synchronization. The clinic that had demanded an eight-minute limit withdrew its objection after confirming battery reserve, then required first priority when cell five came alive.

Minute twenty arrived. The Open Chord validation accumulated enough stable data to prove the local cells could accept a non-phase reference. It also showed restart loads thirty-one percent above Ansa's forecast. A full system loosening would fail unless every jurisdiction raised its local reserve floor.

“Data condition met,” she said. “Halcyon can re-lock now without losing the validation.”

Vassa answered, “Local cells hold atmosphere in ten sectors. We keep phase support out unless school control calls abort.”

The decision surprised Ansa. She checked Edrin's channel.

“Schools concur,” he said. “Water can wait. Air cannot, and local air now holds.”

At twenty-two minutes, cell five started from cell two. Its first output went to atmosphere seals, not lights. Classroom pressure traces climbed above emergency margins. The remaining red sectors turned amber.

No casualty alarm appeared. Seven people had suffered injuries during dark evacuations, none life-threatening. Two hospital procedures had stopped safely. One hundred and nine lift passengers waited for manual extraction. The blackout had harmed Halcyon without producing a mass casualty event, a difference measured by drills, local stores, and work performed in the dark.

Ansa disarmed the automatic re-lock only after Vassa and Edrin supplied separate seals. Cantilevers 66 and 67 remained in their loosened state. The corridor validation continued.

Power did not return as one event. Fabrication cells took loads in uneven islands. School corridors brightened before market boards. Atmosphere pumps woke before transit desks. Water lifts started empty, then accepted passengers after local checks. The priorities appeared in the power trace as a new order of lines.

Halcyon's civil delegate requested a formal connection. She had moved from the powered control center to sector five, where children slept on a fabrication floor behind temporary seals. Her jacket carried a chalk handprint.

“Halcyon will support the Open Chord,” she said. “Conditioned on an atmosphere reserve outside all discretionary allocation. Every jurisdiction sets its own floor. No central optimization may borrow below it, even to improve total survival probability.”

The condition reduced Ansa's modeled corridor by almost one percent. It removed enough flexible power to close two efficient correction branches. Her old practice would have treated the reserve as a constraint to minimize.

“What verification do you require?” she asked.

“Local custody. Public telemetry. Abort authority if the floor drops.”

Edrin added, “And school seals count as infrastructure, not secondary civil load.”

Ansa entered the atmosphere reserve into the shared model. She assigned authorship to Halcyon, custody to local control, and consequences to every branch it altered. The green solution corridor narrowed on her board. Its foundation became visible.

“Accepted for the model,” she said.

“Not only the model,” the delegate answered. “Bring it to the conference.”

Ansa transmitted the condition to Laleh's jurisdictional docket. On Halcyon's live map, the first water lift began climbing through the dark with no passengers aboard, testing each level before anyone trusted it with a body.

Chapter 24 — Nine Voices and Five Silences

Five jurisdictional seals remained gray when Laleh Sorn called the conference to order.

Olan Brace sat beneath them at the Authority table, where his live Cantilever keys occupied a black pane beside his hand. Nine other seals shone white around the Assembly chamber. They indicated permission to negotiate, not approval of the Open Chord. The distinction appeared in the conference rules three times because every delegate had learned what emergency language could conceal.

Laleh stood at the center desk with no presiding emblem. Serein had convened the meeting, but its seal carried the same size and brightness as the others. Behind her, the chamber wall showed power reserves, correction loads, local abort routes, and the Orison migration's measured position. No optimized system total appeared.

“We have six hours before the first distant mandate expires,” she said. “We will settle authority before engineering preference. No region acquires consent through silence. No speaker may convert a refusal into a veto over bodies beyond the refusing region's assigned load.”

Meridia's delegate raised a token. “That second rule assumes the conclusion.”

“It states the problem.”

“It states moving jurisdiction as settled law.”

“Not law. Working procedure. Challenge it with a consequence.”

Olan opened his proposed command charter. He had reduced it from forty-seven clauses to nineteen. Under his design, local councils set reserve floors and named abort conditions. A central conductor verified whether those conditions had been met, issued synchronized commands, and carried legal liability for timing. The conductor could not alter local floors. Local controllers could not interrupt an active sequence without the conductor's confirmation unless communications failed.

The design preserved what Tern had taught him: coordinated motion needed a reachable decision maker. It also preserved the office currently holding the keys.

Laleh recognized his token. “Director Brace.”

“The Cantilevers cannot negotiate after execution begins,” he said. “A twelve-station sequence tolerates local variation only inside calculated windows. Someone must determine when an abort condition exists across shared loads. My draft assigns that determination to a conductor witnessed by every jurisdiction.”

Halcyon's delegate turned her chalk-marked jacket sleeve toward him. She had not changed clothes after the blackout. “Our atmosphere reserve cannot wait for a distant determination.”

“Your local controller may trigger an emergency stop.”

“After communications fail.”

“Or after measured reserve crosses the certified floor.”

“Certified by whom?”

“The conference, before execution.”

Her white seal dimmed as she withdrew negotiation authority for the clause. The change cut one possible approving region from nine to eight.

Olan kept his voice level. “A floor without shared verification lets one bad sensor stop the chord while Neme and the Orison carry active load.”

“And shared verification without local custody lets your office debate our air for three minutes,” she said. “We used those three minutes on SD78. We will not lend them again.”

The Neme delegate joined. “An abort can also kill people outside the region that invokes it. Our tide models require warning.”

“Warning, yes,” Halcyon said. “Permission, no.”

Laleh directed both proposals into the public text. One required local abort authority at the reserve floor. The other required immediate consequence transmission to every affected controller. Neither guaranteed agreement.

Olan looked at the five gray seals above the chamber. Their delegates had joined by witness channel but refused authority to bargain toward approval. One represented a settlement whose local grid could not survive the planned eleven-day loosening. Two objected to Orison participation in Cantilever control. Another faced a public mandate forbidding any deliberate reduction in phase-gate capacity. The last contested post-crisis liability and would not expose its treasury to harms ordered elsewhere.

Under old Assembly practice, those regions counted as obstacles to overcome or minorities to bind after a lawful vote. Laleh had placed them inside the room without pretending they consented.

The first gray-seal delegate spoke through a delayed channel. “We cannot join an execution that drops our reserve below twenty-eight days.”

“Your current allocation reaches nineteen under the locked forecast,” Laleh said.

“That does not create authority to choose a different failure for us.”

“No. What load can you refuse without preventing corrections assigned elsewhere?”

The delegate sent a local diagram. Its Cantilever fed two transfer loops and one power coupling. The region could decline active phase reduction while allowing the adjacent station to decouple, provided Meridia surrendered a scheduled energy draw during the critical window.

Meridia objected at once. “That transfer supports seven urban grids.”

“Then those grids may decline,” the gray delegate said. “They may not call our reserve available by default.”

Olan enlarged the diagram. The proposed exemption added heat to Cantilever 58, which remained outside the twelve-station loosening sequence. His engineers had modeled the transfer as inefficient but possible. “The alternate coupling holds only if Meridia sheds four industrial banks before execution.”

Meridia's delegate examined her constituency panel. Every bank listed contracts, wages, and medical dependencies. “We can shed two.”

“Then the exemption does not close.”

Laleh asked, “What if Serein takes one bank and Vela takes the other after its atmosphere floor?”

Vela required twelve minutes to answer due to local consultation. Serein's engineer began calculating before Laleh finished. The chamber waited. No one filled the delay with argument because any new proposal would change the question traveling outward.

Olan watched the gray delegate's seal. It remained gray even as the rest of Pelagos rearranged power around its refusal. The process looked less efficient than command. It also produced a fact his charter had hidden: the region could avoid direct participation without closing the corridor if others accepted named costs.

Vela returned with conditional assent. Serein accepted half a bank. Meridia agreed to shed the remaining half during the critical window, with hospital and water loads protected.

The gray delegate read the completed assignment. “We do not approve the Open Chord. We will not obstruct the adjacent decoupling if these floors remain public and enforceable.”

Laleh marked the first non-obstruction guarantee. No seal changed color.

They moved to load transparency. Ansa Meren joined from Aphelion with the full provenance model divided into jurisdictional views. Every correction showed its physical source, expected beneficiary, risk carrier, confidence range, and named author. Orison loads appeared by civic mind and host body where the shared clock could distinguish them. Unassigned uncertainty occupied a separate column rather than disappearing into system margin.

Olan opened the Authority verification layer. “Publication creates no assurance that local edits use compatible assumptions.”

Ansa answered after light delay. “Every input declares units, clock reference, observation source, and tolerance. Incompatible edits remain visible as conflicts.”

“Visible conflict does not resolve timing.”

“No. People resolve it.”

“Under whose authority?”

“Under the authority assigned to the consequence they change.”

The formula still irritated him. A correction moved several bodies. Consequences crossed boundaries before law could follow them. Olan selected Neme's final burn. The load tree touched the tide cities, Remainder, seven synchronized Cantilevers, two Orison adults, first-arcs, Halcyon power, and Meridia's industrial banks.

“Name the jurisdiction for this,” he said.

Ansa did not simplify. “Neme holds its foundation boundary. Ivo's crew and Neme local command hold the tug's operational veto. Each Cantilever region holds its reserve floor. Kehr and the participating Orison polity hold their bodily load. The conference holds the timing window that joins them. No one owns the whole correction.”

“Then no one can compel completion.”

“That remains true even if your office issues the command.”

A low murmur moved through the gallery. Olan could have treated the answer as rhetoric. The Remainder's maintenance card supported her. Its crew could refuse. Neme could abort on tide limits. Orison bodies could alter pressure. His key controlled human stations, not completion.

Laleh said, “We need a timing authority, not a command owner.”

Olan's charter pane waited beneath his hand. “You are changing the title.”

“I am changing the power.”

“Timing without enforcement becomes advice.”

“Enforcement without participation becomes your renewal.”

The public challenge against him had reached formal review. Eight jurisdictions now supported removal, though two wanted him to remain through control transfer. His office could dissolve while the lock persisted in hardware. He had built succession packages because continuity required planning for that separation.

“Strike conductor,” Halcyon's delegate said. “Replace with timing convenor. The convenor publishes executable windows and consequence changes. Local controllers execute within them.”

Neme objected. “If one controller misses, everyone downstream needs authority to shift.”

“Bounded shifts can be pre-authorized,” Ansa said. “The model already contains them.”

Olan examined the branch table. Each local controller had three permitted timing variations and one abort. No controller could invent a fourth during execution. The convenor would select the initial window, receive local changes, and recalculate. Authority remained distributed but not unlimited.

“What happens when two valid local changes conflict?” he asked.

Laleh looked toward his key pane. “Then the sequence stops unless the affected jurisdictions resolve them.”

“The Pale Interval will not stop.”

“No.”

“Your procedure can choose failure through inaction.”

“So can yours,” she said. “You have demonstrated it for twelve days.”

Olan read the faces around the chamber. No applause rescued anyone from the accusation. Laleh had not called him malicious. She had named a result.

He closed his command charter.

The action cost nothing physical. His Cantilever keys remained live. Yet Meridia's delegate shifted in her seat, and three Authority lawyers began sending private warnings to his pane.

“Put the timing-convenor text on the wall,” Olan said. “Keep pre-authorized variations narrow enough for structural limits. Require a public conflict notice if execution stops.”

Halcyon's seal brightened again. Nine potential approvals returned.

The second gray region addressed Orison participation. Its public mandate prohibited surrendering infrastructure control to nonhuman actors. The delegate displayed footage from the Neme defense exchange, cutting it before human fire killed the first-arcs.

“Our voters saw mass used against an inhabited moon,” she said. “They do not authorize an Orison hand on a Cantilever.”

Kehr's response traveled through the shared clock and Ansa's approximate labels. Pressure changes marked responsibility boundaries: Orison bodies would carry specified correction loads but receive no station keys. Tull's Narrow Mercy remained outside the agreement. The labels could not convey trust where none existed.

Laleh asked, “Does your mandate forbid load coordination or control transfer?”

“Control transfer.”

“Then no Orison actor receives a human key. Their pressure commitments enter the same public assignment as tug burns and tide limits.”

Olan saw the technical weakness. “A pressure commitment cannot be enforced.”

“Neither can Remainder's burn after its crew refuses,” Neme's delegate said.

“The crew falls under human law.”

“Law does not ignite engines.”

The gray delegate requested a witness condition. Two human observatories, one local to her region, would verify every Orison load before dependent human action. If observed pressure departed tolerance, her region could withdraw its indirect support without obstructing unrelated corrections.

Ansa incorporated the condition. Kehr shaped acknowledgment through the clock. The region's seal stayed gray.

“We do not consent to their participation,” the delegate said. “We agree not to obstruct loads that do not use our station key, provided our observatory receives raw measurements.”

A second guarantee entered the text.

By the third hour, the conference had produced no approving vote and prevented four withdrawals. Food arrived in sealed trays. The delegates ate at their desks because leaving would restart several mandate clocks. Olan drank water and read every clause as staff merged them.

Reserve floors remained local and public. Abort authority stayed with the controller carrying the named boundary. Load assignments included confidence and provenance. Timing belonged to a convenor constrained by pre-authorized windows. Every stop, change, or failure would enter a public record available to all fourteen jurisdictions.

A hidden conflict surfaced when Neme opened its assigned-load table. Authority software had grouped three small correction costs under SYSTEM STABILITY. The label carried no jurisdiction, only a percentage drawn from shared reserve. Olan knew the category. Engineers used it for effects too distributed to bill cleanly: timing noise, thermal drift, and the power required to keep control surfaces synchronized.

Laleh selected the line. “Who carries this?”

“All participating stations in proportion to phase capacity,” Olan said.

“Then name them.”

“Expanding the line adds complexity without changing the total.”

“It changes who can test the total.”

Ansa unfolded the category from Aphelion. One component fell mainly on Cantilever 71, already near its heat limit. Another reduced Halcyon's atmosphere margin during restart. A third drew from a gray-seal region whose guarantee allowed timing relay but prohibited phase load.

The grouped line had not concealed fraud. It had concealed inconvenience, which could become injury just as efficiently.

Olan ordered the Authority layer removed from the conference copy. Engineers republished all three costs with source measurements and affected floors. The third could not remain assigned to the dissenting region. Meridia accepted half. Neme took the rest after lowering a nonessential elevator schedule.

“Does every system category unfold this way?” the first gray delegate asked.

“Every category touching an assigned boundary,” Olan said.

“Who decides what touches?”

“The public model flags measured dependency. Any jurisdiction may challenge an omission.”

Laleh added the rule to the text. Transparent load assignments now meant more than publishing totals. The agreement required every pooled cost to disclose the bodies and reserves beneath it.

Post-crisis liability remained unresolved.

The fifth gray region refused any clause that allowed claims against it for damage from a plan it did not approve. Neme refused any settlement that left tide-city losses with local taxpayers. Meridia wanted liability proportional to formal voting power. Serein proposed attaching liability to assigned load and disclosed benefit, whether a region voted yes, no, or abstained.

Olan listened until the categories repeated. “Liability cannot follow benefit without a method for valuation.”

Laleh said, “Then propose one.”

“The existing Infrastructure Tribunal can assign causation after the event.”

“The existing tribunal inherits Authority records and Authority definitions.”

“It has independent judges.”

“It lacks the Orison evidence, local provenance, and phase-wake archive that created this crisis.”

Olan opened the tribunal statute. Its jurisdiction covered recognized persons, governments, chartered entities, and autonomous civil systems. Orison civic minds fit none of them. Neither did a moving jurisdiction defined by bodily consequence.

“Create a temporary claims panel,” he said. “One reviewer selected by each approving region. Five selected collectively by nonparticipants. Technical evidence drawn from the public load record. Authority liability remains subject to the archive, not extinguished by transfer.”

His lawyers' warnings multiplied.

The gray delegate asked, “Why would approving regions accept fewer reviewers per region than we receive?”

“Because nine individual appointments plus five collective appointments produce fourteen. Equal count without pretending each nonparticipant joined execution.”

Laleh studied the proposal. “Orison harm?”

“The panel may find fact and assign human liability. Any reciprocal remedy requires Compact negotiations after contact permits it.”

Ansa added from the wall, “The witness cohort can preserve claims even if the migration continues.”

No one called the solution complete. It created a place where claims could survive the event without forcing recognition Pelagos lacked language to grant. The fifth gray region accepted immunity from execution liability beyond its published load, but not from harms caused by obstruction or undisclosed action.

“We will not obstruct,” its delegate said. “We will preserve records and submit to the panel for our own conduct.”

The remaining three dissenters negotiated similar boundaries. One allowed its station to relay timing without changing phase. Another guaranteed rescue access while refusing correction mass. The region with the phase-capacity mandate permitted no deliberate reduction, yet agreed not to draw emergency power from neighboring stations during their decoupling. Each silence acquired a defined shape.

At the fifth hour, Laleh displayed the complete authority text. She read no speech. She called each jurisdiction by its registered order and asked for one of three statements: approve and undertake assigned loads; decline and accept a non-obstruction guarantee; or reject without guarantee.

Serein approved first. Neme followed after confirming its tide abort. Halcyon approved with the atmosphere reserve protected. Vela's answer arrived six minutes later and assigned its fabrication cells to local power support. Meridia approved after industrial draw limits became public.

Four more white seals joined them. The count reached nine.

The five gray seals did not brighten. Each delegate stated refusal in the terms of local mandate. Then each read the guarantee attached to that refusal. No one called the result unanimous. No one translated non-obstruction into consent.

Laleh sealed the conference text. “Nine jurisdictions approve the Open Chord and undertake its assigned loads. Five decline participation under published boundaries. All fourteen accept transparent consequence records, local guarantees, and post-crisis review. No jurisdiction has filed rejection without guarantee.”

Olan's Authority pane requested recognition of the political mandate. He granted recognition. The system then presented the next required action.

TRANSFER CANTILEVER CONTROL TO SHARED COUNCIL.

The chamber could authorize the plan. It could not reach into the Authority network and divide his keys. His illegal renewal still held Cantilevers 61 through 72 in phase lock. The succession packages were prepared, but none had activated. Every negotiated window depended on an act only his office could perform.

Laleh came to the Authority table. “We have authority.”

“You have a lawful coordination agreement,” Olan said.

“Do not reduce it by choosing the smaller phrase.”

He looked at the nine white seals and five gray ones. Under his old reading, legitimacy required either a majority powerful enough to bind dissent or an emergency official willing to act despite it. The conference had produced another structure: participation by nine, bounded refusal by five, obligations carried by both.

His key pane remained black beside his hand.

“When will you release control?” Laleh asked.

Olan opened the transfer request and verified every recipient against the conference text. Fourteen local custodians appeared, along with the timing convenor and the public provenance archive. No central conductor occupied the list.

“I will answer after Control validates the packages,” he said.

“That work finished yesterday.”

“Yes.”

Laleh waited.

Olan placed his seal on the recognition record, not on the transfer. Around the chamber, nine voices had committed bodies and power. Five refusals had become promises not to obstruct. The black pane kept the Cantilever keys under his name.

Chapter 25 — The Narrow Mercy

Tull began the seizure by removing a shadow.

One of the Narrow Mercy bodies had sheltered behind Bent Morning for six intervals, dark against the human instruments and quiet inside the migration's correction field. It moved outward now, letting Asteron's charged wind strike its mineral core. Pressure gathered along its sail. Witness swarms fled the rising shear in tight silver knots.

Across Kehr's five adult bodies, pain arrived at different speeds. Ember-Side registered the field change first. Little Weight translated it into a probable thrust. Silt Crown felt the corridor narrow around the first-arcs.
