Harrow of Rain supplied memory: a crossing taken by force, a settled species answering with pursuit, three centuries of routes abandoned afterward.

Kehr held consensus long enough to name the act. Tull intended to push through Cantilevers 61–72 before human authority could transfer control.

The first-arcs behind Bent Morning adjusted to the false shelter. Their young correction loops copied the adult motion without understanding its purpose. If Tull accelerated, they would follow into the locked resonance field. Some might survive. The smallest would come apart where the human phase surfaces met Asteron's tail.

Kehr sent a refusal through pressure, not radio. Little Weight crossed the intended line of thrust. Ember-Side spread its sail, presenting mass where Tull needed emptiness. The move cost three degrees of magnetic angle that the Open Chord model had reserved for the final passage.

Tull answered with a close correction.

The Narrow Mercy body rotated inside Little Weight's wake and struck the field sideways. No surfaces touched. Their magnetic skins compressed until mineral lattices in both bodies rang below human hearing. Kehr lost one interval of Bent Morning's perception. A first-arc cried through a burst of incoherent current, and two witness swarms shed memory grains into the dark.

Human sensors watched from the damaged tug, the Neme stations, and the Aphelion Array. Kehr felt their measuring pulses cross the conflict. Tull felt them too. That made the correction a declaration before both species, not a private dispute hidden inside alien weather.

“We will not close after you,” Kehr transmitted through the shared clock.

The clock could not carry the Orison relation called closing-after: bodies entering a path whose danger another body had chosen without them. It rendered the thought as three trajectories. Tull's line crossed the corridor. First-arcs followed. Their projected coherence failed in sequence.

Tull returned a harder geometry. Human lock. Human weapons. Human delay. The three charges came through as repeated pressure scars, each one witnessed at the point where human action had killed Orison young.

Kehr did not deny them. Denial would have required erasing the dead.

Harrow of Rain moved closer to Little Weight, reinforcing the failing magnetic skin. Its oldest sail bore pale rents from crossings before Pelagos had built a phase gate. Memory traveled through those rents with poor precision and immense authority. Harrow offered the image of Vaul's abandoned first-arcs, then the human names recorded after Neme: Ren Adek, twenty-three juveniles, 1,140 civilians. No innocence traveled with the names. Obligation did.

Tull tightened the intended thrust.

Through Silt Crown, Kehr detected another disturbance behind the migration. It resembled a wake dragged out of alignment, too weak to belong to an adult and too broad for witness swarms. Silt Crown folded one edge of its sail across the reading before Kehr could resolve it.

The concealment broke consensus.

Ember-Side wanted to hold Tull at once. Bent Morning warned that a second close correction could tear Little Weight's outer lattice. Harrow asked why Silt Crown had accepted the shadow. Silt Crown answered with pressure withheld from the other bodies, an absence shaped by long practice.

Kehr became we.

We remembered Tull's doctrine as a cruelty spoken in public: sacrifice damaged stragglers to preserve nurseries. We had not asked which damaged lives the doctrine already protected. The omission pressed through all five bodies until even the first-arcs altered their intervals around us.

Tull used our division. Its body slid past Little Weight and gained the inner edge of the corridor. In another forty human minutes, the human lock would provide a surface it could push against. Seven Cantilevers would answer the shove by holding Neme. The accumulated correction debt would fall into inhabited tides.

Kehr recovered consensus by surrendering pursuit.

Instead of chasing Tull, all five bodies changed relation. Ember-Side exposed its conductive underside. Bent Morning narrowed its sail. Little Weight accepted the close shear. Silt Crown opened the hidden portion of its wake, despite the resistance still moving through it. Harrow carried the shared clock's living pattern across the arrangement.

Together they made a witnessed prediction.

If Tull seized the corridor, Little Weight would not counter. The first-arcs visible to human instruments would enter behind Narrow Mercy. Neme's held orbit would shift at the wrong phase. Pelagos would answer, perhaps with weapons and perhaps with the automatic violence of infrastructure. Behind Silt Crown, the concealed disturbance would lose even its poor shelter.

Tull broke its acceleration by less than one part in a thousand.

The pause sufficed.

Silt Crown unfolded.

A second, damaged cluster of first-arcs lay inside the body's magnetic lee. They carried injuries older than Pelagos: torn sails that could hold charge only when another body supplied the missing edge, mineral seeds fused after phase exposure, correction loops so fragile that the young shared one prediction among several bodies. Witness swarms packed the gaps between them. From human range, the whole cluster had resembled a flaw in Silt Crown's wake.

Kehr received them as pressure against memory. Some had come from Vaul. Others carried nursery signatures from the second sterilized star. Their trajectories did not match the migration manifest given to the human shared clock. Tull had hidden them from humans and from rival Orison polities alike.

Harrow sent grief without softening it.

Silt Crown returned shame, then resistance. Disclosure had once meant classification. Classification had preceded taking. The Orison silence doctrine had grown from more than fear of predation. It had grown from the measured fact that settled species often preserved what they could count and abandoned what exceeded their categories.

Kehr carried a human memory in answer. The trace came from Ansa Meren's model exchange, not from her private mind: fourteen jurisdictions, each marking bodies and systems that the optimized plan had omitted. School atmosphere lifts. Tide-city foundations. Rescue fuel. Local abort authority. The human plan had changed because excluded people forced themselves into its arithmetic.

Tull pressed a question into the shared field. Would they change it for these?

Kehr could not promise. The humans had approved an Open Chord for a migration whose visible burdens already approached their tolerance. Adding the hidden cluster would alter timing, load, and the angle reserved for Harrow. The nine supporting jurisdictions could withdraw. The five that had agreed not to obstruct could claim the bargain had changed. Olan Brace still held the Cantilever keys.

A promise without authority would repeat the old error in another species' grammar.

Kehr asked Tull to witness the current plan before demanding its expansion.

The human model entered through light-delayed packets and correction pulses. No single map arrived. Aphelion sent trajectories with provenance marks. Neme supplied foundation limits. Halcyon attached atmosphere reserve floors. Outer settlements asserted local abort conditions. The damaged tug contributed one remaining burn envelope and the crew's renewed consent constraints. Nine jurisdictions carried affirmative signatures. Five carried boundaries around their non-obstruction.

Tull examined the human sacrifices first.

Cantilevers 61–72 would decouple. Meridia would lose central dispatch over cheap orbital transfer. Halcyon had accepted days of rationed school power after its blackout proved local cells could hold only minimum atmosphere. Neme would move 1,400 kilometers, carrying changed tides through every foundation and elevator line. The Remainder had fuel for one final full correction and no survivable margin afterward. Human regions had assigned harm to themselves before asking Orison bodies to enter the Pale Interval's edge.

None of that made the distribution just. The plans still protected some dense populations before scattered ones. Meridia retained deeper reserves than Serein. Human models described Orison loads with error bands broad enough to conceal a death. Yet sacrifice traveled both directions. Pelagos did not offer a locked home while demanding that migrants spend their bodies outside it.

Tull examined the proof as an adversary, not a petitioner. It selected a Cantilever load assigned to Serein and showed how Meridia's deeper stores could outlast the same failure. It selected Neme's local abort authority and asked whether any first-arc possessed an equal veto. It selected the human rescue corridor around the Remainder and laid that protected path beside the unprotected edge where Orison witness swarms would carry timing corrections. Every comparison exposed a preference.

Kehr carried those preferences into the shared clock. Concealment would have made agreement faster. Instead Harrow marked the adult loads that could shear memory. Little Weight marked its damaged lattice. Silt Crown opened one narrow view of the hidden cluster, enough for human instruments to measure irregular charge but not enough to expose every young body. Human replies arrived with provenance traces attached. Neme engineers rejected one load estimate. A Serein team raised the reserve demanded for its local shelters. Halcyon kept its atmosphere floor fixed even when the change worsened the passage prediction.

The model lost elegance as it became more honest.

Through Ember-Side, Kehr remembered the old predatory contact. That species had also offered a clean corridor. Its maps had rendered Orison bodies by extractable mineral mass, then classified witness swarms as debris. The route had functioned exactly as promised for those whom the map recognized. Silence doctrine followed because intelligibility had become a method of capture.

Pelagos offered no safety from that history. Its weapons had killed young. Its Authority had hidden the wake created by its gates. But the plan Kehr now witnessed preserved arguments inside its structure. A local refusal did not disappear when the aggregate forecast improved. The five human silences remained visible beside the nine approvals. Their conditions changed the maneuver even without granting central command to those who withheld consent.

Tull answered with the Vaul partition. Orison consensus had also preserved disagreement, then locked unbearable memory away until the chosen path looked unanimous. Visible provenance could still serve as decoration around a decision already taken.

Kehr accepted the correction. The Open Chord deserved no trust merely because its records exposed conflict. It deserved a test: when an omitted body entered the map, did the route change around that body, or did the body acquire a better description of its death?

Tull set the seizure vector beside the Open Chord.

The comparison crossed all nearby witness swarms. Under seizure, Tull gained the corridor four intervals earlier. Visible first-arcs had a narrow passage. The hidden cluster did not. Their damaged loops could not survive the pressure turn behind Silt Crown once Tull abandoned the shielding position.

Under the human plan, Silt Crown retained its shelter through the first decoupling. The final turn remained too fast. The second cluster would reach the corridor mouth after the safe interval closed.

Narrow Mercy had prepared to save those it could conceal by sacrificing those it could display.

The realization did not defeat Tull. It clarified the doctrine.

Tull returned the memory of two dead nurseries. At the first, debate had consumed the interval needed for departure. At Vaul, silence had preserved the migration but left injured young behind. Mercy, in Tull's usage, meant a boundary drawn before grief could make action impossible. Every crossing generated such a boundary. Those who refused to draw it merely hid the line inside delay.

Kehr felt the accusation enter the partitioned places of the Twelve Returns. Harrow had restored the Vaul memory to consensus, but restoration did not make the path smooth. Some portions arrived as pressure without image. Others carried young trajectories that ended at a blank edge. Kehr had once allowed procedure to name abandonment as silence.

“We will draw a boundary where both sides can witness it,” Kehr sent.

Tull answered that witness did not widen a corridor.

“No. Bodies do.”

Kehr changed position again. Silt Crown remained over the damaged cluster. Little Weight moved to inherit part of Silt Crown's planned load. Ember-Side offered correction capacity reserved for guiding the visible first-arcs. Bent Morning took the resulting shear against an already weakened edge. The redistribution opened a fraction of interval at the corridor mouth.

It did not open enough.

Harrow of Rain supplied the remainder.

The oldest body rotated toward the Pale Interval's projected approach and exposed the deep conductive channels beneath its torn sail. Through those channels, Kehr carried memories that no other body held at full resolution: the first of the Twelve Returns, an unnamed star's corrected eclipse, kin lost during the predatory contact, the exact pressure of Vaul before departure. Loading Harrow beyond the approved model risked more than its body. It risked the continuity Kehr used to recognize past correction as obligation.

Ember-Side refused. Silt Crown joined the refusal. Little Weight could not hold enough coherence to choose. For several intervals, Kehr divided into incompatible selves.

Harrow corrected them.

It did not offer nobility. It transmitted capacity, likely damage, and a condition. The hidden first-arcs would enter the shared record. Humans would witness their injuries before accepting the altered load. Tull would cease using concealment as exclusive authority over their survival. Harrow's risk would purchase no obedience.

Bent Morning tested the offered capacity against actual field pressure. The result carried a failure branch Harrow had not emphasized. If the oldest body's deep channels lost coherence during the final turn, its memory could spill into nearby hosts without sequence. Kehr might retain images yet lose their relation. A migration could mistake fragments for history and build law from events in the wrong order.

Harrow did not withdraw. Its answer came as twelve remembered paths laid beside one another. None had survived unchanged. Continuity did not require perfect possession; it required accountable correction by those who carried the loss. Kehr felt resistance from Ember-Side, whose own memories depended on Harrow's ordering. Silt Crown feared the damaged cluster would gain passage by consuming the witness that made their injuries legible.

The first-arcs altered the argument. Several young bodies reached weak filaments toward Harrow's exposed channels. They did not know the full risk. They recognized a support being offered and attempted to share it, each fragile loop contributing less pressure than a human instrument could reliably separate. Harrow accepted their contact without counting it as consent. The distinction moved through Kehr's hosts and changed the condition again: adults would own the decision, while the young would remain participants in the correction rather than cargo hidden inside its wake.

Tull objected to human witness. Instruments could become claims. A record of damaged bodies might later support custody, study, or exclusion from routes labeled safe for healthier ecologies.

Kehr shaped a narrower disclosure. Humans would receive tolerances needed for passage and proof that the cluster existed. Detailed memory structures would remain under Orison custody. Witness swarms from Tull, Kehr, and the first-arcs would each preserve what had been given. No single polity could later enlarge the disclosure by claiming emergency necessity.

Silt Crown tested that boundary and joined it.

Kehr accepted the condition.

The decision changed the field. Harrow moved one body-width beyond the migration's safe relation and took pressure that had belonged to Silt Crown. The hidden cluster became visible to Aphelion's instruments. Human ranging pulses struck damaged sails and returned irregularly. Alarm traffic rose across the shared clock.

Ansa's first model response came after the light lag. It marked the new cluster as unresolved, not hostile. A later packet requested tolerances. Neme offered a smaller final tide margin. Halcyon refused any reduction in its atmosphere reserve. The five non-obstructing jurisdictions demanded a new load disclosure before their guarantees could hold.

No answer widened the interval quickly enough.

Kehr proposed a bounded delay.

The phrase entered human approximation through two clock cycles and a set of held trajectories. Tull would stop its seizure for a fixed span. Pelagos would keep the corridor from narrowing further while local engineers revised the Open Chord. Silt Crown would maintain shelter. Harrow of Rain would accept the extra load only if the damaged cluster gained witnessed passage conditions. If the revision failed by the declared boundary, every polity would recover its prior freedom of action.

Tull tested the boundary with repeated predictions. Could Olan extend the lock? Yes. Could nine jurisdictions impose risk on five? Within some physical systems, yes. Could Kehr prevent human weapons from firing? No. Could Harrow survive the added correction? The model did not support a promise.

Kehr answered each uncertainty without compression. Trust could not grow from a clean forecast. The shared clock held tolerated error because neither species had earned certainty about the other.

A packet from the human tug arrived. Its timing carried no semantic nuance, only a mechanical fact: the damaged vessel had shifted rescue fuel to preserve an evacuation path while maintaining its final burn commitment. Someone had chosen to keep a route out before agreeing to go in.

Tull watched that correction through the same instruments as Kehr.

Narrow Mercy reduced its thrust. The dangerous pressure between its body and Little Weight eased by measurable degrees. Witness swarms entered the gap, carrying both trajectories. First-arcs stopped copying the seizure line and gathered again behind the adults.

Tull accepted the fixed span. It accepted Harrow's added load as part of the revised Open Chord. It transmitted the hidden cluster's injury tolerances into the shared record, stripping Silt Crown of sole custody without surrendering responsibility for their shelter.

Kehr offered a correction toward common formation.

Tull did not take it.

The Narrow Mercy body moved into its assigned Open Chord position by a separate path, leaving clean distance between its field and Kehr's five bodies. Its final transmission carried no forgiveness, no restoration of shared memory, and no claim that Vaul had been redeemed.

It carried consent to the passage.

Chapter 26 — The Authority Lets Go

The survival figure arrived with a legal warning attached.

Olan Brace opened both on the standing console in Meridian Control. The room had been built for disasters that still obeyed jurisdiction: collisions, station failures, a phase-gate collapse contained inside one transit arc. Its basalt walls carried seventy-two vertical bands of light, one for each Cantilever. Twelve outer bands shone amber under the lock he had renewed without lawful consent.

FINAL OPEN CHORD MODEL, read the first file. The second carried counsel's seal and a title without euphemism: CONSEQUENCES OF UNAUTHORIZED KEY TRANSFER.

He read the model first.

Sixty-two percent.

The number represented successful passage for Pelagos and the Orison migration together, not the proportion of people who would live. Every major inhabited body remained within survival tolerances in that branch. The migration cleared the low-shear corridor with most first-arcs intact, including the damaged cluster revealed behind Silt Crown. Cantilevers 61–72 decoupled. Neme completed its shift. The Pale Interval crossed a deliberately loosened lattice whose failures no central office could fully predict.

A locked-system forecast offered seventy-one percent if Orison bodies were removed from the calculation.

Include the migration's actual position and likely corrections, and the locked forecast fell below the Open Chord. Include Tull's accepted delay, Harrow of Rain's extra load, local human vetoes, the Remainder's final burn, and the shared clock's timing constraints, and the sixty-two percent held through three model families. It remained uncertain. That uncertainty had edges, names, and provenance marks from fourteen jurisdictions.

Olan scrolled to the exclusions. Weapons fire after Cantilever decoupling. Unreported Orison political division. Failure of any two local power islands. A Neme abort called after the moon passed the first tide threshold. The first-arc cluster departing Silt Crown's shelter earlier than agreed. Human sabotage. Alien coercion. Ordinary error under extraordinary load.

Each branch gave him a reason to keep control.

The model's summary displayed 62% in a typeface sized for decision. Olan changed the view to branch populations. Percentages dissolved into schedules: tide-city evacuations, school atmosphere draws, correction burns, first-arc passage windows, and the hours during which local clinics would depend on batteries. No branch labeled the people who fell outside tolerance. It labeled the mechanism that failed them.

A red cluster marked Neme's western foundation arc. If the council honored every local abort, the final moon shift could stop short and close the corridor on the damaged Orison young. If it overrode the abort, three tide districts faced loads beyond their rebuilt anchors. A green branch at Halcyon assumed fabrication cells held longer than they had during the blackout. Another branch gave the Remainder enough time to evacuate thirty-two crew only if Sana Vi called the withdrawal before the last correction became visibly necessary.

Olan requested the locked-system comparison without Orison exclusion.

The console refused to produce a single headline. Tull's likely seizure, Kehr's counter-correction, weapons response, and the Pale Interval's effect on a fully coherent lattice widened the result beyond the Authority's publication standard. One family of branches preserved Meridian power while losing the outer Cantilevers. Another held the lattice until phase surfaces began correcting against one another. The apparent precision of seventy-one percent came entirely from removing the bodies most likely to reject the lock.

He opened the model's authorship ledger. Ansa's team had certified orbital dynamics but left political action unresolved. Neme engineers owned foundation tolerances. Halcyon had signed its atmosphere assumptions under protest. Orison contributions entered as witnessed predictions with error grades rather than testimony. Remainder supplied measured burn capacity. The final synthesis carried no signature field for a director.

That absence once would have disqualified it. Accountability, in Olan's architecture, required a person with power to answer for the whole. Yet no person had measured the whole. A single signature would not create comprehension. It would conceal the distribution of knowledge beneath a visible name.

He exported both forecasts to the public-release queue, including exclusions and branch ledgers. The system warned that nontechnical readers could misinterpret uncertainty ranges. Olan added a note that experts could misinterpret them too when institutional need selected the comparison.

“Director.” Counsel Mara Ess stood beyond the console's privacy arc. She carried a thin projection wafer and no paper. Meridian Control had eliminated paper after Tern because unsecured instructions had circulated faster than corrections. “The Assembly feed opens in twenty-eight minutes.”

“I can see the clock.”

“The council has requested the live keys before your statement.”

“They will receive them during the statement.”

“Then you have not accepted my advice.”

Olan closed the model without dismissing it. “I have read its title.”

Mara crossed the privacy line. She had served three directors and had never mistaken access for permission. “Transfer outside a completed Assembly vote can be charged as abandonment of statutory duty. Publishing protected infrastructure records compounds exposure. Admitting the renewal exceeded its purpose may prejudice your defense before any inquiry convenes.”

“My defense is not an infrastructure requirement.”

“Your office remains one for another twenty-eight minutes.” She placed the wafer beside his hand. “You can release operational control under supervised delegation without characterizing the renewal. That preserves review rights and avoids creating evidence against every official who acted under your orders.”

Olan looked at the amber bands. Officials had acted under those orders. Transit dispatchers canceled outer routes. Cantilever crews accumulated heat in the phase surfaces. Defense stations treated trapped migrants as targets. Some had objected. Some had complied because his seal moved liability upward, away from the hands carrying out the work.

“Those officials need a record showing where authority sat,” he said.

“The archive already shows that.”

“Not the public one.”

Mara's expression tightened. “The file you propose to publish includes protected minutes, phase-wake models, renewal drafts, and personal annotations made under counsel privilege.”

“It includes the Authority liability archive.”

“It includes every unresolved question anyone ever wrote into that archive. Opposing counsel will present doubt as foreknowledge and provisional models as established fact.”

“Some doubt constituted foreknowledge.”

“Not yours.”

The distinction had protected him for weeks. He had not commissioned the first wake study. He had not ordered the annex concealed. He had inherited a classification, then renewed it because the Cantilever network carried power, transit, atmosphere reserves, and the ordinary expectation that tomorrow would resemble today. Institutions distributed guilt across time more efficiently than they distributed authority.

Olan reopened the model. “How would you challenge sixty-two percent?”

Mara understood the question. “If I meant to preserve your control?”

“Yes.”

“I would separate human-certified inputs from Orison behavior. The latter lacks an Authority evidence standard. I would challenge the damaged cluster as late disclosure. I would characterize Tull's bounded delay as revocable under an alien political process we cannot audit. Then I would run the locked-system forecast without cooperative corrections and publish seventy-one percent.”

“Would that figure describe the system we have?”

“No.”

“Could the Assembly detect the exclusion?”

“Technical delegates could. Not before the first decoupling window closes.”

There it stood: a lawful-looking route to continued command. He could discredit uncertain inputs, retain the keys, and demand another review. By the time review exposed the method, urgency would have hardened his temporary choice into the only executable plan.

Pell had died inside such a sequence.

During the Tern Cascade, four local controllers had delayed a shared correction while waiting for one another's authority. Olan had spent twenty-two years reducing the space in which delay could hide. He built reporting chains, key custody, automatic escalation, and personal liability for command decisions. He believed unowned action killed Pell.

The renewal had taught him the adjacent fact. An owned decision could kill just as completely when no affected body could alter it.

Olan put Mara's wafer into the reader. Her opinion filled one side of the console. On the other, he opened the transfer instrument drafted by the fourteen-jurisdiction council. It did not create a new director. The Cantilever keys would split across a live custody mesh. Nine supporting regions held execution shares. Five non-obstructing regions held abort shares tied to their negotiated guarantees. Neme engineers could stop loads that crossed foundation limits. Halcyon could refuse draws beneath its atmosphere reserve. Orison corrections entered through witnessed predictions rather than command access.

No one could issue the Open Chord alone.

That design offended every emergency principle in his office. It also contained deadlines, bounded vetoes, and automatic publication of each altered assumption. Fragmented authority did not have to mean unowned authority. He had treated those as synonyms because one catastrophe had joined them in his memory.

A control officer approached from the outer stations. “Director, Cantilever 64 reports phase temperature above transfer forecast.”

“How far?”

“Point eight percent. Within lock tolerance. Outside the council's opening envelope.”

“Publish to the custody mesh.”

The officer hesitated. “Before transfer?”

“Now.”

Olan watched the reading propagate. Serein acknowledged first. Neme added a revised tide projection. Meridia power control objected to the proposed reduction. Halcyon marked its reserve floor and suggested shifting an industrial fabrication cycle. The model changed while no single participant possessed enough control to enforce a preferred correction.

At the end of four minutes, Cantilever 64's opening window moved by eleven seconds.

Under Authority practice, the same adjustment would have taken one order. Under shared control, it required six visible commitments and left a public trail of who accepted which risk. The procedure cost time. It also prevented Meridia from borrowing heat capacity that belonged to an outer station without naming the debt.

Mara watched the custody responses. “This does not cure the transfer statute.”

“No.”

“You may face trial.”

“Yes.”

“Your admission may expose the Authority to claims larger than its remaining assets.”

“That belongs in the model too.”

She removed her wafer from the reader. “Then state precisely what you did. Do not confess to motives you did not have.”

It took Olan a moment to recognize the advice inside the rebuke.

“I renewed the lock to prevent uncontrolled decoupling.”

“You also knew the Assembly had not consented.”

“Yes.”

“You did not know Tull would accept the Open Chord.”

“No.”

“You knew the lock trapped first-arcs and increased correction debt.”

“Yes.”

Mara nodded once. “Put those in separate sentences.”

The Assembly feed opened before the public causeway feed. Fourteen delegation panes arranged themselves around the console, each backed by a live allocation display. Laleh Sorn appeared from Serein with tide schematics still visible behind her. Olan recognized exhaustion in the way she kept one hand flat against the desk. Ansa Meren joined from Aphelion, framed by moving provenance lines. Neme's local council crowded three people into one pane. Five regions displayed their non-obstruction conditions above their seals.

No one greeted him.

Laleh spoke first. “Do you have the keys?”

“Yes.”

“Have you altered their permissions since the SD82 conference?”

“No.”

“Does any hidden Authority routine permit Meridian Control to restore master access after transfer?”

Olan had ordered such routines inventoried. Three legacy recovery paths existed. One had been installed after Tern. One dated to the first phase-gate trials. The third had no author in the surviving record.

“All recovery paths are listed in the liability archive,” he said. “Two have been physically severed. The third depends on a Cantilever 3 relay whose key share a council custodian now carries at this table. Meridian Control cannot restore master access without that share.”

A delegate from Rook leaned toward her pickup. “Cannot, or will not?”

“Cannot after transfer.”

That answer changed the room. Several delegates looked away to their technical staff. Laleh kept watching him.

“The final model?” she asked.

“Sixty-two percent for joint system and migration survival within declared tolerances.”

“Competing locked forecast?”

“Seventy-one percent only if Orison action is excluded. Applied to present conditions, it misstates the available system.”

Meridia's delegate interrupted. “The Authority brief we received twelve minutes ago led with seventy-one.”

“I know.”

“Did you authorize that brief?”

“No. My office's standards permitted it.” Olan opened the comparison on every pane. “Both runs will publish with their exclusions.”

Ansa enlarged the uncertainty layers. “The sixty-two percent branch includes Harrow's additional load. We have no physical confirmation that the body can hold it through the full Interval.”

“Then say so on the public feed,” Olan replied.

“I intend to.”

“Good.”

Her face changed by almost nothing. Weeks earlier, he would have heard defiance. Now he heard custody of a fact that did not belong to either of them.

The causeway feed opened.

Thousands had gathered between the Assembly basalt and the Authority tower. More watched through jurisdictional relays. Some carried names from Neme's 1,140 dead. Others held the black-and-white mark used by families of the twenty-three first-arcs killed in the magnetotail, a human symbol created for lives humans could not name. Authority workers stood at the edge in blue bars. Several had removed them.

Olan could have delivered the prepared history. It began with Tern, explained the Cantilever charter, and placed every disputed act inside an emergency inherited from earlier failures. Nothing in it amounted to a lie. The structure made consequence appear after necessity, where it could no longer alter judgment.

He deleted it.

“My authority to impose a phase lock lasts seventy-two hours,” he said. “Renewal requires Assembly consent. On System Day 70, that consent had not been completed. I renewed the lock using Tern precedent despite knowing the statute did not authorize indefinite control under an unresolved vote.”

The public channel erupted. His console reduced the sound to protect intelligibility. He saw mouths moving, hands raised, a line of Neme mourners pressing against the causeway barrier. No reaction could reach him without mediation, which suited the office too well.

“The renewal prevented an immediate uncontrolled decoupling,” Olan continued. “It also trapped additional Orison first-arcs, increased phase heat across Cantilevers 61–72, prolonged shortages in outer jurisdictions, and denied affected regions the authority to alter risks imposed upon them. Those consequences were known when I maintained the order.”

Mara stood beyond the cameras. She did not signal approval.

Olan published the Authority liability archive.

The release moved through the public network in sections because the whole file exceeded emergency broadcast limits. Phase-wake studies appeared first. Then annex indexes, classification renewals, local-risk objections, defense requests, his own margin notes, and the key-recovery inventory. Every file kept its date and uncertainty status. He did not call provisional work proof. He did not strip out the people who had warned the Authority before a conclusion became convenient.

The earliest objection came from a Cantilever materials team that had tracked coherence damage across maintenance cycles. Their note had asked whether local wake measurements could accumulate beyond the survey boundary. A classification officer routed it to the annex and removed it from routine safety review. Years later, an outer-jurisdiction analyst compared two nursery failures and requested access. The Authority denied the request because neither nursery lay inside Pelagos liability space.

Olan's own entries arrived much later. One approved continued classification while a revised model remained incomplete. Another accepted a legal reading that disclosure might trigger claims before causation had been established. The final entry carried his instruction to strip institutional liability from the public annex summary. He had written that speed required clarity. The archive restored the specific ambiguity his clarity had removed.

Incoming claims began before the release finished. Neme families requested preservation of lock orders. Serein filed notice over exported phase heat. Authority crews asked whether the archive protected internal objections from retaliation. Mara sent a blanket hold against record destruction, then copied every jurisdiction so no successor office could quietly narrow it.

A message from the Pell memorial archive appeared in Olan's private queue. Its automated custodian reported that Tern records cited in his renewal would now join the public release. Pell's death certificate, rescue delay, and Olan's testimony had long supplied moral weight to central command. Making the precedent inspectable meant others could decide whether his grief had supported the law or distorted it.

He authorized that release too.

Meridian Control filled with alarms. Legal seals broke. Records officers received contradictory injunctions from courts that had not yet compared jurisdiction. The archive continued outward through recipients Olan could no longer remove.

“I am transferring live Cantilever control to the fourteen-jurisdiction council under the published custody instrument,” he said. “I am resigning emergency authority effective upon key acceptance. This does not resolve liability for my renewal or for prior concealment. I will provide testimony and submit to inquiry.”

Someone on the causeway threw a blue Authority bar against the tower doors. Others shouted for his arrest. A smaller group began to clap and stopped when the Neme mourners did not join them. The absence of applause reached him more clearly than applause would have.

Laleh brought up the custody mesh. “Council recipients, verify shares.”

One by one, fourteen regions read cryptographic checks into the record. The procedure took too long for theater and not long enough for forgiveness. Neme verified its foundation abort. Halcyon verified the atmosphere floor. Rook accepted a load assignment after publishing its reserve estimate. Meridia's delegate paused, consulted someone outside her pane, then accepted the reduced power share.

The Orison witness channel contributed no key. It supplied predicted corrections and the measured position of the damaged cluster behind Silt Crown. Ansa attached those trajectories to the same live record.

Olan placed both hands on the console. His seal waited over the final transfer field.

For twenty-two years, every control surface had been designed to make responsibility tactile. A director should feel the moment an order became force. The seal warmed under his palm, recognizing blood flow and nerve pattern. Beyond the tower, fourteen jurisdictions held their shares. Beyond them, nineteen adult Orison bodies and hundreds of first-arcs occupied a corridor no human charter had imagined.

He authorized transfer.

The amber bands along Meridian Control's walls went dark. For one breath, the room showed no outer-system state at all. Then twelve thinner bands returned, divided into colored segments that shifted as local acknowledgments arrived. No segment belonged to the Authority.

A prompt asked whether he wished to retain observer access pending inquiry.

Olan selected yes. Refusing knowledge would have turned resignation into another form of escape.

Mara stepped beside him as his command permissions disappeared. “You no longer have authority to stand inside the control boundary.”

He moved back across the floor marker.

Laleh's voice came from the shared channel. “Cantilevers 61–72, begin staged decoupling on local confirmations.”

Cantilever 64 answered first. Its light divided, dimmed, and held at the new phase. Then 67 followed under Neme's revised tide limit. Halcyon held 69 at the opening note while its atmosphere reserve recalculated. The sequence did not move outward from Meridia. It moved wherever consent and physical readiness met.

Olan stood among the observers while shared control began taking the locked system apart.

Chapter 27 — The Open Chord

Cantilever 69 refused the opening note.

Its light on Ansa Meren's board remained amber while the other eleven outer stations entered their decoupling phases. The delay cut a narrow red wedge through every corridor model in Cartography Bay Four. Neme still needed forty-three kilometers of its planned shift. The leading edge of the Pale Interval had crossed the outer survey boundary. Behind Silt Crown, the damaged first-arcs held one another in a shelter too thin for another timing loss.

“Halcyon veto,” said the council relay.

Ansa opened the provenance trace. Halcyon Ring's local cells had dropped below the atmosphere reserve negotiated at SD82. Releasing Cantilever 69 on schedule would pull spin power from two school sectors and one water lift. The shared-control mesh had blocked the instruction before Meridian Control could borrow against the reserve.

Old instinct reached for optimization. She could move the draw to Serein, advance Cantilever 67, and recover most of the interval. Her model offered the substitution before she asked. It rendered the displaced Serein shelter load as a pale uncertainty band.

Ansa closed the automatic branch.

“Halcyon, state earliest release under your floor.”

Edrin's voice arrived through the local engineering channel. “Nineteen minutes if fabrication seven restarts. Thirty-two if it doesn't.”

“Neme, consequence of thirty-two?”

A tide engineer answered from the western city. “Foundation abort moves ahead of the final lift. We stop seven kilometers short.”

Kehr's witness pattern entered the shared clock. Seven kilometers at the corridor mouth translated into pressure across Harrow of Rain's exposed channels. The alien prediction did not call the load fatal. It showed memory intervals losing sequence.

Ansa placed all three consequences on the council mesh. “Available changes. Halcyon release at its verified floor. Neme accepts a shorter final lift. Or another jurisdiction offers an assigned draw with local conditions.”

She did not choose among them.

For forty-one seconds, the system held its incompatible needs in public. Meridia offered power but attached a restoration claim that would let it recover the draw before outer clinics. Laleh rejected the condition. Rook offered less without priority. Serein added a reserve slice if Cantilever 64 delayed its own opening by nine minutes. Neme engineers recalculated their tide peak around that delay.

The council accepted Rook and Serein together. Halcyon kept its atmosphere floor. Cantilever 69 released twenty-two minutes late.

The red wedge narrowed. It did not disappear.

Halcyon's delayed release propagated into schedules no orbital map displayed by default. Four school sectors canceled transfer drills because their doors now drew from local batteries. A water crew shifted pressure between ring segments to spare the stalled lift. Fabrication seven restarted with one furnace isolated, giving Edrin the reserve margin he had promised without taking the power from classrooms.

He sent Ansa the verified meter trace. No message accompanied it.

She remembered how she used to answer his objections with a cleaner number. During the transit reduction that divided them, her model had shown nine months of outer delay as the minimum-cost stabilization. It did not show Min Calder's scholarship route, the students waiting in unsupported berths, or Edrin deciding which school repairs could be postponed. She had treated those omissions as missing data that experts would eventually collect.

Now Edrin's local copy carried information her master model could not hold without stripping away who possessed the right to change it. Halcyon's atmosphere floor did not express a universal optimum. It expressed a commitment made by people who would breathe the consequence.

“Fabrication seven stable,” Edrin said when the reserve crossed its mark. “We can release the last draw if Neme still needs it.”

“Neme found another branch.”

“Then keep ours local.”

Ansa attached his trace to the completed veto. “Your delay cost corridor margin.”

“I know.”

“It also forced a lower tide load on Neme.”

“I know that too.”

The exchange stopped there. Neither of them converted work into reconciliation. The changed model preserved enough.

At Serein, the nine-minute alteration moved a thermal draw into eclipse storage. Laleh's engineers accepted it only after publishing the households subject to rationing. Rook's offered power closed an ore refinery whose restart would consume a month of scarce catalyst. The shared decision had not erased sacrifice. It had made the assignment visible before the burn rather than after the damage.

On the deep-field display, the Pale Interval had no clean front. It appeared as a region where confident lines became frayed bundles. Stars beyond it still occupied their measured positions. Phase-linked instruments disagreed on when each measurement had happened. The disturbance reached Cantilever 72 and split its control surface into three equally plausible states.

Local crew severed the phase relation manually.

Power vanished across two outer transfer stations. A ballast lane near Vela lost its cheap correction path. Halcyon dimmed nonessential fabrication. Serein's thermal stores began a draw they could sustain for hours, not days. Pelagos remained inhabited, but abundance fell out of it circuit by circuit.

Ansa tracked no master solution. Fourteen verified copies of the choreography moved through the mesh, each carrying local measurements and accepted obligations. When Cantilever 72's state diverged, every copy branched. Ansa could certify orbital consequences. She could not overwrite the conditions held by the people living inside them.

At Neme, seven Cantilevers pulled against the moon's prepared correction mass. Eleven days of smaller burns had moved the inhabited body 1,357 kilometers. The remaining distance required a final sequence through tidal stress that engineers had spent months learning to fear.

“Begin lift one,” Neme local control said.

The moon moved by no distance a human eye could see. On Ansa's board, tide curves climbed across three inhabited moons. Elevators stopped between foundation anchors and transferred passengers into pressure shelters. Water withdrew from one Neme harbor, exposing maintenance pylons that had not seen open air since installation. The Remainder held beyond the final anchor line, radiators scarred and port side dark where the fuel spine had cracked.

Ivo Calder's channel carried machinery vibration. “Propulsion confirms one full correction. Nothing useful after.”

Sana Vi answered from the same bridge. “Escape paths remain two. I retain veto under renewed cards.”

“Recorded,” Neme said.

Ansa entered neither voice as reassurance. The burn envelope showed the tug surviving only if no emergency correction occurred. The Open Chord model had spent every ordinary failure already. What remained belonged to events it could name but not schedule.

Lift one ended within tolerance. Neme's accumulated shift reached 1,371 kilometers.

Tull moved before the second lift.

The Narrow Mercy body entered its assigned pressure arc by a path separate from Kehr. It carried no gesture of reunion. Its field took the leading load from Silt Crown, protecting the damaged cluster while Bent Morning opened a route for the visible first-arcs. Human weapons held fire under local locks whose keys no longer sat in one office.

Kehr's other bodies formed a staggered correction around Harrow. Little Weight carried a damaged magnetic skin from the close confrontation. Ember-Side shed charged material into Asteron's tail. Witness swarms filled the distances among them, measuring every adult promise against actual motion.

The shared clock translated pressure into timing. Before. After. Harm. Passage.

Ren Adek had died preserving that clock at Neme. His final relay still appeared in the provenance record as a damaged source with verified continuity. Ansa could not ask what he would have called the pattern. She used his method instead. Every change kept its origin. No elegant phrase replaced the uncertainty that followed it.

“Lift two available,” Neme control said. “Local foundation abort remains armed.”

The burn began.

Cantilevers 62 and 67 released correction mass. Neme's prepared anchors answered with sustained thrust. The moon moved through its altered orbit while tides struck foundations built for the old one. Three jurisdictions reported power oscillation. Halcyon sealed two classroom sectors by schedule. Serein lost one transfer loop and rejected a request to reopen it before thermal stabilization.

Ansa watched the moon cross 1,380 kilometers. Then 1,386.

At 1,391, the western foundation arc exceeded its negotiated load.

Neme exercised the abort.

Seven Cantilevers cut thrust in a sequence set by local engineers. The stop preserved the tide cities and stranded the migration nine kilometers short of the modeled corridor center. Tull's pressure arc narrowed. Silt Crown rolled to protect the hidden young. The roll exposed the visible first-arcs to the first coherence failures of the Pale Interval.

Ansa's board generated a recovery burn using Meridia reserves. It violated Neme's abort. She discarded it.

“State new tolerance,” she said.

“Western arc can accept another lift after tide reversal,” Neme replied. “Six hours minimum.”

“The front reaches the first-arcs in eleven minutes,” Aphelion deep field reported.

Kehr transmitted a correction that shifted adult load toward the juveniles. Tull matched it from the opposite side. For the first time, their fields touched without contest. The combined shelter held the visible cluster but opened a seam behind Silt Crown.

One damaged first-arc drifted through.

Others followed because their shared prediction loop treated the first motion as guidance. The cluster slipped away from Silt Crown's lee and entered the fraying edge of the Pale Interval. Their weak magnetic relations began to disagree. One body corrected toward Neme. Another corrected toward the adult migration. Several held both intentions and moved into a shear that would separate them.

Ansa had no intact branch for the drift.

The council copies erupted with proposals. Defense offered a station tug whose approach would arrive too late. Neme could resume thrust only by overriding its local abort. Kehr could abandon the visible first-arcs and fold around the damaged cluster. Tull's model did the reverse. Each plan preserved one group by spending another.

A new trajectory entered from the Remainder.

Ivo had placed the tug inside the seam. Its remaining fuel could push the damaged first-arcs back toward Silt Crown while adding the missing momentum to Neme's final shift. The burn would cross the cracked port spine's containment limit. Hull survival probability vanished before the planned cutoff.

Sana's voice came hard over the propulsion channel. “Veto.”

Ivo did not answer for two seconds. “The burn keeps both paths.”

“The crew does not.”

“We can transfer nonessential stations.”

“All stations become nonessential now.”

The bridge feed showed bodies moving before argument ended. Sana had opened evacuation orders under the operational veto Ivo had ceded at Neme. Rescue skiffs and pressure capsules separated from the tug's scarred hull. Maintenance teams left consoles they had kept alive for years. Each departure removed a manual fallback from the proposed burn.

Ivo stayed at his rail console.

Sana crossed the feed and clipped his suit tether to the final skiff line. “Your consent card does not own thirty-one others.”

“It owns mine.”

“Not after you gave me the veto.”

She pulled the line. Ivo resisted long enough to touch the propulsion rail once, then let the tether carry him through the open lock. The last two crew followed. Thirty-two suit beacons appeared aboard the skiffs.

The tug remained on remote control with no maintenance hand inside it.

Evacuation changed the burn before fuel moved. One capsule carried propulsion technicians and could not clear the plume by itself. A rescue skiff gave up half its maneuvering reserve to tow it. The bridge team split control across three weak links that the shared clock could not certify. Sana assigned one link to steering, one to cutoff, and one to fuel isolation. No channel received master priority.

A maintenance card appeared in Ansa's provenance pane. Ivo's crew had written it at Neme after renewing consent. Condition four required rescue fuel for every person. Condition seven voided consent if a burn consumed all escape paths. Sana attached live fuel readings to both conditions. The final correction remained authorized only because the tow skiff preserved a narrow route beyond the debris cone.

One crew member refused the first capsule after seeing its damaged seal. Sana did not order her aboard. A Neme rescue pilot brought the skiff alongside and opened a second route, spending reserve the city had protected for foundation casualties. The crew member crossed. Neme local control updated its own rescue inventory and kept the corridor open.

Ivo's suit beacon joined the last skiff. His face remained on the propulsion channel, smaller now, lit by emergency strips instead of the bridge displays. Behind him, Sana fastened herself to a restraint web and handed cutoff authority to the crew member nearest the remote board.

“You wanted local command,” she told Ivo.

“I wanted competent command.”

“You got both. Try not to make it sound like bereavement.”

His mouth moved at one corner. “Remainder doesn't know how to burn without someone hitting the coolant brace.”

“Then tell the remote arm where the brace sticks.”

He did. The instruction entered as maintenance knowledge, not a captain's exception. A technician copied it into the shared burn sequence. Neme accepted the revision. Ansa watched authority move from a sacrificed captain, through the crew who had preserved the fact, into hands that could still refuse.

“Remainder commits final correction,” Sana said. “Crew clear. Escape path one closing.”

Ansa published the burn to every copy. Neme accepted the added momentum but preserved its abort for foundation collapse. Serein moved Cantilever 64 by four seconds. Rook offered a stabilizing draw. Halcyon refused another atmosphere reduction and suggested changing the juvenile return angle instead.

Kehr altered Bent Morning's field. Tull took the opposite load. The first-arcs began turning toward Silt Crown.

The Remainder fired.

Its intact fuel spines lit first. Thrust crossed the tug's old frame and entered the broken port structure. The hull twisted along repair seams Ivo's crew had documented for years. The correction pushed through the first-arc cluster without touching it, a broad field carrying young bodies away from phase shear.

Neme moved to 1,396 kilometers.

The port spine ruptured. Fuel entered the correction field as an uneven plume. Remote control lost three thruster groups. The tug rotated toward an angle that would cancel part of its own work.

Sana called for cutoff.

Ivo overrode no one. From the skiff, he sent the tug a maintenance sequence that isolated two lines and let the damaged third burn itself empty. Remainder steadied for another nineteen seconds.

The first-arcs reached Silt Crown's outer field.

Neme crossed 1,400 kilometers.

Then the Pale Interval struck the shared clock.

Its living magnetic pattern split across incompatible phase readings. Aphelion measured the next Orison correction after Neme's response. Halcyon measured it before. The council copies diverged by seventeen seconds, enough to make Harrow and Tull load the same corridor edge instead of opposite ones.

Ansa saw Cantilever 68's first wrong orbit laid over the failure.

Seventeen seconds had never carried a greeting. It had marked the difference between the human lattice's certified time and the pressure front's experienced time. Ren's shared clock preserved both. Under the Pale Interval, neither could replace the other. The gap itself supplied the final reference.

“Apply the seventeen-second key,” she said. “Do not reconcile clocks. Offset actions by witnessed frame.”

Aphelion accepted. Neme accepted with its foundation abort still active. Halcyon delayed its release against local time. Serein advanced by the same interval in the Orison frame. The fourteen copies remained different, but their predicted consequences aligned.

Kehr understood through the clock.

Harrow of Rain moved into the unreconciled gap.

The oldest body opened every conductive channel it had offered in the revised plan. Pressure from the Pale Interval entered memories carried for centuries. Harrow bent the front around Silt Crown and the damaged first-arcs. Tull took the resulting corridor load without trying to preserve Narrow Mercy's separate path. Little Weight and Ember-Side corrected Neme's trailing oscillation.

Harrow's sail tore from its mineral core.

Memory crossed Kehr as fragments: Vaul before abandonment, an eclipse at an unnamed star, the first return without its cause, Ren's clock held against Neme's radiation, young bodies reaching toward offered support. Then sequence failed. Kehr's five-part pattern lost one interval and returned across four bodies.

The migration cried through pressure changes too large for the shared clock to translate.

Ansa kept the raw trace.

The Remainder broke along its port frame. Its final field persisted as the hull separated, carrying the first-arcs through Silt Crown's shelter and adding the last correction to Neme. Rescue skiffs tumbled in the debris wake. Seven crew beacons reported injuries. All thirty-two remained lit.

Cantilever 61 dropped out of phase. Then 63, 66, and 70 followed under local confirmations. Power islands separated across Pelagos. Transfer lanes disappeared from public boards. Meridia's reserve estimate fell by half. Neme tide cities held. Halcyon schools stayed sealed on local cells. Serein's thermal stores crossed into emergency draw.

The Pale Interval moved inward.

For three days it passed through a system no longer coherent enough to amplify it. Instruments disagreed. Local grids failed and returned in pieces. Orison bodies crossed the opened corridor by witnessed prediction, with Tull carrying loads beside Kehr without joining Kehr's consensus. First-arcs followed adults whose corrections they had learned to question.

No station possessed a complete view. Ansa worked from partial traces, published uncertainty, and requests she could not rank alone. Her recommendations entered the mesh beside tide limits, reserve floors, crew positions, and alien tolerances. Some were accepted. Others arrived too late or yielded to local veto.

On SD95, the rear edge cleared Cantilever 68.

Cartography Bay Four attempted to rebuild the certified system map. Twelve outer trajectories refused assimilation because their authority copies preserved different clocks. Neme occupied its new orbit. The Orison migration moved beyond Asteron's tail. Debris from the Remainder crossed three local custody zones. The software asked Ansa to select a master record.

She canceled the request.

Across fourteen jurisdictions, verified copies continued exchanging corrections without collapsing into one line. Kehr's four surviving bodies added a broken memory interval. Tull supplied a separate witness. Power moved in fractured islands, less abundant and locally owned.

On Ansa's board, the old green wireframe dissolved. In its place, custody marks traveled with every moving body.

Chapter 28 — After the Migration

The plate refused to come free.

Nine local dawns had passed since the Pale Interval's rear edge cleared Pelagos.

Ivo Calder braced both boots against the rescue skiff's salvage frame and leaned on the manual driver. Beyond his visor, the remains of Remainder turned above Neme in three slow pieces. The forward hull had lost its bridge windows. One radiator wing curled around a truss like burned paper. The port fuel spine had split lengthwise, exposing repair layers applied by crews whose names no registry kept.

His driver clicked at the torque limit.

“Stop trying to win an argument with sixty-year-old steel,” Sana Vi said over suit radio.

“It started this.”

“It finished something larger.”

“Steel doesn't get credit. People do.”

Sana held the skiff steady against the debris field with short correction pulses. She had captained rescue work since the Open Chord, partly because the skiff had command authority and partly because Ivo's right shoulder no longer tolerated a control rail for more than an hour. Seven members of the old crew carried injuries. All thirty-two had answered roll that morning. Ivo treated the count like a gauge that might change if neglected.

He backed the driver out and sprayed the bolt with thermal release. The warped metal contracted by a fraction. On his second attempt, the fastener turned.

The fuel-spine plate lifted away in his gloves.

It measured less than a meter across. Cracks divided the blue Authority serial, and one edge carried a line of weld beads from Ivo's first year aboard. The plate had once covered a manifold no visitor saw. It had survived forty thousand burns because technicians removed it, inspected what lay behind it, and put it back without assuming prior survival guaranteed another cycle.

Ivo clipped it to the salvage frame.

“Memorial office wants an intact name panel,” Sana said.

“They can have the registry plate from the stern.”

“Already recovered. This one has no ship name.”

“It has the repair record.”

She glanced at the welds through the skiff camera. “Fair.”

A warning pulsed across Ivo's visor. Cantilever debris would cross their work volume in twelve minutes. Before the crisis, Meridian Control would have cleared the route with one phase instruction. Now Neme local traffic sent a recommendation, Rook confirmed the debris track, and Serein declined to energize a remote Cantilever for a salvage crew. The skiff had to move.

Ivo released the hull tether. Sana backed them away while the wreck turned on. A glint passed along the cracked fuel channels, then vanished behind Neme's limb.

Traffic control assigned them a return arc lasting nine hours.

“That used to take fifty minutes,” Ivo said.

“That used to require a phase gate,” Sana replied.

“The old system had excellent travel times.”

“It also tried to kill our passengers.”

“I didn't say I wanted it back.”

Cheap transit had disappeared with central coherence. Pelagos now measured distance in fuel again. Medical cargo moved before machine parts. School transfers waited behind atmosphere cartridges. Families who had lived one phase hop apart discovered how far apart their worlds had always been.

The rescue lane displayed forty-six pending requests. A Neme clinic needed membrane stock from Vela. Serein offered thermal exchangers but lacked a tug certified for the altered orbit. Meridia held three compatible drives in an Authority warehouse while courts argued whether releasing them would compromise evidence. Sana copied the listing to the Compact working channel and marked the drives as both legal exhibits and life-support equipment. The contradiction could no longer wait inside one office.

A jury-rigged courier carried the membrane stock first. It would arrive in four days. Under phase transit, the same cargo used to arrive before anyone considered where the power came from. Now every route named fuel, crew hours, missed cargo, and the settlement accepting delay. Scarcity produced honesty only when records stayed public. Otherwise it produced favored manifests.

Ivo opened Remainder's salvage claims. The old Authority contract assigned recoverable propulsion components to Meridian Control. Neme's local court had frozen that clause because the ship died during a shared correction under transferred keys. The crew claimed tools and personal effects. Rescue teams claimed fuel reimbursement. Families of Neme's dead had filed notice against any sale before liability hearings.

He marked the port manifold unsalvageable and preserved its inspection record. A recycler could have recovered useful alloy from the plate now clipped behind him. Its material value would run one clinic pump for several hours. Memorial use had to answer that fact, not rise above it. He attached a provision requiring the seven jurisdictions maintaining the orbit to contribute equivalent pump stock from their restoration allotments.

Sana read the addition. “You turned a scrap plate into a supply contract.”

“Memorials still use mass.”

“Pell would have liked you.”

“I never met him.”

“That has rarely stopped anyone from using his opinion.”

Ivo approved the provision and sent it to the signing draft. If the jurisdictions rejected the replacement stock, the plate would go to recycling. Keeping an object in orbit could not become another way to make someone else pay invisibly.

The crew vote arrived in thirty-two separate marks. Twenty-nine supported memorial use with the replacement clause. Two preferred recycling. One abstained because her family had not yet held its own service. Ivo attached the tally without calling it unanimous. The Compact office accepted the plate only after assigning the equivalent stock to Neme's western clinic.

The skiff entered Neme's local rescue lane under chemical thrust. Its cabin carried recovered suit lockers, a section of propulsion rail, two black boxes, and one sealed container of crew effects. They had found no bodies aboard Remainder. Funerals still occupied every public channel.

Neme named the 1,140 civilians lost during the second correction in groups small enough for families to hear. Human observatories kept the twenty-three dead first-arcs as measured trajectories because no shared language had recovered individual names. Aphelion held Ren Adek's service in Cartography Bay Four beside the clock he had preserved. The Orison mourning for Harrow of Rain moved through Asteron's tail as a pressure absence repeated by four adult bodies.

Ivo had attended none of them in person. Rescue schedules and slow transit made attendance a question of fuel. He watched delayed recordings between salvage shifts, never at the speed the services requested. Grief had joined every other system need in a ration queue.

Neme rose beneath the skiff in changed shades of blue. Its ocean basins had not settled after the 1,400-kilometer shift. Temporary sea walls divided the western tide city. Elevator towers stood idle until foundation surveys could certify their new loads. On the night side, power districts appeared as uneven islands rather than a single luminous web.

A maintenance request entered Sana's board. Vela atmosphere team sought landing priority for six technicians and two portable scrubbers.

“That's Min's crew,” Ivo said.

“I know.”

“You checked before I did.”

“I command a rescue skiff. I read the manifest.”

Sana forwarded the request to Neme local control without adding Ivo's name. The harbor granted priority based on scrubber capacity. He appreciated the omission more than he would have appreciated influence.

Their long return arc crossed the witness station at low relative speed. Human crews had assembled it from a disused survey habitat beyond Neme's third moon. Orison witness swarms occupied the station's magnetic lee, entering and leaving through apertures cut after both sides rejected the word dock. A small human team maintained the shared clock. Neither group possessed custody over the other.

The witness cohort had separated from Kehr's migration two days earlier by mutual consent. Some swarms carried memories of Vaul. Others had learned the first human timing ratios at the ballast canister. Their continued presence offered no embassy and no promise of species agreement. They would observe the Compact, preserve correction records, and retain a route to depart when local conditions allowed.

Consent had required three failed arrangements. Human planners first offered a sealed magnetic volume under Neme jurisdiction. The swarms treated the enclosure as capture. An Orison proposal placed the station inside a permanent adult correction field, which Neme treated as foreign control over local orbit. The accepted design used the old survey habitat as human shelter while an unowned magnetic lee traveled beside it. Station thrusters maintained relation without claiming that the two spaces formed one territory.

Even translation of remain had caused trouble. Human drafts treated presence as a continuing state. The swarms rendered it as repeated decisions not to depart. Their departure route therefore required fuel and clearance to remain available, not merely a legal promise that they could ask later. Vela assigned correction mass. Neme guaranteed a lane. The cohort contributed forecasts for the faint ecologies that human radar might otherwise endanger.

Ivo respected the arrangement because it came with maintenance cards. Every guarantee named inspection intervals, reserve floors, and the hands permitted to refuse unsafe work. No clause depended on friendship surviving longer than a seal.

A swarm crossed the skiff's bow. Its magnetic pressure touched the salvage frame lightly enough to move a loose strap. The fuel-spine plate shifted against its clip.

Ivo tightened it.

“Do you think they recognize the ship?” Sana asked.

“They recognize mass and timing.”

“So did Ren.”

“That is not an answer.”

“No. We have a cohort for those now.”

The station transmitted a correction request. Its human translation asked the skiff to move fourteen meters above the assigned plane, avoiding a juvenile witness pattern too faint for Neme radar. Sana made the change. The swarm corrected away at the same moment. Both maneuvers were logged. Neither canceled the other.

By the time the skiff reached Neme's harbor, Min's transport had already landed.

She stood beneath the rescue gantry in an atmosphere suit with the helmet clipped to one hip. Grease marked the seam of her left glove. Her hair had been cut short enough to fit a hood seal without catching. Ivo looked for the scholarship case she used to carry between Vela examinations and found a scrubber toolkit instead.

Min saw the shoulder brace beneath his jacket. “You said bruising.”

“It became better-documented bruising.”

“Sana?”

“Partial tendon tear,” Sana said from behind him. “He follows medical instructions when they agree with him.”

“I follow the feasible parts.”

Min stepped close, stopped before touching the brace, then pressed her forehead once against his uninjured shoulder. The contact lasted less than a breath. Afterward she took one of the sealed crew-effects cases from the cart without asking whether he could manage it.

“Your landing got priority,” Ivo said.

“Scrubbers got priority. We came with them.”

“I read the manifest.”

“Do you?”

He looked toward the Vela team. Six technicians were unloading equipment onto Neme carts while local crews checked cartridge standards. No one waited for Min. She had already joined the work by carrying the case toward the correct queue.

“What happened to the scholarship office?” he asked.

“It still exists.”

“The route doesn't.”

“Then a route number can keep a desk warm.” She set down the case. “Vela offered to defer us until phase transit returns. They couldn't say whether that means a year or twenty.”

“You could take a slow ship when schedules stabilize.”

“I could.”

He waited.

Min looked out through the harbor shield at Neme's temporary sea wall. Pumps climbed its inner face, each one feeding a district whose old gravity drainage no longer matched the tide. “The atmosphere teams here need people who understand local cells. Halcyon wants the same. Vela is building a training exchange that moves instructors instead of students until transit improves.”

“You're staying.”

“I'm working.”

“That can be both.”

“It can also change.” She studied him with the expression he used to reserve for cracked manifolds. “You keep asking permanence questions because you want to know what to protect me from.”

He nearly denied it. The denial had good parts. Scholarship loss narrowed her choices. Rationing made technical work dangerous. Pelagos had built a moral victory out of infrastructure failure and still expected young workers to crawl inside the consequences.

Min did not need him to pretend those facts were freedom.

“What do you need from me?” he asked.

“A lift to the western pump station after the signing.”

“The skiff belongs to Neme rescue.”

“Then ask Sana like everyone else.”

Sana, already walking toward the council hall, raised one hand without turning. “Request denied until the crew finishes its mandated rest.”

Min smiled. “See? Functional government.”

The Compact signing took place in a converted tide-control chamber because the Assembly causeway on Meridia could not hold the people whose systems now moved together. Pipes crossed the ceiling. Pumps vibrated beneath the floor. Every delegate stood beside a live display of the obligation their jurisdiction accepted.

Ivo attended as custodian of Remainder's salvage, not as a delegate. Sana represented the crew on the memorial route article. Min sat with atmosphere technicians behind Vela's local team. Ansa appeared from Aphelion beside Edrin's Halcyon feed. They argued over school-power guarantees while the chamber microphones remained open.

“Reserve floor before transfer allocation,” Edrin said.

“Local floor, then published interdependence,” Ansa replied. “A district cannot hide surplus while another loses atmosphere.”

“And central review cannot redefine a classroom seal as surplus.”

“I wrote that boundary into the current draft.”

“You wrote essential educational volume. Use atmosphere.”

Ansa changed the phrase. The edit propagated to fourteen jurisdictional copies. Edrin verified it. Neither thanked the other.

Ivo recognized reconciliation when it arrived wearing work clothes.

Olan Brace joined by inquiry channel. He sat at a plain table without the blue bar of office. Two legal observers occupied the room behind him. The Authority seal beside his name had been replaced by TECHNICAL WITNESS, PENDING REVIEW.

A Meridia delegate asked whether the Compact's distributed keys could respond fast enough to another Tern-scale cascade.

“They will respond slower than unilateral command under clean information,” Olan said. “Under conflicting information, local custody can respond faster because it need not wait for Meridian interpretation.”

“Do you recommend adoption?”

“I am here to testify about control architecture, not to recover decision authority through advice.”

He explained the three severed master paths and the surviving Cantilever 3 relay. He listed failure modes in the custody mesh without offering a hidden emergency key as a cure. When Serein's counsel asked about the illegal renewal, Olan answered from the published record. No one applauded. The inquiry scheduled further testimony and left his liability unresolved.

Laleh brought the Compact of Moving Jurisdictions to its final reading.

The document did not declare a new order complete. Its first article set a provisional term and a public revision procedure. Later articles bound power reserves, local abort authority, shared model provenance, Orison passage tolerances, witness rights, salvage routes, and liability claims. Jurisdiction followed bodies made vulnerable by a correction for as long as the consequence traveled. Fixed settlements retained territorial law. Neither principle automatically erased the other.

Material clauses gave the words teeth. Meridia released reserve cells to outer clinics before restoring phase research. Neme funded tide foundations from former gate revenue. Every Cantilever change required a load assignment visible to affected regions. The witness cohort retained custody of its memory records and could leave without forfeiting claims. Human stations could deny a dangerous maneuver while remaining responsible for offering an alternate path when one existed.

Five jurisdictions that had refused approval at SD82 signed limited obligations rather than retrospective consent. Their signatures did not become votes they had never cast. Nine supporters accepted reserve floors that would slow reconstruction. Orison witness swarms marked the shared clock through measured intervals. Kehr's four-body pattern appeared at the edge of the system, delayed by distance and diminished where Harrow's memory no longer answered.

Tull's polity did not join the Compact. It transmitted a passage record and nothing resembling reconciliation.

One by one, the human representatives signed.

No central chime declared completion. Each jurisdiction verified its copy, then published the resources it owed during the provisional term. The chamber displays changed from promises to schedules. Power cells received destinations. Repair crews gained dates. Inquiry archives acquired custodians outside Meridia.

Ivo watched Neme accept maintenance of the memorial route.

The article named a small orbit beyond the rebuilt tide elevators. It would hold fragments from human and Orison losses only when their custodians consented. No fragment could become evidence of ownership. Keeping the path stable required minor corrections from seven jurisdictions over each local year. Any region could decline a particular burn, but the others then had to publish a replacement plan.

The fuel-spine plate waited on the salvage cart beside him.

After the signing, Sana secured the rescue skiff for one last local flight. The old crew joined by relay from clinics, repair berths, and temporary quarters. Seven injured faces occupied the top row. The other twenty-five filled in below. Min came aboard with her scrubber team on the way to the western pumps, then stayed strapped into a rear seat while the skiff climbed.

Neme fell away beneath them. Temporary sea walls formed bright hooks along the coast. Dark districts interrupted the cities. Farther out, the witness station crossed Asteron's glow with its mixed human and Orison traffic.

The memorial orbit appeared on Sana's board as fourteen overlapping custody traces. None claimed master status. Every jurisdiction had agreed to maintain the path, through scheduled burns or published reserve commitments. Their error envelopes met around an orbit modest enough for chemical thrusters to hold.

Ivo carried the plate to the release lock.

He ran one glove over the weld beads. Sana held the skiff on the agreed plane. Min read the orbit verification from the rear board. Seven jurisdictions supplied the first correction schedule; the others published reserve commitments for later cycles. A witness swarm marked the release interval with a small pressure change.

“Path verified,” Sana said.

Ivo opened the outer door.

The cracked port fuel-spine plate moved from his glove into memorial orbit, turning until the old welds caught Asteron's light.
